**Transitivity:** both — transitive (we binged the whole season), intransitive (he binged on chocolate)
**Contrast:** The -ing form usually keeps the e: bingeing (binging is also seen). Binge ON food or drink.

To do something to excess in a short burst, especially eating, drinking, or watching episodes.

1. They *binge* on snacks every time exams come around.
2. We *binged* the entire season in one weekend.
3. He has been *bingeing* on old detective shows all month.

---

### biodegrade

**Pronunciation:** /ˌbaɪoʊdɪˈɡreɪd/ &middot; *by-oh-di-GRAYD*
**Русский:** биологически разлагаться
**Forms:** *biodegrade* &middot; *biodegraded* &middot; *biodegraded*
**Third person:** *biodegrades* &middot; **-ing form:** *biodegrading*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive

To break down naturally through the action of bacteria and other living things.

1. These cups *biodegrade* in about ninety days.
2. The test bags *biodegraded* completely within a year.
3. Ocean plastic is still not *biodegrading* the way the label promised.

---

### bisect

**Pronunciation:** /baɪˈsɛkt/ &middot; *by-SEKT*
**Русский:** делить пополам, рассекать
**Forms:** *bisect* &middot; *bisected* &middot; *bisected*
**Third person:** *bisects* &middot; **-ing form:** *bisecting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To divide something into two equal parts.

1. Use a compass to *bisect* the angle exactly.
2. A new highway *bisected* the old neighborhood.
3. The park is *bisected* by a narrow stream.

---

### bivouac

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbɪvuˌæk/ &middot; *BIV-oo-ak*
**Русский:** располагаться биваком, ночевать под открытым небом
**Forms:** *bivouac* &middot; *bivouacked* &middot; *bivouacked*
**Third person:** *bivouacs* &middot; **-ing form:** *bivouacking*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** A k appears before the ending: bivouacked, bivouacking. Same rule as panic - panicked.

To camp for the night outdoors with little or no shelter.

1. Climbers often *bivouac* on the ledge below the summit.
2. The troops *bivouacked* in a field outside the town.
3. We spent that night *bivouacking* under an overhang.

---

### blackball

**Pronunciation:** /ˈblækbɔːl/ &middot; *BLAK-bawl*
**Русский:** забаллотировать; подвергнуть остракизму
**Forms:** *blackball* &middot; *blackballed* &middot; *blackballed*
**Third person:** *blackballs* &middot; **-ing form:** *blackballing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To vote against letting someone join a group, or to shut someone out of a profession.

1. A single member can *blackball* a candidate.
2. The studio *blackballed* him after the lawsuit.
3. He claims he has been *blackballed* by every agency in town.

---

### blacken

**Pronunciation:** /ˈblækən/ &middot; *BLAK-uhn*
**Русский:** чернить, очернять; чернеть
**Forms:** *blacken* &middot; *blackened* &middot; *blackened*
**Third person:** *blackens* &middot; **-ing form:** *blackening*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (smoke blackened the walls), intransitive (the sky blackened)

To make something black or dark, or to become black or dark.

1. Smoke from the stove will *blacken* the ceiling over time.
2. Storm clouds *blackened* the sky before noon.
3. The beams had been *blackened* by years of soot.

---

### blacklist

**Pronunciation:** /ˈblæklɪst/ &middot; *BLAK-list*
**Русский:** вносить в чёрный список
**Forms:** *blacklist* &middot; *blacklisted* &middot; *blacklisted*
**Third person:** *blacklists* &middot; **-ing form:** *blacklisting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To put someone or something on a list of those to be refused or avoided.

1. Most mail servers *blacklist* addresses that send spam.
2. The airline *blacklisted* the passenger after the incident.
3. Several screenwriters were *blacklisted* during the 1950s.

---

### blaspheme

**Pronunciation:** /blæsˈfiːm/ &middot; *blas-FEEM*
**Русский:** богохульствовать, кощунствовать
**Forms:** *blaspheme* &middot; *blasphemed* &middot; *blasphemed*
**Third person:** *blasphemes* &middot; **-ing form:** *blaspheming*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (he blasphemed the name of God), intransitive (they accused her of blaspheming)
**Contrast:** The verb is stressed on the second syllable (blas-FEEM); the noun blasphemy is stressed on the first (BLAS-fuh-mee).

To speak about God or sacred things with disrespect.

1. In several countries it is still a crime to *blaspheme*.
2. He was accused of having *blasphemed* in the article.
3. The crowd shouted that the preacher was *blaspheming*.

---

### bleat

**Pronunciation:** /bliːt/ &middot; *BLEET*
**Русский:** блеять; ныть, жалобно причитать
**Forms:** *bleat* &middot; *bleated* &middot; *bleated*
**Third person:** *bleats* &middot; **-ing form:** *bleating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive

To make the thin, shaky cry of a sheep or goat, or to complain in a weak, whiny voice.

1. The goats *bleat* whenever anyone walks past the fence.
2. A lamb *bleated* somewhere behind the barn.
3. He kept *bleating* about how unfair the schedule was.

---

### blemish

**Pronunciation:** /ˈblɛmɪʃ/ &middot; *BLEM-ish*
**Русский:** портить, испортить (репутацию); пятнать, запятнать
**Forms:** *blemish* &middot; *blemished* &middot; *blemished*
**Third person:** *blemishes* &middot; **-ing form:** *blemishing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To spoil the appearance or the good name of something.

1. One bad review will not *blemish* a solid reputation.
2. The scandal *blemished* a career that had been spotless for thirty years.
3. Her credit history is *blemished* by a single late payment.

---

### blight

**Pronunciation:** /blaɪt/ &middot; *BLYT*
**Русский:** губить, погубить; поражать болезнью (о растениях)
**Forms:** *blight* &middot; *blighted* &middot; *blighted*
**Third person:** *blights* &middot; **-ing form:** *blighting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To spoil or damage something badly over a long period.

1. Empty storefronts *blight* the whole downtown.
2. A fungus *blighted* the potato crop two years running.
3. The town has been *blighted* by the closing of the mill.

---

### blindfold

**Pronunciation:** /ˈblaɪndfoʊld/ &middot; *BLYND-fohld*
**Русский:** завязывать глаза, завязать глаза
**Forms:** *blindfold* &middot; *blindfolded* &middot; *blindfolded*
**Third person:** *blindfolds* &middot; **-ing form:** *blindfolding*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To cover someone's eyes with a cloth so that they cannot see.

1. They *blindfold* the contestants before the taste test.
2. The kidnappers *blindfolded* him and pushed him into a van.
3. She sat *blindfolded* while we hid the presents.

---

### blindside

**Pronunciation:** /ˈblaɪndsaɪd/ &middot; *BLYND-syd*
**Русский:** заставать врасплох, застать врасплох; огорошить
**Forms:** *blindside* &middot; *blindsided* &middot; *blindsided*
**Third person:** *blindsides* &middot; **-ing form:** *blindsiding*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To hit or shock someone from a direction they were not watching.

1. A question like that can *blindside* even an experienced speaker.
2. The layoffs *blindsided* the entire department.
3. I was completely *blindsided* by his resignation.

---

### blister

**Pronunciation:** /ˈblɪstər/ &middot; *BLIS-tur*
**Русский:** покрываться волдырями, покрыться волдырями; натирать мозоли
**Forms:** *blister* &middot; *blistered* &middot; *blistered*
**Third person:** *blisters* &middot; **-ing form:** *blistering*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*new boots blistered my heels*), intransitive (*the paint blistered in the sun*)

To form, or to cause, small bubbles of fluid or air on a surface.

1. Cheap shoes always *blister* my heels.
2. The paint *blistered* where the afternoon sun hit it.
3. His palms were *blistering* by the end of the first hour.

---

### bloat

**Pronunciation:** /bloʊt/ &middot; *BLOHT*
**Русский:** раздувать, раздуть; вздуваться, пухнуть
**Forms:** *bloat* &middot; *bloated* &middot; *bloated*
**Third person:** *bloats* &middot; **-ing form:** *bloating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*salt bloats you*), intransitive (*the budget bloated*)

To swell up, or to make something swell up, with liquid, gas or extra content.

1. Too much salt will *bloat* you for days.
2. The payroll *bloated* to twice its original size.
3. The app has become *bloated* with features nobody asked for.

---

### bludgeon

**Pronunciation:** /ˈblʌdʒən/ &middot; *BLUJ-uhn*
**Русский:** избивать дубинкой, забить; принуждать грубым напором
**Forms:** *bludgeon* &middot; *bludgeoned* &middot; *bludgeoned*
**Third person:** *bludgeons* &middot; **-ing form:** *bludgeoning*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To strike someone repeatedly with a heavy object, or to force them by crude pressure.

1. Do not *bludgeon* your readers with statistics.
2. The victim had been *bludgeoned* with a length of pipe.
3. He kept *bludgeoning* us with the same argument all evening.

---

### blunder

**Pronunciation:** /ˈblʌndər/ &middot; *BLUN-dur*
**Русский:** допускать грубую ошибку, оплошать; брести наугад
**Forms:** *blunder* &middot; *blundered* &middot; *blundered*
**Third person:** *blunders* &middot; **-ing form:** *blundering*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive

To make a careless, serious mistake, or to move clumsily without looking.

1. Even seasoned negotiators sometimes *blunder*.
2. He *blundered* into the meeting a full hour late.
3. The agency has *blundered* badly on this one.

---

### blunt

**Pronunciation:** /blʌnt/ &middot; *BLUNT*
**Русский:** притуплять, притупить; смягчать, смягчить (удар, воздействие)
**Forms:** *blunt* &middot; *blunted* &middot; *blunted*
**Third person:** *blunts* &middot; **-ing form:** *blunting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To make something less sharp, less strong or less effective.

1. Tax credits should *blunt* the impact of higher fuel prices.
2. Years of bad news *blunted* their outrage.
3. The medication has *blunted* the pain without removing it.

---

### blurt

**Pronunciation:** /blɜːrt/ &middot; *BLURT*
**Русский:** выпалить, ляпнуть, сболтнуть
**Forms:** *blurt* &middot; *blurted* &middot; *blurted*
**Third person:** *blurts* &middot; **-ing form:** *blurting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive — nearly always with *out*
**Contrast:** Almost always *blurt **out***; bare *blurt* sounds incomplete. It carries regret built in — you blurt what you wish you had not said, so it is not a neutral way to report speech.

To say something suddenly and without thinking, usually something you meant to keep quiet.

1. He *blurted* out the answer before anyone else had finished reading.
2. I *blurt* things out when I'm nervous.
3. She had *blurted* the whole story to a stranger on the train.

---

### bluster

**Pronunciation:** /ˈblʌstər/ &middot; *BLUS-tur*
**Русский:** бушевать, шуметь; хорохориться, бахвалиться
**Forms:** *bluster* &middot; *blustered* &middot; *blustered*
**Third person:** *blusters* &middot; **-ing form:** *blustering*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive

To talk in a loud, boastful or threatening way that has little behind it.

1. Some candidates *bluster* when they have nothing to offer.
2. He *blustered* about suing everyone involved, then did nothing.
3. The wind was *blustering* against the windows all night.

---

### boast

**Pronunciation:** /boʊst/ &middot; *BOHST*
**Русский:** хвастаться, похвастаться; обладать (чем-л. ценным)
**Forms:** *boast* &middot; *boasted* &middot; *boasted*
**Third person:** *boasts* &middot; **-ing form:** *boasting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*the hotel boasts two pools*), intransitive (*he boasted all evening*)

To speak with too much pride about yourself, or to possess something impressive.

1. The city *boasts* the largest public library in the state.
2. He *boasted* that he had never failed an exam.
3. She accepted the prize without *boasting* about it.

---

### bob

**Pronunciation:** /bɑːb/ &middot; *BAHB*
**Русский:** покачиваться (на воде), подпрыгивать; кивать
**Forms:** *bob* &middot; *bobbed* &middot; *bobbed*
**Third person:** *bobs* &middot; **-ing form:** *bobbing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — intransitive (*the buoy bobbed*), transitive (*she bobbed her head*)
**Contrast:** The b doubles before an ending: *bob → bobbed, bobbing*.

To move up and down with short, quick movements.

1. Empty bottles *bob* along the edge of the harbor.
2. The apple *bobbed* back up to the surface.
3. Her head was *bobbing* in time with the music.

---

### bode

**Pronunciation:** /boʊd/ &middot; *BOHD*
**Русский:** предвещать, сулить
**Forms:** *bode* &middot; *boded* &middot; *boded*
**Third person:** *bodes* &middot; **-ing form:** *boding*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*the silence boded trouble*), intransitive (*it bodes well*)
**Contrast:** Almost always used with *well* or *ill*: *this bodes well for us*.

To be a sign of what is going to happen.

1. Early sales figures *bode* well for the holiday season.
2. The long silence *boded* nothing good.
3. Two straight quarters of losses do not *bode* well for the company.

---

### boggle

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbɑːɡəl/ &middot; *BAH-guhl*
**Русский:** поражать, ошеломлять; не укладываться в голове
**Forms:** *boggle* &middot; *boggled* &middot; *boggled*
**Third person:** *boggles* &middot; **-ing form:** *boggling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*it boggles the mind*), intransitive (*the mind boggles*)

To overwhelm someone's mind so that it cannot take something in.

1. The size of the national debt *boggles* the mind.
2. The numbers *boggled* everyone at the table.
3. It is still *boggling* my mind that she said yes.

---

### bootleg

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbuːtlɛɡ/ &middot; *BOOT-leg*
**Русский:** незаконно производить или продавать; торговать контрабандой
**Forms:** *bootleg* &middot; *bootlegged* &middot; *bootlegged*
**Third person:** *bootlegs* &middot; **-ing form:** *bootlegging*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The g doubles: *bootleg → bootlegged, bootlegging*.

To make, copy or sell something illegally, especially alcohol or recordings.

1. Some sites still *bootleg* movies while they are in theaters.
2. During Prohibition they *bootlegged* whiskey across the state line.
3. The album was *bootlegged* from a concert tape.

---

### bootstrap

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbuːtstræp/ &middot; *BOOT-strap*
**Русский:** развивать своими силами, поднимать без внешних вложений
**Forms:** *bootstrap* &middot; *bootstrapped* &middot; *bootstrapped*
**Third person:** *bootstraps* &middot; **-ing form:** *bootstrapping*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The p doubles: *bootstrap → bootstrapped, bootstrapping*.

To start or build something using only your own limited resources.

1. They plan to *bootstrap* the company for one more year.
2. She *bootstrapped* the business with her own savings.
3. The team has *bootstrapped* everything out of a spare bedroom.

---

### border

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbɔːrdər/ &middot; *BOR-dur*
**Русский:** граничить с; окаймлять, окаймить
**Forms:** *border* &middot; *bordered* &middot; *bordered*
**Third person:** *borders* &middot; **-ing form:** *bordering*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*Canada borders the United States*), intransitive (*his tone bordered on rude*)
**Contrast:** In the figurative sense it takes **on**: *bordering on absurd*.

To lie along the edge of something, or to be next to it.

1. Four states *border* Nevada.
2. Tall hedges *bordered* the driveway.
3. His confidence is *bordering* on arrogance.

---

### bottle

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbɑːtəl/ &middot; *BAH-tuhl*
**Русский:** разливать по бутылкам, разлить
**Forms:** *bottle* &middot; *bottled* &middot; *bottled*
**Third person:** *bottles* &middot; **-ing form:** *bottling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To put a liquid into bottles.

1. They *bottle* the spring water right at the source.
2. The winery *bottled* six thousand cases last year.
3. All of the juice sold here is *bottled* on site.

---

### braid

**Pronunciation:** /breɪd/ &middot; *BRAYD*
**Русский:** заплетать, заплести; плести косу
**Forms:** *braid* &middot; *braided* &middot; *braided*
**Third person:** *braids* &middot; **-ing form:** *braiding*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To weave three or more strands of hair, rope or cloth together.

1. Can you *braid* my hair before school?
2. She *braided* three ribbons into a cord.
3. The child sat *braiding* blades of grass.

---

### brainwash

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbreɪnwɑːʃ/ &middot; *BRAYN-wahsh*
**Русский:** промывать мозги, промыть мозги
**Forms:** *brainwash* &middot; *brainwashed* &middot; *brainwashed*
**Third person:** *brainwashes* &middot; **-ing form:** *brainwashing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To force someone to accept ideas by repeating them relentlessly.

1. Advertising does not *brainwash* anyone overnight.
2. The group *brainwashed* its recruits over several years.
3. He claims he was *brainwashed* into signing the papers.

---

### branch

**Pronunciation:** /bræntʃ/ &middot; *BRANCH*
**Русский:** разветвляться, разветвиться; расходиться
**Forms:** *branch* &middot; *branched* &middot; *branched*
**Third person:** *branches* &middot; **-ing form:** *branching*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive

To divide into two or more parts that go in different directions.

1. The trail *branches* just past the bridge.
2. The river *branched* into three narrow channels.
3. A wide crack was *branching* across the ceiling.

---

### brandish

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbrændɪʃ/ &middot; *BRAN-dish*
**Русский:** размахивать (оружием), потрясать
**Forms:** *brandish* &middot; *brandished* &middot; *brandished*
**Third person:** *brandishes* &middot; **-ing form:** *brandishing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To wave something, especially a weapon, in a threatening or showy way.

1. Nobody should *brandish* a weapon in a crowded store.
2. He *brandished* the letter as proof.
3. The suspect was *brandishing* a knife when officers arrived.

---

### brave

**Pronunciation:** /breɪv/ &middot; *BRAYV*
**Русский:** смело встречать, не бояться; бросать вызов (стихии)
**Forms:** *brave* &middot; *braved* &middot; *braved*
**Third person:** *braves* &middot; **-ing form:** *braving*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To face something dangerous or unpleasant instead of avoiding it.

1. Thousands *brave* the cold for the parade every year.
2. She *braved* rush-hour traffic to get here on time.
3. They are *braving* a storm to reach the island.

---

### bread

**Pronunciation:** /brɛd/ &middot; *BRED*
**Русский:** панировать, запанировать
**Forms:** *bread* &middot; *breaded* &middot; *breaded*
**Third person:** *breads* &middot; **-ing form:** *breading*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The verb *bread* is BRED, exactly like *bred*, the past of *breed*.

To coat food in breadcrumbs before cooking it.

1. *Bread* the cutlets before you put them in the pan.
2. He *breaded* the fish with crushed crackers.
3. The chicken here is *breaded* and baked, not fried.

---

### bridge

**Pronunciation:** /brɪdʒ/ &middot; *BRIJ*
**Русский:** наводить мост; преодолевать, преодолеть (разрыв)
**Forms:** *bridge* &middot; *bridged* &middot; *bridged*
**Third person:** *bridges* &middot; **-ing form:** *bridging*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To connect two things or to close the gap between them.

1. The program is meant to *bridge* the gap between school and work.
2. A single plank *bridged* the ditch.
3. Nothing has *bridged* the distance between the two sides.

---

### brim

**Pronunciation:** /brɪm/ &middot; *BRIM*
**Русский:** быть полным до краёв, переполняться
**Forms:** *brim* &middot; *brimmed* &middot; *brimmed*
**Third person:** *brims* &middot; **-ing form:** *brimming*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** The m doubles: *brim → brimmed, brimming*. It normally takes **with**.

To be so full that the contents are about to spill over.

1. His eyes *brim* with tears whenever he tells that story.
2. The creek *brimmed* over its banks after the rain.
3. She came back from the trip *brimming* with ideas.

---

### bristle

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbrɪsəl/ &middot; *BRIS-uhl*
**Русский:** ощетиниваться, ощетиниться; возмущаться, вспылить
**Forms:** *bristle* &middot; *bristled* &middot; *bristled*
**Third person:** *bristles* &middot; **-ing form:** *bristling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** The t is silent: BRIS-uhl. In the angry sense it takes **at**.

To react with sudden visible anger, or to stand up stiffly like short hairs.

1. Some readers *bristle* at any criticism of the author.
2. He *bristled* when I mentioned the deadline.
3. The roof was *bristling* with antennas.

---

### broker

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbroʊkər/ &middot; *BROH-kur*
**Русский:** выступать посредником, заключать (сделку) при посредничестве
**Forms:** *broker* &middot; *brokered* &middot; *brokered*
**Third person:** *brokers* &middot; **-ing form:** *brokering*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To arrange an agreement between two sides who will not deal with each other directly.

1. Diplomats are still trying to *broker* a ceasefire.
2. She *brokered* the sale of the entire building.
3. The deal was *brokered* late on a Friday night.

---

### brood

**Pronunciation:** /bruːd/ &middot; *BROOD*
**Русский:** мрачно размышлять, хандрить; сидеть на яйцах
**Forms:** *brood* &middot; *brooded* &middot; *brooded*
**Third person:** *broods* &middot; **-ing form:** *brooding*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** Takes **over** or **about**: *brooding over a mistake*.

To think about something unpleasant for a long time in a gloomy way.

1. Try not to *brood* over things you cannot change.
2. He *brooded* about the argument for a week.
3. She sat *brooding* by the window all afternoon.

---

### brutalize

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbruːtəlaɪz/ &middot; *BROO-tuh-lyz*
**Русский:** жестоко обращаться, зверски избивать; ожесточать
**Forms:** *brutalize* &middot; *brutalized* &middot; *brutalized*
**Third person:** *brutalizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *brutalizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** American English spells it *brutalize*; British English writes *brutalise*.

To treat someone with extreme cruelty, or to make someone cruel.

1. War *brutalizes* everyone it touches.
2. Guards *brutalized* prisoners for years before anyone noticed.
3. Several detainees said they had been *brutalized* in custody.

---

### buck

**Pronunciation:** /bʌk/ &middot; *BUK*
**Русский:** противиться, идти наперекор; брыкаться (о лошади)
**Forms:** *buck* &middot; *bucked* &middot; *bucked*
**Third person:** *bucks* &middot; **-ing form:** *bucking*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*buck the trend*), intransitive (*the horse bucked*)

To resist something, or, of a horse, to jump with the back arched.

1. Few companies dare to *buck* the trend right now.
2. The horse *bucked* and threw him into the dirt.
3. Their sales have been *bucking* the downturn all year.

---

### budge

**Pronunciation:** /bʌdʒ/ &middot; *BUJ*
**Русский:** сдвигаться с места, сдвинуть; уступать, уступить
**Forms:** *budge* &middot; *budged* &middot; *budged*
**Third person:** *budges* &middot; **-ing form:** *budging*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*I could not budge the lid*), intransitive (*he would not budge*)
**Contrast:** Almost always used with a negative: *it won't budge*.

To move slightly, or to change your position in an argument.

1. The window will not *budge*.
2. Neither side *budged* an inch during the talks.
3. The seller has not *budged* on the price.

---

### bulge

**Pronunciation:** /bʌldʒ/ &middot; *BULJ*
**Русский:** выпирать, выпячиваться; вздуваться
**Forms:** *bulge* &middot; *bulged* &middot; *bulged*
**Third person:** *bulges* &middot; **-ing form:** *bulging*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive

To swell out beyond the usual surface or shape.

1. His pockets *bulge* with candy every Halloween.
2. Her eyes *bulged* when she saw the bill.
3. The suitcase was *bulging* at the seams.

---

### bulldoze

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbʊldoʊz/ &middot; *BUUL-dohz*
**Русский:** сносить бульдозером, снести; продавливать (решение)
**Forms:** *bulldoze* &middot; *bulldozed* &middot; *bulldozed*
**Third person:** *bulldozes* &middot; **-ing form:** *bulldozing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To knock down or clear something with heavy machinery, or to force something through.

1. The city plans to *bulldoze* the old stadium.
2. Developers *bulldozed* the orchard in a single weekend.
3. The bill was *bulldozed* through the legislature in two days.

---

### bum

**Pronunciation:** /bʌm/ &middot; *BUM*
**Русский:** клянчить, стрелять (сигарету); бездельничать
**Forms:** *bum* &middot; *bummed* &middot; *bummed*
**Third person:** *bums* &middot; **-ing form:** *bumming*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*bum a cigarette*), intransitive with *around* (*bum around*)
**Contrast:** Informal, and the doubled **m** matters: *bummed*, *bumming*. Three separate uses live here — *bum something off someone* (cadge), *bum around* (drift), and *be bummed* (be disappointed). In British English the noun *bum* means a part of the body, which makes this a word to place carefully.

To get something small from someone by asking, without paying for it.

1. Can I *bum* a ride to the station?
2. He *bummed* a cigarette off a guy outside.
3. They spent the summer *bumming* around the coast.

---

### bungle

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbʌŋɡəl/ &middot; *BUNG-guhl*
**Русский:** портить, испортить дело; напортачить
**Forms:** *bungle* &middot; *bungled* &middot; *bungled*
**Third person:** *bungles* &middot; **-ing form:** *bungling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To do something badly and spoil it through carelessness.

1. A nervous witness can *bungle* even a simple answer.
2. The department *bungled* the investigation from the start.
3. The rollout was so badly *bungled* that they started over.

---

### buoy

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbuːi/ &middot; *BOO-ee*
**Русский:** поддерживать, воодушевлять; держать на плаву
**Forms:** *buoy* &middot; *buoyed* &middot; *buoyed*
**Third person:** *buoys* &middot; **-ing form:** *buoying*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** In American English *buoy* has two syllables, BOO-ee, not BOY.

To keep something afloat, or to lift someone's spirits or prospects.

1. Good jobs numbers usually *buoy* the market.
2. Strong exports *buoyed* the economy last quarter.
3. The team was *buoyed* by the crowd behind them.

---

### burden

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbɜːrdən/ &middot; *BUR-duhn*
**Русский:** обременять, обременить; отягощать, отяготить
**Forms:** *burden* &middot; *burdened* &middot; *burdened*
**Third person:** *burdens* &middot; **-ing form:** *burdening*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Takes **with**: *burdened with responsibility*.

To load someone with something heavy or difficult to carry.

1. I do not want to *burden* you with my problems.
2. The new rules *burdened* small shops with extra paperwork.
3. Graduates are *burdened* with enormous debt.

---

### burgeon

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbɜːrdʒən/ &middot; *BUR-juhn*
**Русский:** бурно расти, быстро развиваться, разрастаться
**Forms:** *burgeon* &middot; *burgeoned* &middot; *burgeoned*
**Third person:** *burgeons* &middot; **-ing form:** *burgeoning*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive

To grow or develop quickly.

1. Interest in the sport continues to *burgeon*.
2. The population *burgeoned* after the plant opened.
3. A *burgeoning* middle class is reshaping the market.

---

### burnish

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbɜːrnɪʃ/ &middot; *BUR-nish*
**Русский:** полировать, отполировать; укреплять (репутацию)
**Forms:** *burnish* &middot; *burnished* &middot; *burnished*
**Third person:** *burnishes* &middot; **-ing form:** *burnishing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To polish a surface until it shines, or to improve a reputation.

1. The trip was meant to *burnish* his image abroad.
2. She *burnished* the copper pot until it gleamed.
3. He has spent a decade *burnishing* his credentials.

---

### bus

**Pronunciation:** /bʌs/ &middot; *BUS*
**Русский:** возить на автобусе, перевозить автобусом
**Forms:** *bus* &middot; *bused* &middot; *bused*
**Third person:** *buses* &middot; **-ing form:** *busing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** American English keeps one s: *buses, bused, busing*. The doubled forms *busses, bussed, bussing* are British.

To carry a group of people somewhere by bus.

1. The district *buses* children across town every morning.
2. They *bused* the fans to the stadium in shifts.
3. Students were *bused* in from three counties.

---

### bustle

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbʌsəl/ &middot; *BUS-uhl*
**Русский:** суетиться, хлопотать; сновать
**Forms:** *bustle* &middot; *bustled* &middot; *bustled*
**Third person:** *bustles* &middot; **-ing form:** *bustling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** The t is silent: BUS-uhl.

To move around quickly and busily.

1. Vendors *bustle* through the market every Saturday.
2. She *bustled* around the kitchen making coffee.
3. The station was *bustling* with commuters.

---

### bypass

**Pronunciation:** /ˈbaɪpæs/ &middot; *BY-pas*
**Русский:** обходить, обойти; объезжать, объехать
**Forms:** *bypass* &middot; *bypassed* &middot; *bypassed*
**Third person:** *bypasses* &middot; **-ing form:** *bypassing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To go around something instead of through it, or to skip a required step.

1. You can *bypass* the form and call the office directly.
2. The new highway *bypassed* the old town center.
3. By *bypassing* the spam filter, the message reached everyone.

---

### cage

**Pronunciation:** /keɪdʒ/ &middot; *KAYJ*
**Русский:** сажать в клетку, посадить в клетку; запирать
**Forms:** *cage* &middot; *caged* &middot; *caged*
**Third person:** *cages* &middot; **-ing form:** *caging*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To shut an animal or a person in a cage.

1. Good zoos no longer *cage* large animals the way they once did.
2. They *caged* the parrot for the drive home.
3. He felt *caged* in that windowless office.

---

### cajole

**Pronunciation:** /kəˈdʒoʊl/ &middot; *kuh-JOHL*
**Русский:** уговаривать, уговорить; умасливать, задабривать
**Forms:** *cajole* &middot; *cajoled* &middot; *cajoled*
**Third person:** *cajoles* &middot; **-ing form:** *cajoling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Takes **into**: *cajoled into signing*.

To persuade someone gently and flatteringly to do something.

1. You cannot *cajole* him into changing his mind.
2. She *cajoled* her brother into driving her to the airport.
3. After an hour of *cajoling*, they finally agreed.

---

### calibrate

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkæləbreɪt/ &middot; *KAL-uh-brayt*
**Русский:** калибровать, откалибровать; настраивать, выверять
**Forms:** *calibrate* &middot; *calibrated* &middot; *calibrated*
**Third person:** *calibrates* &middot; **-ing form:** *calibrating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To adjust an instrument or a judgment so that it measures correctly.

1. Technicians *calibrate* the scales every morning.
2. We *calibrated* our expectations after the first week.
3. The sensor has not been *calibrated* since it was installed.

---

### cannibalize

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkænəbəlaɪz/ &middot; *KAN-uh-buh-lyz*
**Русский:** разбирать на запчасти; поглощать собственные продажи
**Forms:** *cannibalize* &middot; *cannibalized* &middot; *cannibalized*
**Third person:** *cannibalizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *cannibalizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To take parts from one thing to repair another, or to take business away from your own product.

1. A cheaper model could *cannibalize* sales of the flagship.
2. They *cannibalized* two dead machines to build one that worked.
3. The new store is *cannibalizing* its own customers.

---

### capsize

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkæpsaɪz/ &middot; *KAP-syz*
**Русский:** опрокидываться, опрокинуться; переворачиваться (о лодке)
**Forms:** *capsize* &middot; *capsized* &middot; *capsized*
**Third person:** *capsizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *capsizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — intransitive (*the boat capsized*), transitive (*a wave capsized the kayak*)

To turn over in the water.

1. Small boats *capsize* easily in that channel.
2. The ferry *capsized* in heavy seas.
3. Two kayaks had *capsized* before the guides noticed.

---

### care

**Pronunciation:** /kɛr/ &middot; *KAIR*
**Русский:** заботиться; беспокоиться; быть небезразличным
**Forms:** *care* &middot; *cared* &middot; *cared*
**Third person:** *cares* &middot; **-ing form:** *caring*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** The standard American idiom is *couldn't care less*, not *could care less*.

To feel that something or someone matters to you.

1. Who *cares* what time it starts?
2. Honestly, I do not *care* who wins.
3. She *cared* deeply about her students, even after she retired.

---

### careen

**Pronunciation:** /kəˈriːn/ &middot; *kuh-REEN*
**Русский:** нестись неуправляемо, мчаться; резко крениться
**Forms:** *careen* &middot; *careened* &middot; *careened*
**Third person:** *careens* &middot; **-ing form:** *careening*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** In American English *careen* is the usual verb for lurching out of control; *career* in that sense is British.

To move fast and out of control, swerving from side to side.

1. Shopping carts *careen* down that hill all summer.
2. The truck *careened* into a ditch.
3. The car went *careening* across three lanes of traffic.

---

### caricature

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɛrɪkətʃʊr/ &middot; *KER-ih-kuh-chuur*
**Русский:** изображать карикатурно, карикатурить; утрировать
**Forms:** *caricature* &middot; *caricatured* &middot; *caricatured*
**Third person:** *caricatures* &middot; **-ing form:** *caricaturing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To describe or draw someone in an exaggerated way that makes them ridiculous.

1. Critics *caricature* the proposal as a giveaway to the rich.
2. The cartoon *caricatured* the mayor as a sleeping cat.
3. His accent has been *caricatured* on television for years.

---

### carjack

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑːrdʒæk/ &middot; *KAHR-jak*
**Русский:** угонять машину с применением силы, отбирать автомобиль у водителя
**Forms:** *carjack* &middot; *carjacked* &middot; *carjacked*
**Third person:** *carjacks* &middot; **-ing form:** *carjacking*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To steal a car by force from the person driving it.

1. Thieves *carjack* drivers at red lights in that part of the city.
2. Two men *carjacked* a delivery van downtown.
3. She was *carjacked* outside her own garage.

---

### castigate

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkæstɪɡeɪt/ &middot; *KAS-tuh-gayt*
**Русский:** сурово критиковать, бичевать; отчитывать, отчитать
**Forms:** *castigate* &middot; *castigated* &middot; *castigated*
**Third person:** *castigates* &middot; **-ing form:** *castigating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To criticize someone severely, usually in public.

1. Editorials *castigate* the mayor almost every week.
2. The judge *castigated* both attorneys for wasting the court's time.
3. He has been publicly *castigated* for the remark.

---

### castrate

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkæstreɪt/ &middot; *KAS-trayt*
**Русский:** кастрировать; выхолащивать, выхолостить
**Forms:** *castrate* &middot; *castrated* &middot; *castrated*
**Third person:** *castrates* &middot; **-ing form:** *castrating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To remove the testicles of a male animal, or to take the force out of something.

1. Veterinarians routinely *castrate* male cats and dogs.
2. Amendments *castrated* the bill before the final vote.
3. The bull had been *castrated* as a calf.

---

### catalyze

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkætəlaɪz/ &middot; *KAT-uh-lyz*
**Русский:** катализировать; способствовать, ускорять
**Forms:** *catalyze* &middot; *catalyzed* &middot; *catalyzed*
**Third person:** *catalyzes* &middot; **-ing form:** *catalyzing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** American English spells it *catalyze*; British English writes *catalyse*.

To cause or speed up a reaction or a change.

1. Enzymes *catalyze* these reactions at body temperature.
2. The report *catalyzed* a national debate about policing.
3. Cheap credit has *catalyzed* a building boom.

---

### catapult

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkætəpʌlt/ &middot; *KAT-uh-pult*
**Русский:** катапультировать; резко выбрасывать; вознести (к славе)
**Forms:** *catapult* &middot; *catapulted* &middot; *catapulted*
**Third person:** *catapults* &middot; **-ing form:** *catapulting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*the crash catapulted him forward*), intransitive (*he catapulted out of his chair*)

To throw or be thrown suddenly and violently into a new place or position.

1. One hit song can *catapult* a singer to fame.
2. The collision *catapulted* him through the windshield.
3. That role has *catapulted* her into the spotlight.

---

### caulk

**Pronunciation:** /kɔːk/ &middot; *KAWK*
**Русский:** конопатить, заделывать герметиком; герметизировать
**Forms:** *caulk* &middot; *caulked* &middot; *caulked*
**Third person:** *caulks* &middot; **-ing form:** *caulking*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The l is silent: *caulk* is KAWK.

To seal a joint or seam with a waterproof filler.

1. You should *caulk* around the tub before you paint.
2. He *caulked* every window frame before winter.
3. The seams have been *caulked* and sanded smooth.

---

### cause

**Pronunciation:** /kɔːz/ &middot; *KAWZ*
**Русский:** вызывать, вызвать; причинять, причинить
**Forms:** *cause* &middot; *caused* &middot; *caused*
**Third person:** *causes* &middot; **-ing form:** *causing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To make something happen, usually something bad.

1. Heavy rain can *cause* flash flooding in these canyons.
2. The delay *caused* us to miss our connection.
3. Storms have *caused* millions of dollars in damage this year.

---

### cave

**Pronunciation:** /keɪv/ &middot; *KAYV*
**Русский:** уступать, уступить (под давлением); сдаваться, сдаться
**Forms:** *cave* &middot; *caved* &middot; *caved*
**Third person:** *caves* &middot; **-ing form:** *caving*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** It takes **to**: *caved to pressure*. With *in* the meaning shifts to collapsing: *the roof caved in*.

To stop resisting and give in to pressure.

1. Management always *caves* when a strike is threatened.
2. He *caved* after an hour of arguing.
3. The company has *caved* to public pressure and issued refunds.

---

### cede

**Pronunciation:** /siːd/ &middot; *SEED*
**Русский:** уступать, уступить; передавать, передать (территорию, права)
**Forms:** *cede* &middot; *ceded* &middot; *ceded*
**Third person:** *cedes* &middot; **-ing form:** *ceding*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** *cede* sounds exactly like *seed*.

To give up power, land or a right, usually because you have to.

1. No government wants to *cede* control of its own borders.
2. Spain *ceded* the islands in 1898.
3. He has *ceded* the lead to his closest rival.

---

### cement

**Pronunciation:** /sɪˈmɛnt/ &middot; *si-MENT*
**Русский:** закреплять, закрепить, укреплять
**Forms:** *cement* &middot; *cemented* &middot; *cemented*
**Third person:** *cements* &middot; **-ing form:** *cementing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To make a relationship, reputation, or position firm and lasting.

1. A trade deal would *cement* ties between the two countries.
2. Her second album *cemented* her reputation as a songwriter.
3. The win has *cemented* their place at the top of the league.

---

### censure

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsɛnʃər/ &middot; *SEN-shur*
**Русский:** осуждать, осудить, выносить порицание
**Forms:** *censure* &middot; *censured* &middot; *censured*
**Third person:** *censures* &middot; **-ing form:** *censuring*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Do not confuse *censure* (SEN-shur, to condemn formally) with *censor* (SEN-sur, to cut material out of a book or film).

To criticize someone formally and severely, usually by an official vote or statement.

1. The board may *censure* its own chairman.
2. Two senators were *censured* for accepting the gifts.
3. The committee is *censuring* him for misusing public money.

---

### center

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsɛntər/ &middot; *SEN-tur*
**Русский:** центрировать, размещать по центру
**Forms:** *center* &middot; *centered* &middot; *centered*
**Third person:** *centers* &middot; **-ing form:** *centering*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** American English spells it *center, centered, centering*; British English writes *centre, centred, centring*.

To put something in the exact middle of a space.

1. *Center* the heading at the top of the page.
2. He *centered* the vase on the table and stepped back.
3. The photo was not *centered* properly, so we printed it again.

---

### centralize

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsɛntrəlaɪz/ &middot; *SEN-truh-lyz*
**Русский:** централизовать, сосредоточивать
**Forms:** *centralize* &middot; *centralized* &middot; *centralized*
**Third person:** *centralizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *centralizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** British English writes *centralise*; American English uses *centralize*.

To bring control of something under a single authority or place.

1. The reform will *centralize* hiring in one office.
2. They *centralized* all customer records on a single server.
3. Power has been steadily *centralized* in the capital.

---

### chain

**Pronunciation:** /tʃeɪn/ &middot; *CHAYN*
**Русский:** приковывать, приковать цепью, привязывать
**Forms:** *chain* &middot; *chained* &middot; *chained*
**Third person:** *chains* &middot; **-ing form:** *chaining*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To fasten or hold something in place with a chain.

1. Always *chain* your bike to the rack outside.
2. They *chained* the gate shut for the winter.
3. The dog was *chained* to a post in the yard all day.

---

### chance

**Pronunciation:** /tʃæns/ &middot; *CHANS*
**Русский:** рисковать, рискнуть, отважиться
**Forms:** *chance* &middot; *chanced* &middot; *chanced*
**Third person:** *chances* &middot; **-ing form:** *chancing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The silent *-e* drops before *-ing*: *chance → chancing*, but stays in *chanced*.

To do something risky and hope that nothing goes wrong.

1. The ice looks thin — I wouldn't *chance* it.
2. She *chanced* a quick look over her shoulder.
3. They are *chancing* a two-day drive in that old car.

---

### characterize

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɛrəktəraɪz/ &middot; *KER-uk-tuh-ryz*
**Русский:** характеризовать, охарактеризовать, описывать
**Forms:** *characterize* &middot; *characterized* &middot; *characterized*
**Third person:** *characterizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *characterizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** British English writes *characterise*. The first syllable keeps its *r*: KER-uk-tuh-ryz.

To describe the nature of something, or to be a typical feature of it.

1. How would you *characterize* their relationship?
2. He *characterized* the meeting as productive.
3. The season has been *characterized* by heavy rain.

---

### charter

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtʃɑːrtər/ &middot; *CHAR-tur*
**Русский:** фрахтовать, зафрахтовать, арендовать
**Forms:** *charter* &middot; *chartered* &middot; *chartered*
**Third person:** *charters* &middot; **-ing form:** *chartering*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To hire a plane, boat, or bus for your own private use.

1. The team will *charter* a plane for the away game.
2. They *chartered* a bus to take everyone to the wedding.
3. We are *chartering* a fishing boat for the weekend.

---

### chasten

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtʃeɪsən/ &middot; *CHAY-suhn*
**Русский:** смирять, смирить, отрезвлять
**Forms:** *chasten* &middot; *chastened* &middot; *chastened*
**Third person:** *chastens* &middot; **-ing form:** *chastening*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The *t* is silent: *chasten* is CHAY-suhn, like *hasten*.

To make someone humbler and more careful, usually after a failure or a rebuke.

1. A loss like that should *chasten* any team.
2. The scandal *chastened* the whole industry.
3. He came back *chastened* and much quieter than before.

---

### cheapen

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtʃiːpən/ &middot; *CHEE-puhn*
**Русский:** обесценивать, обесценить, опошлять
**Forms:** *cheapen* &middot; *cheapened* &middot; *cheapened*
**Third person:** *cheapens* &middot; **-ing form:** *cheapening*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To make something seem less valuable or less worthy of respect.

1. Constant discounts *cheapen* the brand.
2. That remark *cheapened* an otherwise serious debate.
3. He felt *cheapened* by the whole experience.

---

### cherry-pick

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtʃɛriˌpɪk/ &middot; *CHER-ee-pik*
**Русский:** отбирать выборочно, выбирать только выгодное
**Forms:** *cherry-pick* &middot; *cherry-picked* &middot; *cherry-picked*
**Third person:** *cherry-picks* &middot; **-ing form:** *cherry-picking*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Written with a hyphen in American English: *cherry-pick, cherry-picked, cherry-picking*.

To choose only the items that support your case and ignore the rest.

1. Don't *cherry-pick* the numbers that suit your argument.
2. The report *cherry-picked* two good years and skipped the rest.
3. They are *cherry-picking* quotes from a two-hour interview.

---

### chlorinate

**Pronunciation:** /ˈklɔːrəneɪt/ &middot; *KLOR-uh-nayt*
**Русский:** хлорировать
**Forms:** *chlorinate* &middot; *chlorinated* &middot; *chlorinated*
**Third person:** *chlorinates* &middot; **-ing form:** *chlorinating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To add chlorine to water in order to kill bacteria.

1. The city *chlorinates* its drinking water year-round.
2. They *chlorinated* the pool before the season opened.
3. Heavily *chlorinated* water can irritate your eyes.

---

### chomp

**Pronunciation:** /tʃɑːmp/ &middot; *CHAHMP*
**Русский:** громко жевать, чавкать, грызть
**Forms:** *chomp* &middot; *chomped* &middot; *chomped*
**Third person:** *chomps* &middot; **-ing form:** *chomping*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*chomp a carrot*), intransitive (*chomp on gum*)
**Contrast:** *Chomping at the bit* is a fixed idiom meaning impatient to start.

To chew something loudly and in big bites.

1. The kids *chomp* through a box of cereal every week.
2. He *chomped* on an apple the whole way home.
3. The horse was *chomping* at the long grass by the fence.

---

### chortle

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtʃɔːrtəl/ &middot; *CHOR-tuhl*
**Русский:** посмеиваться, довольно хихикать
**Forms:** *chortle* &middot; *chortled* &middot; *chortled*
**Third person:** *chortles* &middot; **-ing form:** *chortling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive

To laugh out loud in a gleeful, snorting way.

1. He *chortles* every single time he tells that story.
2. The audience *chortled* at the mayor's answer.
3. She was still *chortling* about it when we left.

---

### christen

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkrɪsən/ &middot; *KRIS-uhn*
**Русский:** крестить, окрестить, нарекать
**Forms:** *christen* &middot; *christened* &middot; *christened*
**Third person:** *christens* &middot; **-ing form:** *christening*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The *t* is silent: *christen* is KRIS-uhn.

To baptize a baby and give it a name, or to give a name to something new.

1. They will *christen* the baby in June.
2. The mayor *christened* the new ferry with a bottle of champagne.
3. The hall has been *christened* after the school's founder.

---

### chronicle

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkrɑːnɪkəl/ &middot; *KRAH-ni-kuhl*
**Русский:** описывать по порядку, вести хронику
**Forms:** *chronicle* &middot; *chronicled* &middot; *chronicled*
**Third person:** *chronicles* &middot; **-ing form:** *chronicling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To record a series of events in the order in which they happened.

1. The film *chronicles* one family's escape from the war.
2. She *chronicled* the strike in a series of blog posts.
3. Their rise has been *chronicled* in three separate books.

---

### chuck

**Pronunciation:** /tʃʌk/ &middot; *CHUK*
**Русский:** швырять, швырнуть, выбрасывать
**Forms:** *chuck* &middot; *chucked* &middot; *chucked*
**Third person:** *chucks* &middot; **-ing form:** *chucking*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To throw something carelessly or casually.

1. Just *chuck* it in the back of the truck.
2. He *chucked* the old mattress into the dumpster.
3. She was *chucking* snowballs at her brother all afternoon.

---

### chuckle

**Pronunciation:** /ˈtʃʌkəl/ &middot; *CHUK-uhl*
**Русский:** посмеиваться, усмехаться, хихикать
**Forms:** *chuckle* &middot; *chuckled* &middot; *chuckled*
**Third person:** *chuckles* &middot; **-ing form:** *chuckling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive

To laugh quietly to yourself.

1. The kids *chuckle* every time the dog does that.
2. He *chuckled* and shook his head.
3. She was still *chuckling* about it an hour later.

---

### circumcise

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsɜːrkəmsaɪz/ &middot; *SUR-kuhm-syz*
**Русский:** делать обрезание, совершать обрезание
**Forms:** *circumcise* &middot; *circumcised* &middot; *circumcised*
**Third person:** *circumcises* &middot; **-ing form:** *circumcising*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To cut away the foreskin of a male, usually for religious or medical reasons.

1. Some parents choose to *circumcise* their sons.
2. He was *circumcised* as an infant.
3. Hospitals are *circumcising* fewer newborns than they did in the 1980s.

---

### circumscribe

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsɜːrkəmskraɪb/ &middot; *SUR-kuhm-skryb*
**Русский:** ограничивать, ограничить, очерчивать пределы
**Forms:** *circumscribe* &middot; *circumscribed* &middot; *circumscribed*
**Third person:** *circumscribes* &middot; **-ing form:** *circumscribing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To keep something within strict limits.

1. The constitution *circumscribes* the president's war powers.
2. The court *circumscribed* the agency's authority last year.
3. Their freedom of movement was sharply *circumscribed*.

---

### circumvent

**Pronunciation:** /ˌsɜːrkəmˈvɛnt/ &middot; *sur-kuhm-VENT*
**Русский:** обходить, обойти (закон, правило)
**Forms:** *circumvent* &middot; *circumvented* &middot; *circumvented*
**Third person:** *circumvents* &middot; **-ing form:** *circumventing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To find a way around a rule, obstacle, or difficulty.

1. Some firms *circumvent* the tax by moving money abroad.
2. They *circumvented* the ban with a shell company.
3. The software stops users from *circumventing* the filter.

---

### civilize

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsɪvəlaɪz/ &middot; *SIV-uh-lyz*
**Русский:** цивилизовать, облагораживать, окультуривать
**Forms:** *civilize* &middot; *civilized* &middot; *civilized*
**Third person:** *civilizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *civilizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** British English writes *civilise*; American English uses *civilize*.

To bring a group or a situation to a more orderly and humane state.

1. New rules should *civilize* the comment section.
2. Empires claimed they were *civilizing* the lands they took.
3. The debate was *civilized* by a firm moderator.

---

### clamber

**Pronunciation:** /ˈklæmbər/ &middot; *KLAM-bur*
**Русский:** карабкаться, вскарабкаться, взбираться с трудом
**Forms:** *clamber* &middot; *clambered* &middot; *clambered*
**Third person:** *clambers* &middot; **-ing form:** *clambering*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** Do not confuse *clamber* (to climb awkwardly) with *clamor* (to shout or demand loudly).

To climb somewhere awkwardly, using both hands and feet.

1. Kids *clamber* over those rocks all summer.
2. We *clambered* up the bank to the road.
3. He was *clambering* into the back seat when the bus pulled away.

---

### clamor

**Pronunciation:** /ˈklæmər/ &middot; *KLAM-ur*
**Русский:** шумно требовать, громко возмущаться
**Forms:** *clamor* &middot; *clamored* &middot; *clamored*
**Third person:** *clamors* &middot; **-ing form:** *clamoring*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** You *clamor **for*** something. American English spells it *clamor, clamored, clamoring*; British English writes *clamour, clamoured, clamouring*.

To demand something loudly and insistently.

1. Fans *clamor* for a sequel every single year.
2. Residents *clamored* for the council to act.
3. Reporters were *clamoring* outside the courthouse.

---

### clang

**Pronunciation:** /klæŋ/ &middot; *KLANG*
**Русский:** лязгать, лязгнуть, звенеть
**Forms:** *clang* &middot; *clanged* &middot; *clanged*
**Third person:** *clangs* &middot; **-ing form:** *clanging*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*he clanged the bell*), intransitive (*the gate clanged shut*)

To make, or cause something to make, a loud ringing metallic noise.

1. The bells *clang* on the hour, every hour.
2. The gate *clanged* shut behind us.
3. Somebody was *clanging* a pipe down in the basement.

---

### clatter

**Pronunciation:** /ˈklætər/ &middot; *KLAT-ur*
**Русский:** греметь, грохотать, стучать
**Forms:** *clatter* &middot; *clattered* &middot; *clattered*
**Third person:** *clatters* &middot; **-ing form:** *clattering*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*she clattered the dishes into the sink*), intransitive (*hooves clattered on the stones*)

To make a loud rattling noise by knocking hard objects together.

1. Hooves *clatter* on the cobblestones every morning.
2. A whole tray of glasses *clattered* to the floor.
3. The dishwasher has been *clattering* since breakfast.

---

### claw

**Pronunciation:** /klɔː/ &middot; *KLAW*
**Русский:** царапать, оцарапать, цепляться когтями
**Forms:** *claw* &middot; *clawed* &middot; *clawed*
**Third person:** *claws* &middot; **-ing form:** *clawing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*the cat clawed the sofa*), intransitive (*claw at the door*)

To scratch, tear, or grab at something with claws or fingernails.

1. Cats *claw* the furniture when they are bored.
2. She *clawed* her way back into the race.
3. The dog was *clawing* at the screen door to get in.

---

### cleanse

**Pronunciation:** /klɛnz/ &middot; *KLENZ*
**Русский:** очищать, очистить, промывать
**Forms:** *cleanse* &middot; *cleansed* &middot; *cleansed*
**Third person:** *cleanses* &middot; **-ing form:** *cleansing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** *Cleanse* is KLENZ with the DRESS vowel, not the long *ee* of *clean*.

To clean something thoroughly, especially skin or a wound.

1. *Cleanse* the cut before you put a bandage on it.
2. The nurse *cleansed* the wound with saline.
3. She has been *cleansing* her skin with the same soap for years.

---

### clench

**Pronunciation:** /klɛntʃ/ &middot; *KLENCH*
**Русский:** сжимать, сжать (кулаки, зубы)
**Forms:** *clench* &middot; *clenched* &middot; *clenched*
**Third person:** *clenches* &middot; **-ing form:** *clenching*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*clench your fists*), intransitive (*his jaw clenched*)
**Contrast:** Takes *-es* in the third person: *clench → clenches*.

To close your hand, teeth, or jaw tightly.

1. He *clenches* his jaw whenever he is angry.
2. She *clenched* her fists and said nothing.
3. He was *clenching* the wheel so hard his knuckles went white.

---

### climax

**Pronunciation:** /ˈklaɪmæks/ &middot; *KLY-maks*
**Русский:** достигать кульминации, завершаться кульминацией
**Forms:** *climax* &middot; *climaxed* &middot; *climaxed*
**Third person:** *climaxes* &middot; **-ing form:** *climaxing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** Takes *-es* in the third person: *climax → climaxes*. It *climaxes **in*** or ***with*** something.

To reach the most exciting or important point.

1. The festival *climaxes* with a fireworks display.
2. The trial *climaxed* in a surprise confession.
3. The season was *climaxing* just as he got injured.

---

### clink

**Pronunciation:** /klɪŋk/ &middot; *KLINGK*
**Русский:** звякать, звякнуть, чокаться
**Forms:** *clink* &middot; *clinked* &middot; *clinked*
**Third person:** *clinks* &middot; **-ing form:** *clinking*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*clink glasses*), intransitive (*the ice clinked*)

To make, or cause, a light ringing sound by touching glass or metal together.

1. We always *clink* glasses before the first sip.
2. They *clinked* their mugs and drank.
3. The bottles were *clinking* in the back of the car.

---

### cloak

**Pronunciation:** /kloʊk/ &middot; *KLOHK*
**Русский:** скрывать, скрыть, маскировать, окутывать
**Forms:** *cloak* &middot; *cloaked* &middot; *cloaked*
**Third person:** *cloaks* &middot; **-ing form:** *cloaking*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To hide or cover something up.

1. Officials *cloak* the deal in technical language.
2. Fog *cloaked* the whole valley by morning.
3. The project has been *cloaked* in secrecy from the start.

---

### clobber

**Pronunciation:** /ˈklɑːbər/ &middot; *KLAH-bur*
**Русский:** разгромить, отколотить, сильно ударить
**Forms:** *clobber* &middot; *clobbered* &middot; *clobbered*
**Third person:** *clobbers* &middot; **-ing form:** *clobbering*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To hit someone hard, or to beat or damage them badly.

1. Storms like that *clobber* the coast every fall.
2. Our team got *clobbered* forty to three.
3. Rising rates are *clobbering* small businesses.

---

### clone

**Pronunciation:** /kloʊn/ &middot; *KLOHN*
**Русский:** клонировать, копировать
**Forms:** *clone* &middot; *cloned* &middot; *cloned*
**Third person:** *clones* &middot; **-ing form:** *cloning*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To make an exact copy of a living thing, a device, or a file.

1. Scientists can now *clone* several kinds of animal.
2. Dolly the sheep was *cloned* in 1996.
3. He is *cloning* the whole repository onto a new laptop.

---

### clot

**Pronunciation:** /klɑːt/ &middot; *KLAHT*
**Русский:** свёртываться, свернуться (о крови)
**Forms:** *clot* &middot; *clotted* &middot; *clotted*
**Third person:** *clots* &middot; **-ing form:** *clotting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** The *t* doubles before an ending: *clot → clotted, clotting*.

To thicken into a soft solid lump, as blood does.

1. Blood normally *clots* within a few minutes.
2. The wound *clotted* on its own overnight.
3. Her blood was *clotting* too slowly, the doctor said.

---

### club

**Pronunciation:** /klʌb/ &middot; *KLUB*
**Русский:** бить дубинкой, избивать
**Forms:** *club* &middot; *clubbed* &middot; *clubbed*
**Third person:** *clubs* &middot; **-ing form:** *clubbing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The *b* doubles before an ending: *club → clubbed, clubbing*.

To beat someone or something with a heavy stick.

1. Hunters still *club* seals on some of those beaches.
2. He was *clubbed* over the head and robbed.
3. Police were filmed *clubbing* protesters outside the gate.

---

### cluster

**Pronunciation:** /ˈklʌstər/ &middot; *KLUS-tur*
**Русский:** скапливаться, собираться группой, группировать
**Forms:** *cluster* &middot; *clustered* &middot; *clustered*
**Third person:** *clusters* &middot; **-ing form:** *clustering*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*cluster the chairs by the window*), intransitive (*reporters clustered outside*)

To gather in a tight group, or to put things into one.

1. Shops *cluster* around the train station.
2. Reporters *clustered* outside the front gate.
3. The new cases are *clustered* in three neighborhoods.

---

### clutter

**Pronunciation:** /ˈklʌtər/ &middot; *KLUT-ur*
**Русский:** загромождать, захламлять, заваливать
**Forms:** *clutter* &middot; *cluttered* &middot; *cluttered*
**Third person:** *clutters* &middot; **-ing form:** *cluttering*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To fill a space untidily with too many things.

1. Don't *clutter* the slide with text.
2. Boxes *cluttered* the hallway for weeks.
3. His desk is *cluttered* with old mail and receipts.

---

### co-opt

**Pronunciation:** /koʊˈɑːpt/ &middot; *koh-AHPT*
**Русский:** присваивать, присвоить; переманивать на свою сторону; кооптировать
**Forms:** *co-opt* &middot; *co-opted* &middot; *co-opted*
**Third person:** *co-opts* &middot; **-ing form:** *co-opting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Written with a hyphen in American English: *co-opt*, *co-opted*. It has nothing to do with *cooped up*.

To take over an idea or a group for your own purposes, or to bring someone onto a committee without an election.

1. Big brands routinely *co-opt* the language of protest.
2. The board *co-opted* two new members in June.
3. Organizers complained of being *co-opted* by the party.

---

### coalesce

**Pronunciation:** /ˌkoʊəˈlɛs/ &middot; *koh-uh-LES*
**Русский:** объединяться, объединиться, сливаться
**Forms:** *coalesce* &middot; *coalesced* &middot; *coalesced*
**Third person:** *coalesces* &middot; **-ing form:** *coalescing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** Support *coalesces **around*** a person or idea.

To come together and form a single group or whole.

1. Small protests sometimes *coalesce* into a movement.
2. Support *coalesced* around a single candidate.
3. The story is still *coalescing* in her head.

---

### coauthor

**Pronunciation:** /koʊˈɔːθər/ &middot; *koh-AW-thur*
**Русский:** быть соавтором, писать в соавторстве
**Forms:** *coauthor* &middot; *coauthored* &middot; *coauthored*
**Third person:** *coauthors* &middot; **-ing form:** *coauthoring*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Often written with a hyphen, *co-author*; both spellings are standard in American English.

To write a book, paper, or report together with someone else.

1. The two scientists *coauthor* a paper almost every year.
2. She *coauthored* the study with her former student.
3. The bill was *coauthored* by members of both parties.

---

### cock

**Pronunciation:** /kɑːk/ &middot; *KAHK*
**Русский:** наклонять набок (голову), взводить (курок)
**Forms:** *cock* &middot; *cocked* &middot; *cocked*
**Third person:** *cocks* &middot; **-ing form:** *cocking*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To tilt part of the body to one side, or to pull back the hammer of a gun.

1. Dogs *cock* their heads when they hear a strange sound.
2. She *cocked* an eyebrow but said nothing.
3. He was *cocking* the rifle when the deer bolted.

---

### codify

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑːdəfaɪ/ &middot; *KAH-duh-fy*
**Русский:** кодифицировать, закреплять законодательно
**Forms:** *codify* &middot; *codified* &middot; *codified*
**Third person:** *codifies* &middot; **-ing form:** *codifying*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The *-y* changes to *-ie-*: *codify → codifies, codified*, but *codifying* keeps the *y*.

To write rules or practices down as a formal, official system.

1. Lawmakers want to *codify* the right in state law.
2. The agreement *codified* what had been an informal habit.
3. Those safety standards were *codified* in the 1970s.

---

### cohabit

**Pronunciation:** /koʊˈhæbɪt/ &middot; *koh-HAB-it*
**Русский:** сожительствовать, жить вместе (без брака)
**Forms:** *cohabit* &middot; *cohabited* &middot; *cohabited*
**Third person:** *cohabits* &middot; **-ing form:** *cohabiting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** One *t* in American English: *cohabited, cohabiting*. You *cohabit **with*** someone.

To live together as a couple without being married.

1. More couples *cohabit* now than a generation ago.
2. They *cohabited* for six years before the wedding.
3. She has been *cohabiting* with her partner since college.

---

### coil

**Pronunciation:** /kɔɪl/ &middot; *KOYL*
**Русский:** свёртывать кольцами, сворачиваться, наматывать
**Forms:** *coil* &middot; *coiled* &middot; *coiled*
**Third person:** *coils* &middot; **-ing form:** *coiling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*coil the hose*), intransitive (*the snake coiled*)

To wind something into a ring or spiral, or to take that shape.

1. *Coil* the hose before you put it away.
2. The snake *coiled* around a low branch.
3. A long cable was *coiled* neatly by the door.

---

### coin

**Pronunciation:** /kɔɪn/ &middot; *KOYN*
**Русский:** придумывать (слово, выражение), вводить в обиход
**Forms:** *coin* &middot; *coined* &middot; *coined*
**Third person:** *coins* &middot; **-ing form:** *coining*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To invent a new word or phrase.

1. Journalists *coin* new terms every election.
2. He *coined* the phrase in a 1998 essay.
3. The word was *coined* long before the internet existed.

---

### coincide

**Pronunciation:** /ˌkoʊɪnˈsaɪd/ &middot; *koh-in-SYD*
**Русский:** совпадать, совпасть
**Forms:** *coincide* &middot; *coincided* &middot; *coincided*
**Third person:** *coincides* &middot; **-ing form:** *coinciding*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** One event *coincides **with*** another.

To happen at the same time as something else, or to match it exactly.

1. The two holidays sometimes *coincide*.
2. Her visit *coincided* with the first snow of the year.
3. The dates have *coincided* three years in a row.

---

### collate

**Pronunciation:** /kəˈleɪt/ &middot; *kuh-LAYT*
**Русский:** сопоставлять, сличать, подбирать по порядку
**Forms:** *collate* &middot; *collated* &middot; *collated*
**Third person:** *collates* &middot; **-ing form:** *collating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To gather information or pages together and put them in order.

1. The office *collates* the results every Friday.
2. She *collated* the survey answers into one spreadsheet.
3. The copies have already been *collated* and stapled.

---

### collude

**Pronunciation:** /kəˈluːd/ &middot; *kuh-LOOD*
**Русский:** вступать в сговор, тайно сговариваться
**Forms:** *collude* &middot; *colluded* &middot; *colluded*
**Third person:** *colludes* &middot; **-ing form:** *colluding*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** You *collude **with*** someone ***on*** or ***in*** something.

To cooperate secretly in order to cheat or deceive other people.

1. Regulators asked whether the airlines *collude* on prices.
2. Two suppliers *colluded* to keep their bids high.
3. He denied ever *colluding* with anyone in the company.

---

### colonize

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑːlənaɪz/ &middot; *KAH-luh-nyz*
**Русский:** колонизировать, заселять
**Forms:** *colonize* &middot; *colonized* &middot; *colonized*
**Third person:** *colonizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *colonizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** British English writes *colonise*; American English uses *colonize*.

To settle in a place and take control of it, or (of plants and animals) to spread into a new area.

1. Bacteria *colonize* the gut in the first weeks of life.
2. Spain *colonized* the region in the sixteenth century.
3. The islands were *colonized* twice within a hundred years.

---

### commandeer

**Pronunciation:** /ˌkɑːmənˈdɪr/ &middot; *kah-muhn-DEER*
**Русский:** реквизировать, захватывать, присваивать
**Forms:** *commandeer* &middot; *commandeered* &middot; *commandeered*
**Third person:** *commandeers* &middot; **-ing form:** *commandeering*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To take something for your own use, especially by force or official authority.

1. Troops sometimes *commandeer* private vehicles.
2. The police *commandeered* a taxi to follow the van.
3. Our meeting room has been *commandeered* by the sales team.

---

### commemorate

**Pronunciation:** /kəˈmɛməreɪt/ &middot; *kuh-MEM-uh-rayt*
**Русский:** чтить память, отмечать (годовщину), увековечивать
**Forms:** *commemorate* &middot; *commemorated* &middot; *commemorated*
**Third person:** *commemorates* &middot; **-ing form:** *commemorating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To do something to honor the memory of a person or an event.

1. Each year they *commemorate* the fire with a moment of silence.
2. The stamp *commemorated* the first moon landing.
3. The city is *commemorating* the anniversary with a free concert.

---

### commercialize

**Pronunciation:** /kəˈmɜːrʃəlaɪz/ &middot; *kuh-MUR-shuh-lyz*
**Русский:** коммерциализировать, ставить на коммерческую основу
**Forms:** *commercialize* &middot; *commercialized* &middot; *commercialized*
**Third person:** *commercializes* &middot; **-ing form:** *commercializing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** British English writes *commercialise*; American English uses *commercialize*.

To turn something into a business that makes money, often spoiling its original character.

1. Startups race to *commercialize* the technology.
2. Critics say the holiday has been *commercialized* beyond recognition.
3. They are *commercializing* research that took decades to finish.

---

### commit

**Pronunciation:** /kəˈmɪt/ &middot; *kuh-MIT*
**Русский:** совершать, совершить (преступление, ошибку); выделять (средства)
**Forms:** *commit* &middot; *committed* &middot; *committed*
**Third person:** *commits* &middot; **-ing form:** *committing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The *t* doubles before an ending: *commit → committed, committing* — but the noun *commitment* keeps a single *t*.

To carry out something wrong or serious, or to set aside money, people, or time for a purpose.

1. Most people who *commit* this offense never go to jail.
2. He *committed* the same error twice in one game.
3. The company has *committed* two million dollars to the project.

---

### compact

**Pronunciation:** /kəmˈpækt/ &middot; *kuhm-PAKT*
**Русский:** уплотнять, утрамбовывать, спрессовывать
**Forms:** *compact* &middot; *compacted* &middot; *compacted*
**Third person:** *compacts* &middot; **-ing form:** *compacting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The verb is stressed on the second syllable, kuhm-PAKT; the noun and adjective *compact* (*a compact car*) are stressed on the first, KAHM-pakt.

To press something firmly together so that it takes up less space.

1. These machines *compact* the trash into solid blocks.
2. Heavy trucks *compacted* the soil along the path.
3. The snow had been *compacted* into a sheet of ice.

---

### compel

**Pronunciation:** /kəmˈpɛl/ &middot; *kuhm-PEL*
**Русский:** принуждать, вынуждать, заставлять
**Forms:** *compel* &middot; *compelled* &middot; *compelled*
**Third person:** *compels* &middot; **-ing form:** *compelling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The *l* doubles before an ending: *compel → compelled, compelling*.

To force someone to do something, or to make something unavoidable.

1. A court can *compel* a witness to testify.
2. Bad weather *compelled* them to turn back.
3. He felt *compelled* to apologize in front of everyone.

---

### compound

**Pronunciation:** /kəmˈpaʊnd/ &middot; *kuhm-POWND*
**Русский:** усугублять, усугубить; начислять сложные проценты
**Forms:** *compound* &middot; *compounded* &middot; *compounded*
**Third person:** *compounds* &middot; **-ing form:** *compounding*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The verb is kuhm-POWND; the noun *compound* (*a chemical compound*, *a walled compound*) is KAHM-pownd.

To make a problem worse, or to add interest to a sum so that it grows on itself.

1. Late fees only *compound* the debt.
2. Heavy rain *compounded* the traffic problems.
3. The interest is *compounded* monthly, not yearly.

---

### comprise

**Pronunciation:** /kəmˈpraɪz/ &middot; *kuhm-PRYZ*
**Русский:** состоять из, включать в себя
**Forms:** *comprise* &middot; *comprised* &middot; *comprised*
**Third person:** *comprises* &middot; **-ing form:** *comprising*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The whole *comprises* the parts, never the other way round; careful writers avoid *is comprised of* and use *consists of* or *is composed of* instead.

To be made up of a number of parts.

1. Four small parties *comprise* the ruling coalition.
2. The old town *comprised* nothing but a church and a store.
3. Eight states, *comprising* a third of the population, opposed the plan.

---

### con

**Pronunciation:** /kɑːn/ &middot; *KAHN*
**Русский:** обманывать, обмануть, надувать
**Forms:** *con* &middot; *conned* &middot; *conned*
**Third person:** *cons* &middot; **-ing form:** *conning*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The *n* doubles before an ending: *con → conned, conning*.

To cheat someone out of money or trust by lying to them.

1. Scammers *con* older people out of their savings every day.
2. He *conned* her into signing the papers.
3. She realized she had been *conned* only weeks later.

---

### conceptualize

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈsɛptʃuəlaɪz/ &middot; *kuhn-SEP-choo-uh-lyz*
**Русский:** осмыслять, представлять концептуально, формулировать
**Forms:** *conceptualize* &middot; *conceptualized* &middot; *conceptualized*
**Third person:** *conceptualizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *conceptualizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** British English writes *conceptualise*; American English uses *conceptualize*.

To form a clear general idea of something, or to think about it in a particular way.

1. It helps to *conceptualize* the problem as a network.
2. Early doctors *conceptualized* the disease as a punishment.
3. We are still *conceptualizing* the next version of the app.

---

### concern

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈsɜːrn/ &middot; *kuhn-SURN*
**Русский:** касаться, затрагивать; беспокоить, волновать
**Forms:** *concern* &middot; *concerned* &middot; *concerned*
**Third person:** *concerns* &middot; **-ing form:** *concerning*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** You are *concerned **about*** a worry, but *concerned **with*** a subject you deal with.

To affect or involve someone, or to make them worried.

1. These rules *concern* everyone in the building.
2. The test results *concerned* her doctor.
3. Parents have been *concerned* about the new schedule.

---

### concoct

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈkɑːkt/ &middot; *kuhn-KAHKT*
**Русский:** сочинять, выдумывать; состряпать
**Forms:** *concoct* &middot; *concocted* &middot; *concocted*
**Third person:** *concocts* &middot; **-ing form:** *concocting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To invent a story or excuse, or to put together a dish from whatever is at hand.

1. He can *concoct* a meal out of almost nothing.
2. They *concocted* an alibi on the drive home.
3. She was *concocting* some sort of punch in the kitchen.

---

### concur

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈkɜːr/ &middot; *kuhn-KUR*
**Русский:** соглашаться, согласиться, сходиться во мнении
**Forms:** *concur* &middot; *concurred* &middot; *concurred*
**Third person:** *concurs* &middot; **-ing form:** *concurring*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** The *r* doubles before an ending: *concur → concurred, concurring*. You *concur **with*** a person and *concur **on*** or ***in*** a point.

To agree with someone or with an opinion.

1. Most experts *concur* on that point.
2. Two of the justices *concurred* but wrote separately.
3. Having *concurred* on the facts, the two sides still argued about the penalty.

---

### concuss

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈkʌs/ &middot; *kuhn-KUS*
**Русский:** вызывать сотрясение мозга
**Forms:** *concuss* &middot; *concussed* &middot; *concussed*
**Third person:** *concusses* &middot; **-ing form:** *concussing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Takes *-es* in the third person: *concuss → concusses*. The noun is *concussion*.

To injure someone's brain with a blow to the head.

1. A hit like that can *concuss* a player even with a helmet on.
2. He *concussed* himself falling off a ladder.
3. Two of their forwards have been *concussed* this month.

---

### condescend

**Pronunciation:** /ˌkɑːndɪˈsɛnd/ &middot; *kahn-di-SEND*
**Русский:** держаться свысока, снисходить
**Forms:** *condescend* &middot; *condescended* &middot; *condescended*
**Third person:** *condescends* &middot; **-ing form:** *condescending*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** You *condescend **to*** someone. The participle *condescending* is used as an adjective: *a condescending tone*.

To treat someone as if you were better than they are, or to lower yourself to do something.

1. Try not to *condescend* to the audience.
2. He finally *condescended* to explain it one more time.
3. Nobody likes being *condescended* to.

---

### conflate

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈfleɪt/ &middot; *kuhn-FLAYT*
**Русский:** смешивать, отождествлять (разные понятия)
**Forms:** *conflate* &middot; *conflated* &middot; *conflated*
**Third person:** *conflates* &middot; **-ing form:** *conflating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** You *conflate* one thing ***with*** another.

To treat two different things as if they were one and the same.

1. People often *conflate* the two diseases.
2. The article *conflated* cost and value.
3. Those two terms are constantly being *conflated* in the press.

---

### conflict

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈflɪkt/ &middot; *kuhn-FLIKT*
