**Русский:** противоречить, расходиться, вступать в противоречие
**Forms:** *conflict* &middot; *conflicted* &middot; *conflicted*
**Third person:** *conflicts* &middot; **-ing form:** *conflicting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** The verb is kuhn-FLIKT; the noun *conflict* (*an armed conflict*) is KAHN-flikt. One thing *conflicts **with*** another.

To clash with something else, or to be in opposition to it.

1. The two accounts *conflict* on almost every detail.
2. Her class schedule *conflicted* with the meeting.
3. We found *conflicting* reports about the time of the crash.

---

### conform

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈfɔːrm/ &middot; *kuhn-FORM*
**Русский:** соответствовать; подчиняться, следовать правилам
**Forms:** *conform* &middot; *conformed* &middot; *conformed*
**Third person:** *conforms* &middot; **-ing form:** *conforming*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive — with *to* or *with*
**Contrast:** Takes *to* (or, for agreements, *with*) and never a bare object: ✗ *conform the rules* → *conform to the rules*. Do not confuse it with *confirm* /kənˈfɜːrm/, which means to verify — one letter apart, and a mistake spellcheckers will not catch.

To follow a rule, a standard, or what everyone else does.

1. The wiring *conforms* to the current code.
2. He never *conformed*, and it cost him the job.
3. All units shipped since March have *conformed* to the new spec.

---

### confound

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈfaʊnd/ &middot; *kuhn-FOWND*
**Русский:** сбивать с толку, озадачивать; опровергать (ожидания)
**Forms:** *confound* &middot; *confounded* &middot; *confounded*
**Third person:** *confounds* &middot; **-ing form:** *confounding*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To confuse or surprise someone by defeating what they expected.

1. The results *confound* everything we assumed.
2. The team *confounded* its critics and won the title.
3. Researchers worry that age is *confounding* the data.

---

### congeal

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈdʒiːl/ &middot; *kuhn-JEEL*
**Русский:** застывать, застыть; свёртываться, свернуться
**Forms:** *congeal* &middot; *congealed* &middot; *congealed*
**Third person:** *congeals* &middot; **-ing form:** *congealing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (the cold congealed the gravy), intransitive (the gravy congealed)

To become thick and solid as a liquid cools, or to make a liquid do this.

1. Leave the soup out and the fat will *congeal* on top.
2. By morning the gravy had *congealed* into a gray slab.
3. He watched the wax *congealing* around the wick.

---

### congest

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈdʒɛst/ &middot; *kuhn-JEST*
**Русский:** перегружать, перегрузить; забивать, закупоривать
**Forms:** *congest* &middot; *congested* &middot; *congested*
**Third person:** *congests* &middot; **-ing form:** *congesting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To block a road, a network or the nose so that nothing moves through freely.

1. Delivery trucks *congest* these narrow streets every afternoon.
2. The accident *congested* the whole interstate for hours.
3. His sinuses were badly *congested* after three days of the cold.

---

### conjecture

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈdʒɛktʃər/ &middot; *kuhn-JEK-chur*
**Русский:** предполагать, предположить; строить догадки
**Forms:** *conjecture* &middot; *conjectured* &middot; *conjectured*
**Third person:** *conjectures* &middot; **-ing form:** *conjecturing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (she conjectured that he had left), intransitive (we can only conjecture)

To form an opinion from incomplete evidence.

1. Historians can only *conjecture* what the room looked like.
2. She *conjectured* that the letter had never been mailed.
3. Reporters were *conjecturing* about a resignation weeks before it came.

---

### conjoin

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈdʒɔɪn/ &middot; *kuhn-JOYN*
**Русский:** соединять, соединить; сращивать, срастить
**Forms:** *conjoin* &middot; *conjoined* &middot; *conjoined*
**Third person:** *conjoins* &middot; **-ing form:** *conjoining*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (the essay conjoins two arguments), intransitive (the two rivers conjoin)
**Contrast:** You will meet this verb mostly as the participle in *conjoined twins*. For everyday joining, use *join* or *combine*.

To join two things into one.

1. The two rivers *conjoin* just north of the city.
2. Surgeons separated twins who had been *conjoined* at the hip.
3. His article *conjoined* two arguments that do not belong together.

---

### connive

**Pronunciation:** /kəˈnaɪv/ &middot; *kuh-NYV*
**Русский:** тайно сговариваться, сговориться; потворствовать
**Forms:** *connive* &middot; *connived* &middot; *connived*
**Third person:** *connives* &middot; **-ing form:** *conniving*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive

To cooperate secretly in something dishonest, or to look the other way while it happens.

1. Officials who *connive* with smugglers are rarely punished.
2. They *connived* to keep her name off the list.
3. He has a *conniving* smile that nobody in the office trusts.

---

### connote

**Pronunciation:** /kəˈnoʊt/ &middot; *kuh-NOHT*
**Русский:** подразумевать; нести оттенок значения
**Forms:** *connote* &middot; *connoted* &middot; *connoted*
**Third person:** *connotes* &middot; **-ing form:** *connoting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** *Connote* is what a word suggests; *denote* is what it plainly means. *Slender* and *scrawny* denote the same thing but connote very different attitudes.

To suggest a feeling or an idea in addition to a word's plain meaning.

1. In American English, 'cheap' often *connotes* poor quality.
2. The word 'notorious' has always *connoted* fame of the worst kind.
3. 'Thrifty' and 'stingy' describe the same habit while *connoting* opposite judgments.

---

### conscript

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈskrɪpt/ &middot; *kuhn-SKRIPT*
**Русский:** призывать на военную службу, призвать
**Forms:** *conscript* &middot; *conscripted* &middot; *conscripted*
**Third person:** *conscripts* &middot; **-ing form:** *conscripting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The verb stresses the second syllable (kuhn-SKRIPT); the noun, a person who has been drafted, stresses the first (KAHN-skript).

To force someone by law to serve in the armed forces.

1. Some countries still *conscript* every young man at eighteen.
2. His grandfather was *conscripted* in 1943.
3. The army kept *conscripting* farmers until the harvest failed.

---

### consecrate

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑːnsəkreɪt/ &middot; *KAHN-suh-krayt*
**Русский:** освящать, освятить; посвящать, посвятить
**Forms:** *consecrate* &middot; *consecrated* &middot; *consecrated*
**Third person:** *consecrates* &middot; **-ing form:** *consecrating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To declare something sacred in a religious ceremony, or to devote it entirely to a purpose.

1. The bishop will *consecrate* the new chapel next spring.
2. The cemetery was *consecrated* in 1847.
3. They are *consecrating* the bread and wine now.

---

### consign

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈsaɪn/ &middot; *kuhn-SYN*
**Русский:** передавать, передать; отправлять; обрекать, обречь
**Forms:** *consign* &middot; *consigned* &middot; *consigned*
**Third person:** *consigns* &middot; **-ing form:** *consigning*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Almost always followed by *to*: *consigned to* the archives, *consigned to* history.

To hand something or someone over, especially to an unwanted place or fate.

1. Editors *consign* most manuscripts to a pile nobody reads.
2. She *consigned* her old coats to a secondhand shop.
3. The reform was *consigned* to a committee and never heard of again.

---

### consist

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈsɪst/ &middot; *kuhn-SIST*
**Русский:** состоять (из), заключаться (в)
**Forms:** *consist* &middot; *consisted* &middot; *consisted*
**Third person:** *consists* &middot; **-ing form:** *consisting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive — always with *of* or *in*, never with a bare object
**Contrast:** *Consist* never takes a plain object and is **never passive**: ✗ *the team is consisted of* — say *the team consists of* or *the team is made up of*. Two prepositions, two meanings: *consist **of*** lists the physical parts (*water consists of hydrogen and oxygen*), while *consist **in*** names where something's essence lies (*her charm consists in never trying*). It also has no progressive — ✗ *is consisting of*.

To be made up of the parts that are then listed.

1. The test *consists* of forty questions and one essay.
2. Breakfast *consisted* of coffee and whatever was left in the fridge.
3. The course has always *consisted* of a lecture plus a lab.

---

### constitute

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑːnstətuːt/ &middot; *KAHN-stuh-toot*
**Русский:** составлять, составить; являться (чем-л.)
**Forms:** *constitute* &middot; *constituted* &middot; *constituted*
**Third person:** *constitutes* &middot; **-ing form:** *constituting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To be the parts that form a whole, or to amount officially to something.

1. Women *constitute* nearly two-thirds of the staff.
2. The judge ruled that the messages *constituted* a threat.
3. The review panel was *constituted* in 2019.

---

### constrain

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈstreɪn/ &middot; *kuhn-STRAYN*
**Русский:** ограничивать, ограничить; сковывать, вынуждать
**Forms:** *constrain* &middot; *constrained* &middot; *constrained*
**Third person:** *constrains* &middot; **-ing form:** *constraining*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To keep something within limits, or to force someone to act in a particular way.

1. Tight budgets *constrain* what the department can promise.
2. The new rules *constrained* banks from lending so freely.
3. We are *constrained* by the size of the room, not by the money.

---

### constrict

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈstrɪkt/ &middot; *kuhn-STRIKT*
**Русский:** сужать, сузить; сжимать, сжать
**Forms:** *constrict* &middot; *constricted* &middot; *constricted*
**Third person:** *constricts* &middot; **-ing form:** *constricting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (cold constricts the blood vessels), intransitive (the airway constricts)

To make something narrower or tighter, or to become narrower.

1. Cold air *constricts* the blood vessels in your fingers.
2. The collar *constricted* his throat every time he swallowed.
3. Her airways were *constricting*, so they reached for the inhaler.

---

### construe

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈstruː/ &middot; *kuhn-STROO*
**Русский:** истолковывать, истолковать; понимать (как)
**Forms:** *construe* &middot; *construed* &middot; *construed*
**Third person:** *construes* &middot; **-ing form:** *construing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The final *-e* disappears in the *-ing* form: *construing*, not *construeing*.

To understand a word or an action as having a particular meaning.

1. Lawyers *construe* the phrase far more narrowly than the public does.
2. She *construed* his silence as agreement.
3. Any gift over fifty dollars could be *construed* as a bribe.

---

### consummate

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑːnsəmeɪt/ &middot; *KAHN-suh-mayt*
**Русский:** завершать, завершить; доводить до конца
**Forms:** *consummate* &middot; *consummated* &middot; *consummated*
**Third person:** *consummates* &middot; **-ing form:** *consummating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The verb is KAHN-suh-mayt; the adjective meaning 'perfect, complete' is kuhn-SUH-muht — *a consummate professional*.

To complete something, especially a deal or a marriage.

1. It takes months to *consummate* a sale this size.
2. The merger was *consummated* in January.
3. They spent the week *consummating* an agreement that had taken years to draft.

---

### contextualize

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈtɛkstʃuəlaɪz/ &middot; *kuhn-TEKS-choo-uh-lyz*
**Русский:** рассматривать в контексте, поместить в контекст
**Forms:** *contextualize* &middot; *contextualized* &middot; *contextualized*
**Third person:** *contextualizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *contextualizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** American English spells it *-ize*; British writing usually has *contextualise*.

To place a fact or an event in its wider setting so that it makes sense.

1. Good museums *contextualize* the objects they display.
2. The professor *contextualized* the letter within the debates of the 1850s.
3. Without *contextualizing* the figures, the report is simply misleading.

---

### contort

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈtɔːrt/ &middot; *kuhn-TORT*
**Русский:** искажать, исказить; скручивать, искривлять
**Forms:** *contort* &middot; *contorted* &middot; *contorted*
**Third person:** *contorts* &middot; **-ing form:** *contorting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (pain contorts his face), intransitive (his face contorted)

To twist something out of its normal shape, especially the face or the body.

1. Fear can *contort* a familiar face into something unrecognizable.
2. He *contorted* himself to reach the pipe behind the sink.
3. Her mouth was *contorted* with pain.

---

### contour

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑːntʊr/ &middot; *KAHN-toor*
**Русский:** моделировать по форме, очерчивать контур
**Forms:** *contour* &middot; *contoured* &middot; *contoured*
**Third person:** *contours* &middot; **-ing form:** *contouring*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To shape something so that it follows the curves of a surface or a body.

1. Engineers *contour* the seat to fit the driver's back.
2. The road was *contoured* to follow the hillside.
3. She spends ten minutes *contouring* her cheekbones every morning.

---

### contravene

**Pronunciation:** /ˌkɑːntrəˈviːn/ &middot; *kahn-truh-VEEN*
**Русский:** нарушать, нарушить (закон, правило); противоречить
**Forms:** *contravene* &middot; *contravened* &middot; *contravened*
**Third person:** *contravenes* &middot; **-ing form:** *contravening*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To break a law, a rule or an agreement.

1. Those ads *contravene* state advertising law.
2. The company *contravened* the terms of its own permit.
3. He was fired for *contravening* a direct order.

---

### contrive

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈtraɪv/ &middot; *kuhn-TRYV*
**Русский:** ухитряться, ухитриться; изобретать, измышлять
**Forms:** *contrive* &middot; *contrived* &middot; *contrived*
**Third person:** *contrives* &middot; **-ing form:** *contriving*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** As an adjective, *contrived* means artificial and unconvincing — a *contrived* plot, a *contrived* apology.

To invent or arrange something with effort or cleverness, often in a devious way.

1. Somehow they *contrive* to finish every project on the last day.
2. He *contrived* a way to open the lock with a paper clip.
3. The ending felt *contrived*, as if the writer had run out of ideas.

---

### convalesce

**Pronunciation:** /ˌkɑːnvəˈlɛs/ &middot; *kahn-vuh-LES*
**Русский:** выздоравливать, выздороветь; поправляться, поправиться
**Forms:** *convalesce* &middot; *convalesced* &middot; *convalesced*
**Third person:** *convalesces* &middot; **-ing form:** *convalescing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive

To rest and slowly get your strength back after an illness or an operation.

1. Patients *convalesce* faster at home than on a ward.
2. She *convalesced* at her sister's place for a month.
3. He is still *convalescing* and cannot travel.

---

### converge

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈvɜːrdʒ/ &middot; *kuhn-VURJ*
**Русский:** сходиться, сойтись; сближаться
**Forms:** *converge* &middot; *converged* &middot; *converged*
**Third person:** *converges* &middot; **-ing form:** *converging*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive — often with *on* or *at*
**Contrast:** The opposite is *diverge*. It is intransitive: things converge, but you do not ✗ *converge them* — for that use *bring together* or *merge*. With *on*, it carries a sense of closing in from all sides, which is why it suits crowds and search parties.

To come together from different directions toward one point or one result.

1. Three trails *converge* at the summit.
2. Reporters *converged* on the courthouse by noon.
3. The two estimates have slowly *converged*.

---

### convulse

**Pronunciation:** /kənˈvʌls/ &middot; *kuhn-VULS*
**Русский:** сотрясать, сотрясти; биться в судорогах
**Forms:** *convulse* &middot; *convulsed* &middot; *convulsed*
**Third person:** *convulses* &middot; **-ing form:** *convulsing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (the strike convulsed the capital), intransitive (the patient convulsed)

To shake violently and uncontrollably, or to make something shake that way.

1. Strikes and protests *convulse* the capital every spring.
2. The child *convulsed* twice before the ambulance arrived.
3. The whole audience was *convulsed* with laughter.

---

### coo

**Pronunciation:** /kuː/ &middot; *KOO*
**Русский:** ворковать; говорить нежно, сюсюкать
**Forms:** *coo* &middot; *cooed* &middot; *cooed*
**Third person:** *coos* &middot; **-ing form:** *cooing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (she cooed a few soft words), intransitive (the pigeons coo)

To make the soft low sound of a pigeon, or to speak in that soft admiring way.

1. Doves *coo* outside the window every morning.
2. She *cooed* over the baby's tiny shoes.
3. He kept *cooing* to the dog until it calmed down.

---

### copulate

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑːpjəleɪt/ &middot; *KAH-pyuh-layt*
**Русский:** спариваться, спариться; совокупляться
**Forms:** *copulate* &middot; *copulated* &middot; *copulated*
**Third person:** *copulates* &middot; **-ing form:** *copulating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive

To have sexual intercourse, said especially of animals.

1. Most frogs *copulate* in shallow water.
2. The pair *copulated* within minutes of meeting.
3. Two beetles were *copulating* on the underside of the leaf.

---

### copyright

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɑːpiraɪt/ &middot; *KAH-pee-ryt*
**Русский:** защищать авторским правом, закрепить авторские права
**Forms:** *copyright* &middot; *copyrighted* &middot; *copyrighted*
**Third person:** *copyrights* &middot; **-ing form:** *copyrighting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The past is *copyrighted*, never *copywrote* — the word is built on *right*, not on *write*.

To register a legal right over a work so that others may not copy it.

1. Photographers *copyright* their best images before posting them.
2. The song was *copyrighted* in 1968.
3. She is *copyrighting* the whole series this month.

---

### core

**Pronunciation:** /kɔːr/ &middot; *KOR*
**Русский:** вырезать сердцевину, удалять сердцевину
**Forms:** *core* &middot; *cored* &middot; *cored*
**Third person:** *cores* &middot; **-ing form:** *coring*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To cut the hard center out of a fruit or a vegetable.

1. *Core* the apples before you slice them.
2. He *cored* and peeled a whole basket of pears.
3. A cheap plastic gadget makes *coring* tomatoes easy.

---

### cork

**Pronunciation:** /kɔːrk/ &middot; *KORK*
**Русский:** закупоривать пробкой, закупорить
**Forms:** *cork* &middot; *corked* &middot; *corked*
**Third person:** *corks* &middot; **-ing form:** *corking*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To close a bottle with a cork.

1. Small wineries still *cork* their best bottles by hand.
2. He *corked* the bottle and put it back on the shelf.
3. It had been *corked* so tightly that we broke the opener.

---

### corner

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɔːrnər/ &middot; *KOR-nur*
**Русский:** загонять в угол, загнать в угол; монополизировать (рынок)
**Forms:** *corner* &middot; *cornered* &middot; *cornered*
**Third person:** *corners* &middot; **-ing form:** *cornering*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To trap someone where there is no escape, or to take control of a whole market.

1. Two firms *corner* almost the entire market for the chip.
2. A reporter *cornered* the mayor outside the hall.
3. The dog was *cornered* behind the shed and would not move.

---

### corral

**Pronunciation:** /kəˈræl/ &middot; *kuh-RAL*
**Русский:** загонять в загон, загнать; собирать вместе, согнать
**Forms:** *corral* &middot; *corralled* &middot; *corralled*
**Third person:** *corrals* &middot; **-ing form:** *corralling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Stress falls on the second syllable, so the *l* doubles: *corralled*, *corralling*.

To drive animals into a pen, or to gather people or things into one place.

1. Teachers *corral* the kids into two lines before the bus arrives.
2. Volunteers *corralled* the loose goats by nightfall.
3. He spent the morning *corralling* votes for the amendment.

---

### corroborate

**Pronunciation:** /kəˈrɑːbəreɪt/ &middot; *kuh-RAH-buh-rayt*
**Русский:** подтверждать, подтвердить (доказательствами)
**Forms:** *corroborate* &middot; *corroborated* &middot; *corroborated*
**Third person:** *corroborates* &middot; **-ing form:** *corroborating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To support a statement with further evidence.

1. Two witnesses *corroborate* her account.
2. Phone records *corroborated* what he had told the police.
3. The story ran without a single *corroborating* document.

---

### corrupt

**Pronunciation:** /kəˈrʌpt/ &middot; *kuh-RUPT*
**Русский:** развращать, развратить; портить, испортить (данные)
**Forms:** *corrupt* &middot; *corrupted* &middot; *corrupted*
**Third person:** *corrupts* &middot; **-ing form:** *corrupting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To make someone dishonest, or to damage a file so that it can no longer be read.

1. Cheap money *corrupts* local politics faster than anything else.
2. The power outage *corrupted* half the database.
3. He claims the files were already *corrupted* when he took the job.

---

### cosign

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkoʊsaɪn/ &middot; *KOH-syn*
**Русский:** подписывать вместе, подписать; выступать поручителем
**Forms:** *cosign* &middot; *cosigned* &middot; *cosigned*
**Third person:** *cosigns* &middot; **-ing form:** *cosigning*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Sounds exactly like *cosine*, the math term, but they are unrelated words.

To sign a loan or a lease together with someone and share the responsibility for it.

1. Few parents want to *cosign* a car loan.
2. Her uncle *cosigned* the lease so she could move in.
3. Nobody told him that *cosigning* makes him liable for every payment.

---

### cosponsor

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkoʊspɑːnsər/ &middot; *KOH-spahn-sur*
**Русский:** выступать соавтором (законопроекта), совместно спонсировать
**Forms:** *cosponsor* &middot; *cosponsored* &middot; *cosponsored*
**Third person:** *cosponsors* &middot; **-ing form:** *cosponsoring*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To put your name to a bill or an event together with someone else.

1. Both senators *cosponsor* the bill every year.
2. She *cosponsored* the measure with a colleague from Ohio.
3. The conference is *cosponsored* by two universities.

---

### costar

**Pronunciation:** /koʊˈstɑːr/ &middot; *koh-STAR*
**Русский:** сниматься вместе в главных ролях, сыграть вместе
**Forms:** *costar* &middot; *costarred* &middot; *costarred*
**Third person:** *costars* &middot; **-ing form:** *costarring*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (the film costars two unknowns), intransitive (she costars with him)
**Contrast:** The stress on the last syllable doubles the *r*: *costarred*, *costarring*.

To appear as one of the leading actors alongside another.

1. The two of them *costar* in a new detective series.
2. She *costarred* with him in three films.
3. He is *costarring* opposite an actor half his age.

---

### couch

**Pronunciation:** /kaʊtʃ/ &middot; *KOWCH*
**Русский:** формулировать, сформулировать; облекать в слова
**Forms:** *couch* &middot; *couched* &middot; *couched*
**Third person:** *couches* &middot; **-ing form:** *couching*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Spelled like the furniture, but unrelated. You will nearly always meet it as *couched in*.

To express something in words of a particular kind.

1. Diplomats *couch* refusals in the politest possible language.
2. The warning was *couched* as friendly advice.
3. By *couching* the demand as a question, she made it easy to refuse.

---

### counteract

**Pronunciation:** /ˌkaʊntərˈækt/ &middot; *kown-tur-AKT*
**Русский:** противодействовать; нейтрализовать
**Forms:** *counteract* &middot; *counteracted* &middot; *counteracted*
**Third person:** *counteracts* &middot; **-ing form:** *counteracting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To act against something in order to reduce or cancel its effect.

1. A pinch of sugar *counteracts* the bitterness.
2. The antidote *counteracted* the venom within minutes.
3. Extra lamps are *counteracting* the gloom of the long winter.

---

### counterattack

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkaʊntərətæk/ &middot; *KOWN-tur-uh-tak*
**Русский:** контратаковать; наносить ответный удар
**Forms:** *counterattack* &middot; *counterattacked* &middot; *counterattacked*
**Third person:** *counterattacks* &middot; **-ing form:** *counterattacking*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (they counterattacked the flank), intransitive (the troops counterattacked at dawn)

To attack back after being attacked.

1. Troops rarely *counterattack* before dawn.
2. Her campaign *counterattacked* with an ad of its own.
3. The team was *counterattacking* every time it won the ball.

---

### couple

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkʌpəl/ &middot; *KUH-puhl*
**Русский:** соединять, соединить; сцеплять, сцепить
**Forms:** *couple* &middot; *coupled* &middot; *coupled*
**Third person:** *couples* &middot; **-ing form:** *coupling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (crews couple the cars), intransitive (the units couple easily)
**Contrast:** *Coupled with* is the form you will meet most often; it simply means 'together with'.

To join two things together so that they work as one.

1. Crews *couple* the last two cars before the train leaves.
2. Low pay, *coupled* with long hours, drove half the staff away.
3. Engineers are *coupling* the new module to the station this week.

---

### course

**Pronunciation:** /kɔːrs/ &middot; *KORS*
**Русский:** течь, струиться; бежать (о крови, слезах)
**Forms:** *course* &middot; *coursed* &middot; *coursed*
**Third person:** *courses* &middot; **-ing form:** *coursing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** Nothing to do with a *course* of study: as a verb the word means 'flow', and it almost always takes *through* or *down*.

To flow or run fast, as blood or tears do.

1. Adrenaline *courses* through you before a first jump.
2. Tears *coursed* down her cheeks before she could stop them.
3. He could feel the caffeine *coursing* through him.

---

### covet

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkʌvət/ &middot; *KUH-vuht*
**Русский:** жаждать, страстно желать (чужого); зариться
**Forms:** *covet* &middot; *coveted* &middot; *coveted*
**Third person:** *covets* &middot; **-ing form:** *coveting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The stress is on the first syllable, so the *t* does not double: *coveted*, *coveting* — unlike *permitted*, *permitting*.

To want something badly, especially something that belongs to someone else.

1. Collectors *covet* the first pressing of that record.
2. He had *coveted* the corner office for years.
3. It is the most *coveted* prize in the field.

---

### cow

**Pronunciation:** /kaʊ/ &middot; *KOW*
**Русский:** запугивать, запугать; усмирять, усмирить
**Forms:** *cow* &middot; *cowed* &middot; *cowed*
**Third person:** *cows* &middot; **-ing form:** *cowing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Spelled like the animal, but unrelated: this verb means 'intimidate'.

To frighten someone into obedience.

1. Loud threats *cow* nobody in that courtroom.
2. The new manager *cowed* the whole department within a week.
3. They looked *cowed*, answering only when spoken to.

---

### cower

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkaʊər/ &middot; *KOW-ur*
**Русский:** съёживаться от страха, сжаться; трусливо прятаться
**Forms:** *cower* &middot; *cowered* &middot; *cowered*
**Third person:** *cowers* &middot; **-ing form:** *cowering*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** Do not mix up with *cow* (to intimidate): people *cower* because someone has *cowed* them.

To crouch or shrink back in fear.

1. Dogs *cower* when they hear fireworks.
2. The children *cowered* under the stairs during the storm.
3. He found her *cowering* behind the door.

---

### crane

**Pronunciation:** /kreɪn/ &middot; *KRAYN*
**Русский:** вытягивать шею, вытянуть шею
**Forms:** *crane* &middot; *craned* &middot; *craned*
**Third person:** *cranes* &middot; **-ing form:** *craning*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (she craned her neck), intransitive (fans craned to see)

To stretch your neck in order to see something better.

1. Fans *crane* to see over the row in front of them.
2. She *craned* her neck to read the sign.
3. They stood *craning* at the window as the motorcade passed.

---

### crater

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkreɪtər/ &middot; *KRAY-tur*
**Русский:** резко падать, обвалиться (о ценах); изрывать воронками
**Forms:** *crater* &middot; *cratered* &middot; *cratered*
**Third person:** *craters* &middot; **-ing form:** *cratering*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (shells cratered the road), intransitive (the stock cratered)

To collapse suddenly, said of prices or plans, or to blast holes in a surface.

1. Ticket sales *crater* the week after New Year's.
2. The stock *cratered* after the recall was announced.
3. Shelling had *cratered* every road into the town.

---

### cremate

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkriːmeɪt/ &middot; *KREE-mayt*
**Русский:** кремировать, сжигать (тело)
**Forms:** *cremate* &middot; *cremated* &middot; *cremated*
**Third person:** *cremates* &middot; **-ing form:** *cremating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To burn a dead body to ashes.

1. More families *cremate* their dead than bury them now.
2. He was *cremated* according to his own instructions.
3. They are *cremating* the remains on Thursday.

---

### criminalize

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkrɪmənəlaɪz/ &middot; *KRIM-uh-nuh-lyz*
**Русский:** криминализировать, объявлять преступлением
**Forms:** *criminalize* &middot; *criminalized* &middot; *criminalized*
**Third person:** *criminalizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *criminalizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** American spelling is *-ize*; British writing uses *criminalise*.

To make an act illegal, or to treat a group of people as criminals.

1. Several states *criminalize* the sale of these pills.
2. The 1919 law *criminalized* the sale of alcohol.
3. Critics accuse the bill of *criminalizing* poverty.

---

### cringe

**Pronunciation:** /krɪndʒ/ &middot; *KRINJ*
**Русский:** съёживаться, сжаться; испытывать неловкость
**Forms:** *cringe* &middot; *cringed* &middot; *cringed*
**Third person:** *cringes* &middot; **-ing form:** *cringing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** The *-e* drops before *-ing*: *cringing*, not *cringeing*.

To feel or show sudden embarrassment or fear, pulling back a little.

1. I *cringe* every time I hear my own voice on tape.
2. She *cringed* at the sound of her old nickname.
3. He kept *cringing* through the whole speech.

---

### crinkle

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkrɪŋkəl/ &middot; *KRING-kuhl*
**Русский:** мять, смять; морщиться, сморщиться
**Forms:** *crinkle* &middot; *crinkled* &middot; *crinkled*
**Third person:** *crinkles* &middot; **-ing form:** *crinkling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (she crinkled the wrapper), intransitive (his eyes crinkle)

To form small folds or wrinkles, or to make something do that.

1. His eyes *crinkle* when he laughs.
2. She *crinkled* the wrapper into a tight ball.
3. The foil was *crinkled* but still usable.

---

### crisscross

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkrɪskrɔːs/ &middot; *KRIS-kraws*
**Русский:** пересекать вдоль и поперёк; перекрещиваться
**Forms:** *crisscross* &middot; *crisscrossed* &middot; *crisscrossed*
**Third person:** *crisscrosses* &middot; **-ing form:** *crisscrossing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (trails crisscross the park), intransitive (the lines crisscross)

To move back and forth across an area, or to lie across something in crossing lines.

1. Bike paths *crisscross* the whole park.
2. They *crisscrossed* the country twice that summer.
3. Scars *crisscrossing* his palm told the rest of the story.

---

### crowd

**Pronunciation:** /kraʊd/ &middot; *KROWD*
**Русский:** толпиться, столпиться; теснить, потеснить
**Forms:** *crowd* &middot; *crowded* &middot; *crowded*
**Third person:** *crowds* &middot; **-ing form:** *crowding*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (don't crowd me), intransitive (people crowded into the hall)

To gather closely in large numbers, or to press too close to someone.

1. Shoppers *crowd* the sidewalks in December.
2. Everyone *crowded* into the small kitchen.
3. Stop *crowding* me — I need room to work.

---

### crowdfund

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkraʊdfʌnd/ &middot; *KROWD-fund*
**Русский:** собирать средства краудфандингом, профинансировать сообща
**Forms:** *crowdfund* &middot; *crowdfunded* &middot; *crowdfunded*
**Third person:** *crowdfunds* &middot; **-ing form:** *crowdfunding*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To raise money for a project from many people online.

1. Small studios *crowdfund* almost every new game.
2. They *crowdfunded* the surgery in three days.
3. She is *crowdfunding* a documentary about her hometown.

---

### crowdsource

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkraʊdsɔːrs/ &middot; *KROWD-sors*
**Русский:** привлекать силы сообщества, собирать краудсорсингом
**Forms:** *crowdsource* &middot; *crowdsourced* &middot; *crowdsourced*
**Third person:** *crowdsources* &middot; **-ing form:** *crowdsourcing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To get work, ideas or information from a large group of people, usually online.

1. Map apps *crowdsource* traffic reports from every driver.
2. The dictionary *crowdsourced* its example sentences.
3. We ended up *crowdsourcing* the translation.

---

### crucify

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkruːsəfaɪ/ &middot; *KROO-suh-fy*
**Русский:** распинать, распять; жестоко разносить (критиковать)
**Forms:** *crucify* &middot; *crucified* &middot; *crucified*
**Third person:** *crucifies* &middot; **-ing form:** *crucifying*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The *-y* becomes *-ie-*: *crucifies*, *crucified*, but *crucifying* keeps the *y*.

To put someone to death on a cross; in everyday use, to attack someone savagely in public.

1. The press *crucifies* any candidate who admits a mistake.
2. Reviewers *crucified* the film on opening weekend.
3. He was *crucified* online for one careless sentence.

---

### crusade

**Pronunciation:** /kruːˈseɪd/ &middot; *kroo-SAYD*
**Русский:** вести кампанию, бороться (за что-л.)
**Forms:** *crusade* &middot; *crusaded* &middot; *crusaded*
**Third person:** *crusades* &middot; **-ing form:** *crusading*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive

To campaign long and hard for a cause you believe in.

1. A few reporters still *crusade* against corruption in the statehouse.
2. She *crusaded* for clean water for twenty years.
3. He is remembered as a *crusading* prosecutor.

---

### cube

**Pronunciation:** /kjuːb/ &middot; *KYOOB*
**Русский:** нарезать кубиками; возводить в куб
**Forms:** *cube* &middot; *cubed* &middot; *cubed*
**Third person:** *cubes* &middot; **-ing form:** *cubing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To cut food into small cubes, or in math, to multiply a number by itself twice.

1. *Cube* the potatoes before you boil them.
2. He *cubed* the cheese and dropped it into the pot.
3. Three *cubed* is twenty-seven.

---

### cull

**Pronunciation:** /kʌl/ &middot; *KUL*
**Русский:** отбирать, отобрать; выбраковывать (скот)
**Forms:** *cull* &middot; *culled* &middot; *culled*
**Third person:** *culls* &middot; **-ing form:** *culling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To pick out the best from a group, or to kill some animals in order to reduce a herd.

1. Editors *cull* three stories from a hundred submissions.
2. The state *culled* deer to slow the spread of the disease.
3. Most of the quotes were *culled* from old interviews.

---

### cup

**Pronunciation:** /kʌp/ &middot; *KUP*
**Русский:** складывать ладони горстью; обхватывать ладонями
**Forms:** *cup* &middot; *cupped* &middot; *cupped*
**Third person:** *cups* &middot; **-ing form:** *cupping*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** One short syllable ending in a single consonant, so the *p* doubles: *cupped*, *cupping*.

To curve your hands into the shape of a cup, or to hold something in hands shaped that way.

1. *Cup* your hands and I'll pour some in.
2. She *cupped* the mug with both hands to warm them.
3. He was *cupping* a match against the wind.

---

### curdle

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɜːrdəl/ &middot; *KUR-duhl*
**Русский:** свёртываться, свернуться (о молоке); скисать, скиснуть
**Forms:** *curdle* &middot; *curdled* &middot; *curdled*
**Third person:** *curdles* &middot; **-ing form:** *curdling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (lemon juice curdles milk), intransitive (the cream curdled)

To separate into solid lumps and liquid, as milk does when it turns sour.

1. Lemon juice *curdles* warm milk almost instantly.
2. The cream had *curdled* in the heat.
3. The sauce kept *curdling* no matter how slowly she stirred.

---

### curry

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkɜːri/ &middot; *KUR-ee*
**Русский:** заискивать, подлизываться; добиваться расположения
**Forms:** *curry* &middot; *curried* &middot; *curried*
**Third person:** *curries* &middot; **-ing form:** *currying*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Nothing to do with the food. The *-y* becomes *-ie-*: *curries*, *curried*.

To try to win someone's favor by flattery, almost always in the phrase *curry favor*.

1. New hires *curry* favor with anyone who signs their reviews.
2. He *curried* favor with the committee for months.
3. She got the job without *currying* favor with a soul.

---

### curse

**Pronunciation:** /kɜːrs/ &middot; *KURS*
**Русский:** ругаться, выругаться; проклинать, проклясть
**Forms:** *curse* &middot; *cursed* &middot; *cursed*
**Third person:** *curses* &middot; **-ing form:** *cursing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (he cursed his luck), intransitive (she cursed under her breath)
**Contrast:** As a verb *cursed* is one syllable (KURST); as an old-fashioned adjective it can be two — KUR-sid ground.

To use angry, offensive words, or to call down harm on someone.

1. Drivers *curse* at each other all along that stretch of road.
2. He *cursed* his luck and started over.
3. She walked off *cursing* under her breath.

---

### cyberbully

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsaɪbərbʊli/ &middot; *SY-bur-buul-ee*
**Русский:** травить в интернете, затравить; преследовать онлайн
**Forms:** *cyberbully* &middot; *cyberbullied* &middot; *cyberbullied*
**Third person:** *cyberbullies* &middot; **-ing form:** *cyberbullying*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The *-y* becomes *-ie-*: *cyberbullies*, *cyberbullied*.

To harass or threaten someone online, especially again and again.

1. Kids who *cyberbully* rarely think of it as bullying.
2. She was *cyberbullied* for a year before anyone noticed.
3. The school suspended two students for *cyberbullying* a classmate.

---

### damn

**Pronunciation:** /dæm/ &middot; *DAM*
**Русский:** проклинать, проклясть; осуждать, осудить
**Forms:** *damn* &middot; *damned* &middot; *damned*
**Third person:** *damns* &middot; **-ing form:** *damning*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The *n* is silent: *damn* sounds exactly like *dam*, and *damning* is DAM-ing.

To condemn someone or something harshly, or in religion, to send to hell.

1. Critics *damn* the plan without offering another one.
2. The report *damned* the agency in plain language.
3. It was the most *damning* evidence in the file.

---

### dangle

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdæŋɡəl/ &middot; *DANG-guhl*
**Русский:** свисать, болтаться; болтать (ногами)
**Forms:** *dangle* &middot; *dangled* &middot; *dangled*
**Third person:** *dangles* &middot; **-ing form:** *dangling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (he dangled the keys), intransitive (the rope dangled)

To hang loosely and swing, or to hold something so that it hangs that way.

1. Wires *dangle* from the ceiling where the light used to be.
2. He *dangled* the keys just out of her reach.
3. She sat on the wall, *dangling* her feet over the edge.

---

### dare

**Pronunciation:** /dɛr/ &middot; *DAIR*
**Русский:** сметь, осмелиться; подначивать, вызывать на спор
**Forms:** *dare* &middot; *dared* &middot; *dared*
**Third person:** *dares* &middot; **-ing form:** *daring*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (his brother dared him to jump), intransitive (she didn't dare ask)
**Contrast:** In questions and negatives *dare* behaves like a modal and takes no *to*: *How dare you?*, *I dare not ask*. In ordinary American English, say *I don't dare*.

To be brave enough to do something, or to challenge someone to do it.

1. I wouldn't *dare* tell her the truth.
2. His brother *dared* him to jump off the wall.
3. Nobody has *dared* raise the subject since.

---

### dart

**Pronunciation:** /dɑːrt/ &middot; *DART*
**Русский:** метнуться, рвануться; бросать (взгляд)
**Forms:** *dart* &middot; *darted* &middot; *darted*
**Third person:** *darts* &middot; **-ing form:** *darting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (she darted a look at him), intransitive (a rabbit darted across the road)

To move suddenly and very quickly in one direction.

1. Lizards *dart* across the hot stones and freeze again.
2. A rabbit *darted* out in front of the car.
3. His eyes kept *darting* toward the door.

---

### daunt

**Pronunciation:** /dɔːnt/ &middot; *DAWNT*
**Русский:** пугать, испугать; обескураживать, обескуражить
**Forms:** *daunt* &middot; *daunted* &middot; *daunted*
**Third person:** *daunts* &middot; **-ing form:** *daunting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To make someone lose confidence by seeming too difficult or frightening.

1. Long reading lists *daunt* most first-year students.
2. The size of the repair bill *daunted* even her father.
3. Learning a new alphabet is *daunting* at first.

---

### daze

**Pronunciation:** /deɪz/ &middot; *DAYZ*
**Русский:** ошеломлять, ошеломить; оглушать, оглушить
**Forms:** *daze* &middot; *dazed* &middot; *dazed*
**Third person:** *dazes* &middot; **-ing form:** *dazing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Not the same as *dazzle*, which means to blind with brightness or to impress.

To stun someone so that they cannot think or react clearly.

1. A hit like that would *daze* anybody.
2. The news *dazed* her for a full day.
3. He wandered out of the wreck, *dazed* but unhurt.

---

### deaden

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɛdən/ &middot; *DED-uhn*
**Русский:** приглушать, приглушить; притуплять, притупить (боль)
**Forms:** *deaden* &middot; *deadened* &middot; *deadened*
**Third person:** *deadens* &middot; **-ing form:** *deadening*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To make a sound, a feeling or a pain weaker.

1. Thick curtains *deaden* the noise from the street.
2. The shot *deadened* the pain within a minute.
3. Years of paperwork had a *deadening* effect on him.

---

### deadlock

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɛdlɑːk/ &middot; *DED-lahk*
**Русский:** заходить в тупик, зайти в тупик
**Forms:** *deadlock* &middot; *deadlocked* &middot; *deadlocked*
**Third person:** *deadlocks* &middot; **-ing form:** *deadlocking*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (the dispute deadlocked the talks), intransitive (the jury deadlocked)

To reach a point where neither side can move and nothing can be agreed.

1. Talks *deadlock* every time pensions come up.
2. The jury *deadlocked* after four days.
3. Negotiations have been *deadlocked* since March.

---

### debase

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈbeɪs/ &middot; *dih-BAYSS*
**Русский:** унижать, унизить; обесценивать, обесценить
**Forms:** *debase* &middot; *debased* &middot; *debased*
**Third person:** *debases* &middot; **-ing form:** *debasing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To lower the value or the quality of something, or someone's dignity.

1. Constant exaggeration *debases* the words we need for real emergencies.
2. Rome *debased* its own coinage by mixing in cheap metal.
3. She felt *debased* by the whole process.

---

### debilitate

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈbɪləteɪt/ &middot; *dih-BIL-uh-tayt*
**Русский:** ослаблять, ослабить; изнурять
**Forms:** *debilitate* &middot; *debilitated* &middot; *debilitated*
**Third person:** *debilitates* &middot; **-ing form:** *debilitating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To make a person or a system seriously weak.

1. Long hospital stays *debilitate* even young patients.
2. The infection *debilitated* him for months.
3. She was *debilitated* by the treatment, not by the disease itself.

---

### debit

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɛbɪt/ &middot; *DEB-it*
**Русский:** списывать со счёта, дебетовать
**Forms:** *debit* &middot; *debited* &middot; *debited*
**Third person:** *debits* &middot; **-ing form:** *debiting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** One t: *debited*, *debiting* — the stress is on DEB-. The opposite of *credit*.

To take money out of a bank account.

1. The gym will *debit* your account on the first of every month.
2. They *debited* the full amount twice by mistake.
3. The fee has already been *debited*.

---

### debone

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈboʊn/ &middot; *dee-BOHN*
**Русский:** обваливать, удалять кости (из мяса, рыбы)
**Forms:** *debone* &middot; *deboned* &middot; *deboned*
**Third person:** *debones* &middot; **-ing form:** *deboning*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To take the bones out of meat or fish before cooking.

1. Ask the butcher to *debone* the chicken thighs.
2. He *deboned* the trout in about a minute.
3. The fillets come already *deboned*.

---

### debunk

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈbʌŋk/ &middot; *dih-BUNGK*
**Русский:** разоблачать, развенчивать (миф)
**Forms:** *debunk* &middot; *debunked* &middot; *debunked*
**Third person:** *debunks* &middot; **-ing form:** *debunking*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To show that a popular belief or claim is false.

1. Nutritionists *debunk* this diet myth every January.
2. The reporter *debunked* the story in one afternoon.
3. That theory has been *debunked* many times over.

---

### decaffeinate

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈkæfəneɪt/ &middot; *dee-KAF-uh-nayt*
**Русский:** удалять кофеин, декофеинизировать
**Forms:** *decaffeinate* &middot; *decaffeinated* &middot; *decaffeinated*
**Third person:** *decaffeinates* &middot; **-ing form:** *decaffeinating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To remove most of the caffeine from coffee or tea.

1. They *decaffeinate* the beans with water instead of chemicals.
2. The company *decaffeinated* its whole house blend.
3. I only drink *decaffeinated* coffee after six.

---

### decamp

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈkæmp/ &middot; *dih-KAMP*
**Русский:** внезапно уезжать, перебираться; сниматься с места
**Forms:** *decamp* &middot; *decamped* &middot; *decamped*
**Third person:** *decamps* &middot; **-ing form:** *decamping*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** You *decamp* **to** or **for** a new place.

To leave a place suddenly, usually for somewhere better.

1. Every August half the office *decamps* to the coast.
2. The band *decamped* to Nashville to record.
3. Fans were already *decamping* to the parking lot in the eighth inning.

---

### decant

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈkænt/ &middot; *dih-KANT*
**Русский:** переливать, сливать (жидкость), декантировать
**Forms:** *decant* &middot; *decanted* &middot; *decanted*
**Third person:** *decants* &middot; **-ing form:** *decanting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To pour liquid slowly from one container into another, leaving the sediment behind.

1. Let the wine breathe, or *decant* it into a pitcher.
2. She *decanted* the soup into three jars.
3. The oil is *decanted* into smaller bottles for the table.

---

### decapitate

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈkæpəteɪt/ &middot; *dih-KAP-uh-tayt*
**Русский:** обезглавливать, обезглавить
**Forms:** *decapitate* &middot; *decapitated* &middot; *decapitated*
**Third person:** *decapitates* &middot; **-ing form:** *decapitating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To cut off the head of a person or an animal.

1. Vandals *decapitate* the garden statues almost every spring.
2. The hailstorm *decapitated* every sunflower in the row.
3. The bronze figure had been *decapitated* sometime in the 1800s.

---

### decarbonize

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈkɑːrbənaɪz/ &middot; *dee-KAHR-buh-nyz*
**Русский:** декарбонизировать, снижать выбросы углерода
**Forms:** *decarbonize* &middot; *decarbonized* &middot; *decarbonized*
**Third person:** *decarbonizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *decarbonizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*decarbonize* the grid), intransitive (heavy industry must *decarbonize*)
**Contrast:** American *decarbonize*; British writing uses *decarbonise*.

To cut the carbon emissions of an industry, a building, or a country to nearly zero.

1. The plan is to *decarbonize* the power grid by 2035.
2. Steelmakers *decarbonized* faster than anyone expected.
3. Shipping is *decarbonizing* more slowly than aviation.

---

### decelerate

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈsɛləreɪt/ &middot; *dee-SEL-uh-rayt*
**Русский:** замедлять, замедляться, снижать скорость
**Forms:** *decelerate* &middot; *decelerated* &middot; *decelerated*
**Third person:** *decelerates* &middot; **-ing form:** *decelerating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*decelerate* the car), intransitive (growth *decelerated*)
**Contrast:** The opposite of *accelerate*.

To slow down, or to make something slow down.

1. Loaded trucks *decelerate* slowly on wet pavement.
2. Inflation *decelerated* for the fourth month in a row.
3. The train was *decelerating* as it entered the tunnel.

---

### decentralize

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈsɛntrəlaɪz/ &middot; *dee-SEN-truh-lyz*
**Русский:** децентрализовать, передавать полномочия на места
**Forms:** *decentralize* &middot; *decentralized* &middot; *decentralized*
**Third person:** *decentralizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *decentralizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*decentralize* the agency), intransitive (the network *decentralized*)
**Contrast:** American *decentralize*; British *decentralise*.

To move power or operations away from one central place to many smaller ones.

1. The reform would *decentralize* hiring to individual schools.
2. The company *decentralized* its support teams last year.
3. Health care has been steadily *decentralized* since the 1990s.

---

### decertify

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈsɜːrtəfaɪ/ &middot; *dee-SUR-tuh-fy*
**Русский:** лишать сертификата, отзывать аккредитацию
**Forms:** *decertify* &middot; *decertified* &middot; *decertified*
**Third person:** *decertifies* &middot; **-ing form:** *decertifying*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Consonant + *-y* changes: *decertifies*, *decertified*, but *decertifying* keeps the y.

To officially take away a license, an accreditation, or a union's recognition.

1. The state can *decertify* an officer who lies under oath.
2. Regulators *decertified* two nursing homes in the county.
3. The union was *decertified* after a very close vote.

---

### decimate

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɛsəmeɪt/ &middot; *DES-uh-mayt*
**Русский:** уничтожать, истреблять; резко сокращать
**Forms:** *decimate* &middot; *decimated* &middot; *decimated*
**Third person:** *decimates* &middot; **-ing form:** *decimating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To destroy a large part of a group or a population.

1. Two bad winters can *decimate* a bee colony.
2. The layoffs *decimated* the newsroom.
3. Whole villages had been *decimated* by the epidemic.

---

### deck

**Pronunciation:** /dɛk/ &middot; *DEK*
**Русский:** сбивать с ног (ударом), врезать
**Forms:** *deck* &middot; *decked* &middot; *decked*
**Third person:** *decks* &middot; **-ing form:** *decking*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Informal. Not the same as the phrasal *deck out*, which means to decorate.

To knock someone to the ground with a punch.

1. One more word and I'll *deck* him.
2. He *decked* the guy in front of the whole bar.
3. The catcher got *decked* at home plate.

---

### declassify

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈklæsəfaɪ/ &middot; *dee-KLAS-uh-fy*
**Русский:** рассекречивать, рассекретить
**Forms:** *declassify* &middot; *declassified* &middot; *declassified*
**Third person:** *declassifies* &middot; **-ing form:** *declassifying*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Consonant + *-y* changes: *declassifies*, *declassified*, but *declassifying*.

To remove the secret status from official documents so that the public can read them.

1. Agencies *declassify* thousands of pages every year.
2. The White House *declassified* the memo on a Friday.
3. The cables were *declassified* thirty years later.

---

### declaw

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈklɔː/ &middot; *dee-KLAW*
**Русский:** удалять когти; лишать силы, обезвреживать
**Forms:** *declaw* &middot; *declawed* &middot; *declawed*
**Third person:** *declaws* &middot; **-ing form:** *declawing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To remove an animal's claws, or to take the force out of something.

1. Most American vets now refuse to *declaw* cats.
2. Amendments *declawed* the bill before the final vote.
3. The agency has been *declawed* by three rounds of budget cuts.

---

### decolonize

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈkɑːlənaɪz/ &middot; *dee-KAH-luh-nyz*
**Русский:** деколонизировать, освобождать от колониальной зависимости
**Forms:** *decolonize* &middot; *decolonized* &middot; *decolonized*
**Third person:** *decolonizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *decolonizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To free a country from colonial rule, or to strip colonial assumptions out of a subject.

1. Britain began to *decolonize* Africa after the war.
2. Ghana was *decolonized* in 1957.
3. Several universities are *decolonizing* their reading lists.

---

### decommission

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdiːkəˈmɪʃən/ &middot; *dee-kuh-MISH-uhn*
**Русский:** выводить из эксплуатации, списывать
**Forms:** *decommission* &middot; *decommissioned* &middot; *decommissioned*
**Third person:** *decommissions* &middot; **-ing form:** *decommissioning*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To take a ship, a power plant, or a piece of equipment permanently out of service.

1. The Navy will *decommission* the carrier next spring.
2. They *decommissioned* the reactor in 2019.
3. The rig has been *decommissioned* and towed away.

---

### decompress

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdiːkəmˈprɛs/ &middot; *dee-kuhm-PRES*
**Русский:** распаковывать (файл); расслабляться, приходить в себя
**Forms:** *decompress* &middot; *decompressed* &middot; *decompressed*
**Third person:** *decompresses* &middot; **-ing form:** *decompressing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*decompress* the file), intransitive (I need to *decompress*)
**Contrast:** Third person adds -es after -ss: *decompresses*.

To open up a compressed file, or to unwind after a stressful stretch.

1. The app will *decompress* the archive automatically.
2. I *decompressed* for a week after the trial ended.
3. He's still *decompressing* from the move.

---

### deconstruct

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdiːkənˈstrʌkt/ &middot; *dee-kuhn-STRUKT*
**Русский:** деконструировать, разбирать по частям, анализировать
**Forms:** *deconstruct* &middot; *deconstructed* &middot; *deconstructed*
**Third person:** *deconstructs* &middot; **-ing form:** *deconstructing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To break an idea, a text, or a dish down into its parts in order to examine them.

1. Critics love to *deconstruct* his early films.
2. The coach *deconstructed* every play on video.
3. The menu features a *deconstructed* apple pie.

---

### decontaminate

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdiːkənˈtæməneɪt/ &middot; *dee-kuhn-TAM-uh-nayt*
**Русский:** обеззараживать, дезактивировать
**Forms:** *decontaminate* &middot; *decontaminated* &middot; *decontaminated*
**Third person:** *decontaminates* &middot; **-ing form:** *decontaminating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To remove dangerous chemicals, radiation, or germs from a place or a person.

1. Crews *decontaminate* the suits after every shift.
2. They *decontaminated* the soil around the old plant.
3. The whole ward had to be *decontaminated*.

---

### decouple

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈkʌpəl/ &middot; *dee-KUP-uhl*
**Русский:** разъединять, отделять; разрывать связь
**Forms:** *decouple* &middot; *decoupled* &middot; *decoupled*
**Third person:** *decouples* &middot; **-ing form:** *decoupling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*decouple* pay from hours), intransitive (the two markets *decoupled*)
**Contrast:** You *decouple* one thing **from** another.

To separate two things that used to move or work together.

1. The proposal would *decouple* health insurance from employment.
2. The two markets *decoupled* for most of the year.
3. Emissions are slowly *decoupling* from economic growth.

---

### decree

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈkriː/ &middot; *dih-KREE*
**Русский:** постановлять, издавать указ
**Forms:** *decree* &middot; *decreed* &middot; *decreed*
**Third person:** *decrees* &middot; **-ing form:** *decreeing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Both e's stay: *decreed*, *decreeing*. Compare *agree — agreed — agreeing*.

To order something officially, by the authority of a ruler or a court.

1. A president cannot simply *decree* a new tax.
2. The king *decreed* a day of mourning.
3. The judge has *decreed* that the records stay sealed.

---

### decriminalize

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈkrɪmənəlaɪz/ &middot; *dee-KRIM-uh-nuh-lyz*
**Русский:** декриминализировать, отменять уголовную ответственность
**Forms:** *decriminalize* &middot; *decriminalized* &middot; *decriminalized*
**Third person:** *decriminalizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *decriminalizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** American *decriminalize*; British *decriminalise*.

To change the law so that something is no longer a crime.

1. Several states have voted to *decriminalize* small amounts.
2. Portugal *decriminalized* drug possession in 2001.
3. Jaywalking has been *decriminalized* in California.

---

### decry

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈkraɪ/ &middot; *dih-KRY*
**Русский:** осуждать, резко критиковать
**Forms:** *decry* &middot; *decried* &middot; *decried*
**Third person:** *decries* &middot; **-ing form:** *decrying*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The *-y* follows a consonant: *decries*, *decried*, but *decrying* keeps the y.

To condemn something publicly and strongly.

1. Editorials *decry* the ruling almost daily.
2. Both parties *decried* the violence.
3. The mayor has been *decrying* the cuts all week.

---

### deed

**Pronunciation:** /diːd/ &middot; *DEED*
**Русский:** передавать по дарственной, оформлять передачу собственности
**Forms:** *deed* &middot; *deeded* &middot; *deeded*
**Third person:** *deeds* &middot; **-ing form:** *deeding*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** You *deed* property **to** someone.

To transfer property to someone by a legal document.

1. Her parents plan to *deed* the farm to the county.
2. He *deeded* the house to his daughter in 1998.
3. The land was *deeded* to the city for a park.

---

### deescalate

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈɛskəleɪt/ &middot; *dee-ES-kuh-layt*
**Русский:** снижать напряжённость, деэскалировать
**Forms:** *deescalate* &middot; *deescalated* &middot; *deescalated*
**Third person:** *deescalates* &middot; **-ing form:** *deescalating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*deescalate* the situation), intransitive (the standoff *deescalated*)
**Contrast:** Usually written with a hyphen: *de-escalate*, *de-escalated*. The opposite of *escalate*.

To make a conflict or a tense situation calmer and less dangerous.

1. Officers are trained to *deescalate* before they draw a weapon.
2. The talks *deescalated* the crisis for a few months.
3. Tensions have been *deescalating* since the ceasefire.

---

### defame

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈfeɪm/ &middot; *dih-FAYM*
**Русский:** клеветать, порочить, очернять
**Forms:** *defame* &middot; *defamed* &middot; *defamed*
**Third person:** *defames* &middot; **-ing form:** *defaming*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To damage someone's reputation by saying or printing untrue things about them.

1. You cannot *defame* a public figure without proving malice.
2. The paper *defamed* him and paid for it in court.
3. She claims she was *defamed* on the podcast.

---

### defang

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈfæŋ/ &middot; *dee-FANG*
**Русский:** обезвреживать, лишать силы
**Forms:** *defang* &middot; *defanged* &middot; *defanged*
**Third person:** *defangs* &middot; **-ing form:** *defanging*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To take away something's power to do harm.

1. Lobbyists worked all spring to *defang* the regulation.
2. One amendment *defanged* the whole bill.
3. The committee has been *defanged* by the new rules.

---

### default

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈfɔːlt/ &middot; *dih-FAWLT*
**Русский:** не выполнять обязательства, объявлять дефолт; выбираться по умолчанию
**Forms:** *default* &middot; *defaulted* &middot; *defaulted*
**Third person:** *defaults* &middot; **-ing form:** *defaulting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** You *default* **on** a loan; a setting *defaults* **to** a value.

To fail to pay back a loan, or to fall back automatically on a standard option.

1. If the country *defaults*, borrowing gets much more expensive.
2. He *defaulted* on two student loans.
3. The form kept *defaulting* to last year's address.

---

### defecate

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɛfɪkeɪt/ &middot; *DEF-i-kayt*
**Русский:** испражняться, опорожнять кишечник
**Forms:** *defecate* &middot; *defecated* &middot; *defecated*
**Third person:** *defecates* &middot; **-ing form:** *defecating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** The clinical word. In conversation Americans say *poop* or *go to the bathroom*.

To pass solid waste out of the body.

1. Most newborns *defecate* several times a day.
2. The dog *defecated* on the neighbor's lawn.
3. A patient who has not *defecated* in three days should call the office.

---

### defile

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈfaɪl/ &middot; *dih-FYL*
**Русский:** осквернять, осквернить; загрязнять, марать
**Forms:** *defile* &middot; *defiled* &middot; *defiled*
**Third person:** *defiles* &middot; **-ing form:** *defiling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To spoil something clean or sacred by treating it with disrespect.

1. Runoff from the mine *defiles* the creek every spring.
2. Someone *defiled* the memorial with spray paint.
3. The temple had been *defiled* and then abandoned.

---

### deflate

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈfleɪt/ &middot; *dih-FLAYT*
**Русский:** сдувать, спускать воздух; сдуваться; сбивать спесь
**Forms:** *deflate* &middot; *deflated* &middot; *deflated*
**Third person:** *deflates* &middot; **-ing form:** *deflating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*deflate* the tire), intransitive (the raft *deflated*)

To let the air out of something, or to make someone lose their confidence.

1. *Deflate* the raft before you roll it up.
2. One comment from her *deflated* him completely.
3. The balloon was *deflating* faster than we could patch it.

---

### defoliate

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈfoʊlieɪt/ &middot; *dee-FOH-lee-ayt*
**Русский:** лишать листвы, уничтожать растительность
**Forms:** *defoliate* &middot; *defoliated* &middot; *defoliated*
**Third person:** *defoliates* &middot; **-ing form:** *defoliating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To strip the leaves off trees and plants, usually with chemicals or insects.

1. Gypsy moths can *defoliate* an entire hillside.
2. The military *defoliated* thousands of square miles of jungle.
3. The orchard was almost completely *defoliated* by June.

---

### deforest

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈfɔːrəst/ &middot; *dee-FOR-uhst*
**Русский:** вырубать леса, обезлесивать
**Forms:** *deforest* &middot; *deforested* &middot; *deforested*
**Third person:** *deforests* &middot; **-ing form:** *deforesting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To cut down all the trees over a large area.

1. Cattle ranching *deforests* more land than logging does.
2. Settlers *deforested* the valley within a generation.
3. Much of the basin has already been *deforested*.

---

### deform

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈfɔːrm/ &middot; *dih-FORM*
**Русский:** деформировать, искажать форму; деформироваться
**Forms:** *deform* &middot; *deformed* &middot; *deformed*
**Third person:** *deforms* &middot; **-ing form:** *deforming*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (heat *deforms* the plastic), intransitive (the frame *deformed*)

To change the shape of something so that it is bent or spoiled.

1. Heat will *deform* the plastic housing.
2. The impact *deformed* the front wheel.
3. The beams were *deforming* under the weight of the snow.

---

### defray

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈfreɪ/ &middot; *dih-FRAY*
**Русский:** покрывать расходы, оплачивать часть затрат
**Forms:** *defray* &middot; *defrayed* &middot; *defrayed*
**Third person:** *defrays* &middot; **-ing form:** *defraying*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The *-y* follows a vowel, so it stays: *defrays*, *defrayed*. Compare *carry — carried*.

To pay part or all of someone's costs.

1. The grant helps *defray* travel expenses.
2. A bake sale *defrayed* most of the cost of the trip.
3. Tuition is partly *defrayed* by the scholarship.

---

### defrock

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈfrɑːk/ &middot; *dee-FRAHK*
**Русский:** лишать духовного сана
**Forms:** *defrock* &middot; *defrocked* &middot; *defrocked*
**Third person:** *defrocks* &middot; **-ing form:** *defrocking*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To remove a priest or minister from the clergy as a punishment.

1. The church can *defrock* a priest without a criminal trial.
2. The diocese *defrocked* him in 2004.
3. Two *defrocked* priests testified at the hearing.

---

### defund

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈfʌnd/ &middot; *dee-FUND*
**Русский:** лишать финансирования, прекращать финансирование
**Forms:** *defund* &middot; *defunded* &middot; *defunded*
**Third person:** *defunds* &middot; **-ing form:** *defunding*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To stop providing money to an organization or a program.

1. Congress threatened to *defund* the agency.
2. The state *defunded* the program in 2011.
3. Arts education has been quietly *defunded* for years.

---

### degenerate

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈdʒɛnəreɪt/ &middot; *dih-JEN-uh-rayt*
**Русский:** вырождаться, деградировать; ухудшаться
**Forms:** *degenerate* &middot; *degenerated* &middot; *degenerated*
**Third person:** *degenerates* &middot; **-ing form:** *degenerating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** The verb ends in -rayt; the adjective and noun end in a schwa: dih-JEN-uh-ruht. Usually *degenerate into* something.

To get steadily worse, or to sink into a lower and uglier state.

1. These meetings always *degenerate* into shouting.
2. The debate *degenerated* within ten minutes.
3. His eyesight has been *degenerating* since the surgery.

---

### deglaze

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈɡleɪz/ &middot; *dee-GLAYZ*
**Русский:** деглазировать сковороду, разводить пригар жидкостью
**Forms:** *deglaze* &middot; *deglazed* &middot; *deglazed*
**Third person:** *deglazes* &middot; **-ing form:** *deglazing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** You *deglaze* a pan **with** wine, stock, or water.

To pour liquid into a hot pan to loosen the browned bits and make a sauce.

1. *Deglaze* the pan with a splash of white wine.
2. She *deglazed* the skillet with stock and poured it over the meat.
3. *Deglazing* takes about thirty seconds.

---

### dehumanize

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈhjuːmənaɪz/ &middot; *dee-HYOO-muh-nyz*
**Русский:** обесчеловечивать, лишать человеческого достоинства
**Forms:** *dehumanize* &middot; *dehumanized* &middot; *dehumanized*
**Third person:** *dehumanizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *dehumanizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** American *dehumanize*; British *dehumanise*.

To treat or portray people as though they were not human beings.

1. Propaganda works by *dehumanizing* the other side.
2. The system *dehumanized* everyone who passed through it.
3. Prisoners described being *dehumanized* by the daily routine.

---

### dehumidify

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdiːhjuːˈmɪdəfaɪ/ &middot; *dee-hyoo-MID-uh-fy*
**Русский:** осушать воздух, снижать влажность
**Forms:** *dehumidify* &middot; *dehumidified* &middot; *dehumidified*
**Third person:** *dehumidifies* &middot; **-ing form:** *dehumidifying*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Consonant + *-y* changes: *dehumidifies*, *dehumidified*, but *dehumidifying*.

To remove moisture from the air in a room or a building.

1. A small unit will *dehumidify* the whole basement.
2. We *dehumidified* the crawl space before laying the floor.
3. The machine has been *dehumidifying* for two days.

---

### deice

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈaɪs/ &middot; *dee-EYSS*
**Русский:** очищать ото льда, обрабатывать противообледенительным составом
**Forms:** *deice* &middot; *deiced* &middot; *deiced*
**Third person:** *deices* &middot; **-ing form:** *deicing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Often written with a hyphen: *de-ice*, *de-iced*, *de-icing*.

To remove ice from a surface, especially an aircraft or a windshield.

1. Crews *deice* the wings right before takeoff.
2. They *deiced* the plane twice and we still missed our slot.
3. The runway is being *deiced* now.

---

### deify

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdiːəfaɪ/ &middot; *DEE-uh-fy*
**Русский:** обожествлять, боготворить
**Forms:** *deify* &middot; *deified* &middot; *deified*
**Third person:** *deifies* &middot; **-ing form:** *deifying*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Consonant + *-y* changes: *deifies*, *deified*, but *deifying*.

To treat a person or a thing as if it were a god.

1. Fans *deify* a coach after one good season.
2. Rome *deified* several of its emperors.
3. He has been *deified* by an industry that once ignored him.

---

### deign

**Pronunciation:** /deɪn/ &middot; *DAYN*
**Русский:** снисходить, соизволить
**Forms:** *deign* &middot; *deigned* &middot; *deigned*
**Third person:** *deigns* &middot; **-ing form:** *deigning*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** The *g* is silent: DAYN, like *Dane*. Almost always *deign to do* something.

To do something you consider beneath you, as though it were a favor.

1. He won't even *deign* to answer the email.
2. She finally *deigned* to look up from her phone.
3. They have never *deigned* to explain the decision.

---

### delegitimize

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdiːlɪˈdʒɪtəmaɪz/ &middot; *dee-lih-JIT-uh-myz*
**Русский:** лишать легитимности, подрывать законность
**Forms:** *delegitimize* &middot; *delegitimized* &middot; *delegitimized*
**Third person:** *delegitimizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *delegitimizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To make an institution, a leader, or a result seem not legitimate.

1. Endless lawsuits *delegitimize* the whole process.
2. The broadcast *delegitimized* the election in the eyes of millions.
3. The court has been *delegitimized* by the fight over appointments.

---

### delineate

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈlɪnieɪt/ &middot; *dih-LIN-ee-ayt*
**Русский:** очерчивать, обозначать границы; чётко описывать
**Forms:** *delineate* &middot; *delineated* &middot; *delineated*
**Third person:** *delineates* &middot; **-ing form:** *delineating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To describe or mark out something clearly and in detail.

1. The contract should *delineate* each party's duties.
2. The treaty *delineated* the border river by river.
3. Responsibilities are carefully *delineated* in the handbook.

---

### delist

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈlɪst/ &middot; *dee-LIST*
**Русский:** исключать из списка, снимать с биржевых торгов
**Forms:** *delist* &middot; *delisted* &middot; *delisted*
**Third person:** *delists* &middot; **-ing form:** *delisting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To remove a company from a stock exchange, or an item from an official list.

1. The exchange can *delist* a stock that trades under a dollar.
2. Nasdaq *delisted* the company in March.
3. The species was *delisted* once the population recovered.

---

### delude

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈluːd/ &middot; *dih-LOOD*
**Русский:** вводить в заблуждение, обманывать
**Forms:** *delude* &middot; *deluded* &middot; *deluded*
**Third person:** *deludes* &middot; **-ing form:** *deluding*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Very often reflexive: *delude yourself*.

To make someone believe something that is not true.

1. Don't *delude* yourself about the numbers.
2. He *deluded* investors for six years.
3. They were *deluded* into thinking the job was safe.

---

### demagnetize

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈmæɡnətaɪz/ &middot; *dee-MAG-nuh-tyz*
**Русский:** размагничивать, размагнитить
**Forms:** *demagnetize* &middot; *demagnetized* &middot; *demagnetized*
**Third person:** *demagnetizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *demagnetizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To take the magnetism out of something.

1. A phone case can *demagnetize* a hotel key.
2. The scanner *demagnetized* my credit card.
3. The strip has been *demagnetized*, so you'll need a new card.

---

### demarcate

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈmɑːrkeɪt/ &middot; *dih-MAHR-kayt*
**Русский:** разграничивать, размечать границы
**Forms:** *demarcate* &middot; *demarcated* &middot; *demarcated*
**Third person:** *demarcates* &middot; **-ing form:** *demarcating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To mark or fix the boundary between two things or areas.

1. Orange cones *demarcate* the work zone.
2. The treaty *demarcated* the two territories in 1904.
3. The two roles are not clearly *demarcated*.

---

### demilitarize

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈmɪlətəraɪz/ &middot; *dee-MIL-uh-tuh-ryz*
**Русский:** демилитаризовать, выводить войска и вооружение
**Forms:** *demilitarize* &middot; *demilitarized* &middot; *demilitarized*
**Third person:** *demilitarizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *demilitarizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** American *demilitarize*; British *demilitarise*.

To remove armed forces and weapons from an area.

1. Both sides agreed to *demilitarize* the border.
2. The islands were *demilitarized* after the war.
3. They are *demilitarizing* the zone one checkpoint at a time.

---

### demo

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɛmoʊ/ &middot; *DEM-oh*
**Русский:** демонстрировать, показывать в работе (продукт)
**Forms:** *demo* &middot; *demoed* &middot; *demoed*
**Third person:** *demos* &middot; **-ing form:** *demoing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The o stays in every form: *demos*, *demoed*, *demoing*.

To show how a product works by using it in front of people.

1. She'll *demo* the new release at the conference.
2. They *demoed* the app for us over lunch.
3. We've been *demoing* the machine in stores all month.

---

### demobilize

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈmoʊbəlaɪz/ &middot; *dee-MOH-buh-lyz*
**Русский:** демобилизовать, распускать войска
**Forms:** *demobilize* &middot; *demobilized* &middot; *demobilized*
**Third person:** *demobilizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *demobilizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*demobilize* the troops), intransitive (the militia *demobilized*)

To send soldiers home and take an army out of active service.

1. The agreement requires both armies to *demobilize*.
2. Washington *demobilized* millions of men in 1945.
3. Fighters are being *demobilized* under UN supervision.

---

### democratize

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈmɑːkrətaɪz/ &middot; *dih-MAH-kruh-tyz*
**Русский:** демократизировать; делать общедоступным
**Forms:** *democratize* &middot; *democratized* &middot; *democratized*
**Third person:** *democratizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *democratizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** American *democratize*; British *democratise*.

To give far more people access to something, or to make a country democratic.

1. Cheap cameras *democratize* filmmaking.
2. The reforms *democratized* the party's nominating process.
3. Access to data has been *democratized* by these tools.

---

### demonetize

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈmɑːnətaɪz/ &middot; *dee-MAH-nuh-tyz*
**Русский:** демонетизировать, лишать возможности зарабатывать; изымать из обращения
**Forms:** *demonetize* &middot; *demonetized* &middot; *demonetized*
**Third person:** *demonetizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *demonetizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To stop a video or a channel from earning money, or to take a banknote out of circulation.

1. Platforms *demonetize* videos that mention certain topics.
2. India *demonetized* its two largest banknotes in 2016.
3. Her whole channel was *demonetized* overnight.

---

### demonize

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdiːmənaɪz/ &middot; *DEE-muh-nyz*
