**Русский:** демонизировать, выставлять злодеем
**Forms:** *demonize* &middot; *demonized* &middot; *demonized*
**Third person:** *demonizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *demonizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** American *demonize*; British *demonise*.

To portray a person or a group as evil.

1. Campaigns *demonize* whoever is easiest to blame.
2. The coverage *demonized* an entire neighborhood.
3. Immigrants have been *demonized* in every recession.

---

### demoralize

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈmɔːrəlaɪz/ &middot; *dih-MOR-uh-lyz*
**Русский:** деморализовать, лишать боевого духа
**Forms:** *demoralize* &middot; *demoralized* &middot; *demoralized*
**Third person:** *demoralizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *demoralizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** American *demoralize*; British *demoralise*.

To take away someone's confidence, hope, or willingness to keep going.

1. Long shifts and no raise *demoralize* even good nurses.
2. The loss *demoralized* the whole team.
3. The staff is completely *demoralized*.

---

### demote

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈmoʊt/ &middot; *dih-MOHT*
**Русский:** понижать в должности, разжаловать
**Forms:** *demote* &middot; *demoted* &middot; *demoted*
**Third person:** *demotes* &middot; **-ing form:** *demoting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The opposite of *promote*.

To move someone to a lower rank or position.

1. They may *demote* him rather than fire him.
2. The league *demoted* two officials after the review.
3. He was *demoted* to a desk job in March.

---

### demotivate

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈmoʊtəveɪt/ &middot; *dee-MOH-tuh-vayt*
**Русский:** демотивировать, лишать мотивации
**Forms:** *demotivate* &middot; *demotivated* &middot; *demotivated*
**Third person:** *demotivates* &middot; **-ing form:** *demotivating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To make someone lose interest in working or trying.

1. Nothing *demotivates* a team faster than a pointless meeting.
2. The new quota *demotivated* half the sales floor.
3. Students are *demotivated* by grading they don't understand.

---

### demur

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈmɜːr/ &middot; *dih-MUR*
**Русский:** возражать, выражать несогласие; уклоняться от ответа
**Forms:** *demur* &middot; *demurred* &middot; *demurred*
**Third person:** *demurs* &middot; **-ing form:** *demurring*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** The r doubles: *demurred*, *demurring*. Not the adjective *demure* (dih-MYOOR, 'modest and quiet').

To object politely, or to hesitate rather than agree.

1. Two board members *demur* whenever the topic comes up.
2. She *demurred* when reporters asked about the money.
3. He was still *demurring* an hour into the negotiation.

---

### demystify

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈmɪstəfaɪ/ &middot; *dee-MIS-tuh-fy*
**Русский:** демистифицировать, объяснять простыми словами
**Forms:** *demystify* &middot; *demystified* &middot; *demystified*
**Third person:** *demystifies* &middot; **-ing form:** *demystifying*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Consonant + *-y* changes: *demystifies*, *demystified*, but *demystifying*.

To make a difficult or mysterious subject easy to understand.

1. The book tries to *demystify* the tax code.
2. Her lecture *demystified* the whole process.
3. He spends his weekends *demystifying* statistics for beginners.

---

### denature

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈneɪtʃər/ &middot; *dee-NAY-chur*
**Русский:** денатурировать, изменять природные свойства
**Forms:** *denature* &middot; *denatured* &middot; *denatured*
**Third person:** *denatures* &middot; **-ing form:** *denaturing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (heat *denatures* the protein), intransitive (the enzyme *denatured*)

To change the natural structure of a protein, or to make alcohol unfit to drink.

1. Heat and acid both *denature* egg proteins.
2. The sample *denatured* at seventy degrees.
3. Industrial alcohol is *denatured* so that nobody will drink it.

---

### denigrate

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɛnɪɡreɪt/ &middot; *DEN-i-grayt*
**Русский:** принижать, очернять, умалять
**Forms:** *denigrate* &middot; *denigrated* &middot; *denigrated*
**Third person:** *denigrates* &middot; **-ing form:** *denigrating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To criticize someone or something unfairly in order to make them seem worthless.

1. He never *denigrates* his competitors in public.
2. The review *denigrated* the entire genre.
3. Her work was *denigrated* for years before anyone reconsidered it.

---

### denote

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈnoʊt/ &middot; *dih-NOHT*
**Русский:** обозначать, означать
**Forms:** *denote* &middot; *denoted* &middot; *denoted*
**Third person:** *denotes* &middot; **-ing form:** *denoting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** *Denote* is what a word or sign literally stands for; *connote* is what it suggests.

To be a sign of something, or to stand for it.

1. In this table an asterisk *denotes* an estimate.
2. The red line *denoted* the old border.
3. The suffix has always *denoted* something small.

---

### denude

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈnuːd/ &middot; *dih-NOOD*
**Русский:** оголять, обнажать, лишать покрова
**Forms:** *denude* &middot; *denuded* &middot; *denuded*
**Third person:** *denudes* &middot; **-ing form:** *denuding*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To strip a place bare of its covering, especially of plants or soil.

1. Overgrazing can *denude* a hillside in a single season.
2. Logging *denuded* the ridge above the town.
3. The slopes had been *denuded* long before the flood.

---

### depend

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈpɛnd/ &middot; *dih-PEND*
**Русский:** зависеть; полагаться, положиться
**Forms:** *depend* &middot; *depended* &middot; *depended*
**Third person:** *depends* &middot; **-ing form:** *depending*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** Always *depend* **on** (or *upon*) something — never 'depend of'.

To be decided by something else, or to rely on someone for support.

1. How long it takes *depends* on the weather.
2. The whole town *depended* on one factory.
3. Thousands of families are *depending* on that check.

---

### deplane

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈpleɪn/ &middot; *dee-PLAYN*
**Русский:** выходить из самолёта, высаживаться
**Forms:** *deplane* &middot; *deplaned* &middot; *deplaned*
**Third person:** *deplanes* &middot; **-ing form:** *deplaning*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** Airline English. In conversation Americans say *get off the plane*.

To get off an airplane.

1. Passengers in rows twenty and up may *deplane* now.
2. We *deplaned* on the tarmac in the rain.
3. They were still *deplaning* when our flight started boarding.

---

### deplore

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈplɔːr/ &middot; *dih-PLOR*
**Русский:** осуждать, порицать; сожалеть о чём-л.
**Forms:** *deplore* &middot; *deplored* &middot; *deplored*
**Third person:** *deplores* &middot; **-ing form:** *deploring*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To say strongly and publicly that you think something is very wrong.

1. We *deplore* the use of force against protesters.
2. The statement *deplored* the attack but named no one.
3. Such language has been *deplored* by every previous chair.

---

### depopulate

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈpɑːpjəleɪt/ &middot; *dee-PAH-pyuh-layt*
**Русский:** обезлюживать, резко сокращать население
**Forms:** *depopulate* &middot; *depopulated* &middot; *depopulated*
**Third person:** *depopulates* &middot; **-ing form:** *depopulating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To greatly reduce the number of people living in a place.

1. Factory closures can *depopulate* a small town in a decade.
2. War and famine *depopulated* the province.
3. Whole counties have been *depopulated* since the 1980s.

---

### depose

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈpoʊz/ &middot; *dih-POHZ*
**Русский:** свергать, смещать; снимать показания под присягой
**Forms:** *depose* &middot; *deposed* &middot; *deposed*
**Third person:** *deposes* &middot; **-ing form:** *deposing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To remove a ruler from power, or to question a witness under oath before a trial.

1. Generals rarely *depose* a leader without foreign help.
2. The army *deposed* the president in 1973.
3. Both executives have already been *deposed* by the plaintiffs' lawyers.

---

### deprecate

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɛprəkeɪt/ &middot; *DEP-ruh-kayt*
**Русский:** не одобрять, осуждать; объявлять устаревшим (в IT)
**Forms:** *deprecate* &middot; *deprecated* &middot; *deprecated*
**Third person:** *deprecates* &middot; **-ing form:** *deprecating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Not *depreciate*, which is about losing value. Note *self-deprecating*, 'making fun of oneself'.

To express disapproval of something, or to mark a software feature as outdated.

1. Most style guides *deprecate* this construction.
2. The team *deprecated* the old API last spring.
3. That function has been *deprecated* — use the new one.

---

### depreciate

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈpriːʃieɪt/ &middot; *dih-PREE-shee-ayt*
**Русский:** обесцениваться, дешеветь; амортизировать
**Forms:** *depreciate* &middot; *depreciated* &middot; *depreciated*
**Third person:** *depreciates* &middot; **-ing form:** *depreciating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*depreciate* the equipment), intransitive (a new car *depreciates*)
**Contrast:** *Depreciate* is about value; *deprecate* is about disapproval.

To fall in value over time, or to write down the value of an asset.

1. A new car starts to *depreciate* the moment you drive it off the lot.
2. The peso *depreciated* sharply against the dollar.
3. The equipment is being *depreciated* over five years.

---

### depressurize

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈprɛʃəraɪz/ &middot; *dee-PRESH-uh-ryz*
**Русский:** разгерметизировать, сбрасывать давление
**Forms:** *depressurize* &middot; *depressurized* &middot; *depressurized*
**Third person:** *depressurizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *depressurizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*depressurize* the tank), intransitive (the cabin *depressurized*)

To release the pressure inside a sealed container or an aircraft cabin.

1. Technicians *depressurize* the tank before opening it.
2. The cabin *depressurized* at thirty thousand feet.
3. The chamber is slowly *depressurizing*.

---

### deprioritize

**Pronunciation:** /diːpraɪˈɔːrətaɪz/ &middot; *dee-pry-OR-uh-tyz*
**Русский:** понижать приоритет, отодвигать на потом
**Forms:** *deprioritize* &middot; *deprioritized* &middot; *deprioritized*
**Third person:** *deprioritizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *deprioritizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To treat something as less important than it was before.

1. Let's *deprioritize* the redesign until after launch.
2. Management *deprioritized* the whole feature.
3. Maintenance has been *deprioritized* for three budget cycles.

---

### deprogram

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈproʊɡræm/ &middot; *dee-PROH-gram*
**Русский:** перевоспитывать, освобождать от навязанных убеждений
**Forms:** *deprogram* &middot; *deprogrammed* &middot; *deprogrammed*
**Third person:** *deprograms* &middot; **-ing form:** *deprogramming*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The m doubles: *deprogrammed*, *deprogramming* — like *program — programmed*.

To free someone from beliefs that a cult or a movement pushed on them.

1. Families sometimes hire specialists to *deprogram* a relative.
2. It took two years to *deprogram* him.
3. She writes about being *deprogrammed* in the 1980s.

---

### deputize

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɛpjətaɪz/ &middot; *DEP-yuh-tyz*
**Русский:** назначать заместителем, наделять полномочиями
**Forms:** *deputize* &middot; *deputized* &middot; *deputized*
**Third person:** *deputizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *deputizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (the sheriff *deputized* them), intransitive (he *deputized* for the chair)
**Contrast:** American *deputize*; British *deputise*.

To give someone official power to act for you, especially as a deputy.

1. A sheriff can *deputize* ordinary citizens in an emergency.
2. He *deputized* two ranchers to help with the search.
3. The volunteers were *deputized* that same afternoon.

---

### deregulate

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈrɛɡjəleɪt/ &middot; *dee-REG-yuh-layt*
**Русский:** дерегулировать, снимать государственные ограничения
**Forms:** *deregulate* &middot; *deregulated* &middot; *deregulated*
**Third person:** *deregulates* &middot; **-ing form:** *deregulating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To remove government rules from an industry.

1. Both parties promised to *deregulate* small banks.
2. Congress *deregulated* the airlines in 1978.
3. Trucking has been *deregulated* for decades.

---

### deride

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈraɪd/ &middot; *dih-RYD*
**Русский:** высмеивать, осмеивать
**Forms:** *deride* &middot; *derided* &middot; *derided*
**Third person:** *derides* &middot; **-ing form:** *deriding*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Often *deride something* **as** something: *derided it as a stunt*.

To speak about someone or something with open contempt.

1. Critics *deride* the plan as a giveaway.
2. Reviewers *derided* the film when it came out.
3. The idea was *derided* for years and then quietly adopted.

---

### desalinate

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈsæləneɪt/ &middot; *dee-SAL-uh-nayt*
**Русский:** опреснять, опреснить
**Forms:** *desalinate* &middot; *desalinated* &middot; *desalinated*
**Third person:** *desalinates* &middot; **-ing form:** *desalinating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To remove the salt from seawater so that it can be used or drunk.

1. The plant can *desalinate* fifty million gallons a day.
2. Israel *desalinated* its way out of a water crisis.
3. Most of the city's water is now *desalinated*.

---

### desecrate

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɛsɪkreɪt/ &middot; *DES-i-krayt*
**Русский:** осквернять святыню, надругаться над
**Forms:** *desecrate* &middot; *desecrated* &middot; *desecrated*
**Third person:** *desecrates* &middot; **-ing form:** *desecrating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To damage or show great disrespect for a holy or honored place.

1. It is a felony to *desecrate* a grave.
2. Someone *desecrated* the synagogue over the weekend.
3. The chapel had been *desecrated* during the occupation.

---

### desegregate

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈsɛɡrəɡeɪt/ &middot; *dee-SEG-ruh-gayt*
**Русский:** десегрегировать, отменять расовое разделение
**Forms:** *desegregate* &middot; *desegregated* &middot; *desegregated*
**Third person:** *desegregates* &middot; **-ing form:** *desegregating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To end the legal separation of racial groups in schools or public places.

1. The ruling ordered the district to *desegregate*.
2. Little Rock *desegregated* Central High in 1957.
3. Many school systems were never fully *desegregated*.

---

### deselect

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdiːsəˈlɛkt/ &middot; *dee-suh-LEKT*
**Русский:** снимать выделение, убирать отметку
**Forms:** *deselect* &middot; *deselected* &middot; *deselected*
**Third person:** *deselects* &middot; **-ing form:** *deselecting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To remove something from a set of selected items, usually by clicking it again.

1. *Deselect* every box except the first one.
2. I *deselected* the photos I didn't want.
3. Nothing happens until all the layers are *deselected*.

---

### desensitize

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈsɛnsətaɪz/ &middot; *dee-SEN-suh-tyz*
**Русский:** снижать чувствительность; приучать, делать невосприимчивым
**Forms:** *desensitize* &middot; *desensitized* &middot; *desensitized*
**Third person:** *desensitizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *desensitizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** American *desensitize*; British *desensitise*. You *desensitize* someone **to** something.

To make someone or something less sensitive or less easily affected.

1. Violent images can *desensitize* young viewers.
2. Years of coverage *desensitized* the public.
3. Patients are gradually *desensitized* to the allergen.

---

### desiccate

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɛsɪkeɪt/ &middot; *DES-i-kayt*
**Русский:** высушивать, обезвоживать
**Forms:** *desiccate* &middot; *desiccated* &middot; *desiccated*
**Third person:** *desiccates* &middot; **-ing form:** *desiccating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** One s, then double c: *desiccate*, *desiccated*.

To dry something out completely.

1. A hot wind can *desiccate* a field in two days.
2. The drought *desiccated* the whole valley.
3. The recipe calls for *desiccated* coconut.

---

### designate

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɛzɪɡneɪt/ &middot; *DEZ-ig-nayt*
**Русский:** назначать, определять; обозначать
**Forms:** *designate* &middot; *designated* &middot; *designated*
**Third person:** *designates* &middot; **-ing form:** *designating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Here the *g* is pronounced: DEZ-ig-nayt — unlike *design*, dih-ZYN.

To officially choose something for a purpose, or to give it a name or a status.

1. The city will *designate* two blocks as a historic district.
2. Congress *designated* the area a national monument in 1996.
3. He has been *designated* as the acting director.

---

### desire

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈzaɪər/ &middot; *dih-ZY-ur*
**Русский:** желать, пожелать; хотеть
**Forms:** *desire* &middot; *desired* &middot; *desired*
**Third person:** *desires* &middot; **-ing form:** *desiring*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To want something strongly.

1. Most parents *desire* a better life for their children.
2. She had long *desired* a place of her own.
3. He got everything he had ever *desired* and still felt empty.

---

### despoil

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈspɔɪl/ &middot; *dih-SPOYL*
**Русский:** разорять, разорить; опустошать, грабить
**Forms:** *despoil* &middot; *despoiled* &middot; *despoiled*
**Third person:** *despoils* &middot; **-ing form:** *despoiling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To rob a place of its beauty or its valuable things.

1. Cheap development can *despoil* an entire coastline.
2. The invaders *despoiled* every village along the river.
3. The valley has been *despoiled* by decades of strip mining.

---

### destabilize

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈsteɪbəlaɪz/ &middot; *dee-STAY-buh-lyz*
**Русский:** дестабилизировать, расшатывать
**Forms:** *destabilize* &middot; *destabilized* &middot; *destabilized*
**Third person:** *destabilizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *destabilizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** American *destabilize*; British *destabilise*.

To make a country, a system or a situation less steady and more likely to fail.

1. A rumor like that can *destabilize* the whole market.
2. The coup *destabilized* the region for a decade.
3. The currency has been *destabilized* by months of protest.

---

### destigmatize

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈstɪɡmətaɪz/ &middot; *dee-STIG-muh-tyz*
**Русский:** снимать клеймо, избавлять от стигмы
**Forms:** *destigmatize* &middot; *destigmatized* &middot; *destigmatized*
**Third person:** *destigmatizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *destigmatizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To make people stop treating something as shameful.

1. The campaign is trying to *destigmatize* asking for help.
2. That one film *destigmatized* therapy for a lot of viewers.
3. Mental illness is slowly being *destigmatized* in the workplace.

---

### destine

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɛstɪn/ &middot; *DES-tin*
**Русский:** предназначать, предопределять
**Forms:** *destine* &middot; *destined* &middot; *destined*
**Third person:** *destines* &middot; **-ing form:** *destining*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Almost always passive: *be destined for* something, *be destined to* do something.

To set someone or something aside in advance for a particular purpose or fate.

1. Nothing in his childhood seemed to *destine* him for politics.
2. Her parents *destined* her for medicine before she could walk.
3. The plan was *destined* to fail from the very first meeting.

---

### detangle

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈtæŋɡəl/ &middot; *dee-TANG-guhl*
**Русский:** распутывать, распутать
**Forms:** *detangle* &middot; *detangled* &middot; *detangled*
**Third person:** *detangles* &middot; **-ing form:** *detangling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To separate hair, cords or threads that have become knotted together.

1. This spray helps *detangle* wet hair.
2. She *detangled* the headphone cords before packing them.
3. We spent an hour *detangling* the Christmas lights.

---

### detox

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈtɑːks/ &middot; *dee-TAHKS*
**Русский:** проходить детоксикацию; очищать организм
**Forms:** *detox* &middot; *detoxed* &middot; *detoxed*
**Third person:** *detoxes* &middot; **-ing form:** *detoxing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*detox* the body), intransitive (he *detoxed* in a clinic)

To clear alcohol, drugs or other harmful substances out of the body.

1. He checked into a clinic to *detox*.
2. She *detoxed* for a week before the surgery.
3. After three days of *detoxing*, he finally slept through the night.

---

### detoxify

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈtɑːksɪfaɪ/ &middot; *dee-TAHK-suh-fy*
**Русский:** выводить токсины, очищать от токсинов
**Forms:** *detoxify* &middot; *detoxified* &middot; *detoxified*
**Third person:** *detoxifies* &middot; **-ing form:** *detoxifying*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The y becomes ie before -s and -d: *detoxifies*, *detoxified*, but *detoxifying* keeps the y.

To remove poisonous substances from a body, a substance or a place.

1. The liver *detoxifies* the blood around the clock.
2. Crews *detoxified* the soil before the park was built.
3. The patient had to be *detoxified* over two days.

---

### detract

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈtrækt/ &middot; *dih-TRAKT*
**Русский:** умалять, принижать; портить впечатление
**Forms:** *detract* &middot; *detracted* &middot; *detracted*
**Third person:** *detracts* &middot; **-ing form:** *detracting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** Almost always with *from*: *detract from* the value, never 'detract the value'.

To make something seem less good or less valuable.

1. One weak chapter does not *detract* from the book.
2. The noise *detracted* from an otherwise perfect evening.
3. Nothing has *detracted* from her reputation as a teacher.

---

### devalue

**Pronunciation:** /diːˈvæljuː/ &middot; *dee-VAL-yoo*
**Русский:** обесценивать, обесценить; девальвировать
**Forms:** *devalue* &middot; *devalued* &middot; *devalued*
**Third person:** *devalues* &middot; **-ing form:** *devaluing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*devalue* the currency), intransitive (the peso *devalued* sharply)

To reduce the worth of money, work or a person's contribution.

1. Printing money like that can *devalue* the currency overnight.
2. The government *devalued* the peso in August.
3. Teachers say they feel *devalued* by the new rules.

---

### deviate

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdiːvieɪt/ &middot; *DEE-vee-ayt*
**Русский:** отклоняться, отклониться; отступать
**Forms:** *deviate* &middot; *deviated* &middot; *deviated*
**Third person:** *deviates* &middot; **-ing form:** *deviating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** Takes *from*: *deviate from* the plan.

To move away from an expected course, plan or standard.

1. Don't *deviate* from the recipe the first time you make it.
2. The plane *deviated* from its route to avoid the storm.
3. He has never *deviated* from that position in public.

---

### devolve

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈvɑːlv/ &middot; *dih-VAHLV*
**Русский:** передавать (полномочия); скатываться, вырождаться
**Forms:** *devolve* &middot; *devolved* &middot; *devolved*
**Third person:** *devolves* &middot; **-ing form:** *devolving*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*devolve* power to the regions), intransitive (the talks *devolved* into shouting)
**Contrast:** *Devolve into* means to deteriorate into; *devolve to* means to be handed down to.

To pass power or duties down to a lower level, or to sink into a worse state.

1. Arguments like that usually *devolve* into name-calling.
2. The debate quickly *devolved* into a shouting match.
3. Most of these decisions have been *devolved* to the states.

---

### die

**Pronunciation:** /daɪ/ &middot; *DY*
**Русский:** умирать, умереть; погибать
**Forms:** *die* &middot; *died* &middot; *died*
**Third person:** *dies* &middot; **-ing form:** *dying*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** The -ing form drops ie for y: *dying*, never 'dieing'. Do not confuse it with *dye — dyed — dyeing*, which means to color cloth.

To stop living.

1. Plants *die* quickly without water.
2. His grandfather *died* last spring.
3. The battery is *dying* and the screen keeps going black.

---

### differ

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɪfər/ &middot; *DIF-ur*
**Русский:** отличаться, различаться; расходиться во мнениях
**Forms:** *differ* &middot; *differed* &middot; *differed*
**Third person:** *differs* &middot; **-ing form:** *differing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** *Differ from* means to be unlike; *differ with* means to disagree with. The stress is on the first syllable, so the r does not double: *differed*, *differing*.

To be unlike something else, or to hold a different opinion.

1. The two reports *differ* on almost every number.
2. Their accounts *differed* in one crucial detail.
3. Historians have *differed* about the cause for a century.

---

### diffuse

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈfjuːz/ &middot; *dih-FYOOZ*
**Русский:** рассеивать, рассеиваться; распространять(ся)
**Forms:** *diffuse* &middot; *diffused* &middot; *diffused*
**Third person:** *diffuses* &middot; **-ing form:** *diffusing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*diffuse* the light), intransitive (the smell *diffused* through the room)
**Contrast:** Not *defuse* (dee-FYOOZ), which means to make a bomb or a tense situation safe. The adjective *diffuse* ends in an s sound: dih-FYOOS.

To spread something out over a wide area, or to spread out in that way.

1. Thin curtains *diffuse* the afternoon light nicely.
2. The smell of coffee *diffused* through the whole apartment.
3. The heat is *diffused* evenly by the metal plate.

---

### dignify

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɪɡnɪfaɪ/ &middot; *DIG-nuh-fy*
**Русский:** удостаивать; облагораживать, придавать достоинство
**Forms:** *dignify* &middot; *dignified* &middot; *dignified*
**Third person:** *dignifies* &middot; **-ing form:** *dignifying*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The y becomes ie before -s and -d: *dignifies*, *dignified*, but *dignifying* keeps the y.

To treat something as worthy of serious attention or respect.

1. I won't *dignify* that question with an answer.
2. He *dignified* the small ceremony simply by showing up.
3. The rumors have never been *dignified* with a response.

---

### digress

**Pronunciation:** /daɪˈɡrɛs/ &middot; *dy-GRES*
**Русский:** отклоняться от темы, отвлекаться
**Forms:** *digress* &middot; *digressed* &middot; *digressed*
**Third person:** *digresses* &middot; **-ing form:** *digressing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive

To wander away from the main subject while speaking or writing.

1. But I *digress* — back to the main point.
2. The lecturer *digressed* for ten minutes about his cat.
3. He kept *digressing* until we ran out of time.

---

### dilate

**Pronunciation:** /daɪˈleɪt/ &middot; *dy-LAYT*
**Русский:** расширять, расширяться; расшириться
**Forms:** *dilate* &middot; *dilated* &middot; *dilated*
**Third person:** *dilates* &middot; **-ing form:** *dilating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (the drops *dilate* the pupils), intransitive (her pupils *dilated*)

To make an opening in the body wider, or to become wider.

1. Your pupils *dilate* in the dark.
2. The doctor *dilated* her eyes before the exam.
3. He arrived with his pupils still *dilated* from the drops.

---

### dirty

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɜːrti/ &middot; *DUR-tee*
**Русский:** пачкать, испачкать; грязнить
**Forms:** *dirty* &middot; *dirtied* &middot; *dirtied*
**Third person:** *dirties* &middot; **-ing form:** *dirtying*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The y becomes ie before -s and -d: *dirties*, *dirtied*, but *dirtying* keeps the y.

To make something dirty.

1. Take your shoes off so you don't *dirty* the rug.
2. The kids *dirtied* every towel in the house.
3. Her white coat was *dirtied* by spray from the road.

---

### disabuse

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdɪsəˈbjuːz/ &middot; *dis-uh-BYOOZ*
**Русский:** выводить из заблуждения, разубеждать
**Forms:** *disabuse* &middot; *disabused* &middot; *disabused*
**Third person:** *disabuses* &middot; **-ing form:** *disabusing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Takes *of*: *disabuse someone of* an idea.

To free someone of a belief that is not true.

1. Let me *disabuse* you of that idea right now.
2. One week on the job *disabused* her of any romantic notions about it.
3. He has been *disabused* of the belief that hard work is always enough.

---

### disappear

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdɪsəˈpɪr/ &middot; *dis-uh-PEER*
**Русский:** исчезать, исчезнуть; пропадать
**Forms:** *disappear* &middot; *disappeared* &middot; *disappeared*
**Third person:** *disappears* &middot; **-ing form:** *disappearing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** Spelling trap: one s but two p's — *disappear*, *disappeared*.

To stop being visible, or to stop existing.

1. Socks *disappear* in this building's laundry room.
2. The sun *disappeared* behind the clouds.
3. Two hikers have *disappeared* in the park this month.

---

### disapprove

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdɪsəˈpruːv/ &middot; *dis-uh-PROOV*
**Русский:** не одобрять, осуждать
**Forms:** *disapprove* &middot; *disapproved* &middot; *disapproved*
**Third person:** *disapproves* &middot; **-ing form:** *disapproving*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (the board *disapproved* the request), intransitive (my parents *disapprove* of him)
**Contrast:** In the everyday sense it takes *of*: *disapprove of* smoking.

To think that something is bad or wrong.

1. My parents *disapprove* of the whole plan.
2. The committee *disapproved* the request without a hearing.
3. She has always *disapproved* of gossip.

---

### disassemble

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdɪsəˈsɛmbəl/ &middot; *dis-uh-SEM-buhl*
**Русский:** разбирать, разобрать (на части); демонтировать
**Forms:** *disassemble* &middot; *disassembled* &middot; *disassembled*
**Third person:** *disassembles* &middot; **-ing form:** *disassembling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Not *dissemble*, which means to hide your real feelings.

To take a machine or a structure apart into its separate pieces.

1. You have to *disassemble* the bed to get it through the door.
2. He *disassembled* the engine on the kitchen table.
3. The stage was *disassembled* in under an hour.

---

### disavow

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdɪsəˈvaʊ/ &middot; *dis-uh-VOW*
**Русский:** отрекаться, отречься; отвергать, дезавуировать
**Forms:** *disavow* &middot; *disavowed* &middot; *disavowed*
**Third person:** *disavows* &middot; **-ing form:** *disavowing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To say publicly that you have no connection with something or no longer support it.

1. The senator was quick to *disavow* the ad.
2. The group *disavowed* any link to the attack.
3. He has publicly *disavowed* everything he wrote back then.

---

### disbar

**Pronunciation:** /dɪsˈbɑːr/ &middot; *dis-BAR*
**Русский:** лишать адвокатского статуса, лишить права практики
**Forms:** *disbar* &middot; *disbarred* &middot; *disbarred*
**Third person:** *disbars* &middot; **-ing form:** *disbarring*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The stress falls on the last syllable, so the r doubles: *disbarred*, *disbarring*.

To take away a lawyer's right to practice law.

1. The state can *disbar* an attorney for stealing from clients.
2. The court *disbarred* him two years ago.
3. She was *disbarred* after the fraud came to light.

---

### disbelieve

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdɪsbɪˈliːv/ &middot; *dis-bih-LEEV*
**Русский:** не верить, сомневаться в правдивости
**Forms:** *disbelieve* &middot; *disbelieved* &middot; *disbelieved*
**Third person:** *disbelieves* &middot; **-ing form:** *disbelieving*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (the jury *disbelieved* him), intransitive (she had no reason to *disbelieve*)

To refuse to accept that something is true.

1. I have no reason to *disbelieve* her.
2. The jury clearly *disbelieved* the witness.
3. His account was *disbelieved* by everyone in the room.

---

### disburse

**Pronunciation:** /dɪsˈbɜːrs/ &middot; *dis-BURS*
**Русский:** выплачивать, выплатить; выдавать (средства)
**Forms:** *disburse* &middot; *disbursed* &middot; *disbursed*
**Third person:** *disburses* &middot; **-ing form:** *disbursing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Not *disperse* (to scatter). *Disburse* is about paying money out.

To pay out money from a fund that has been set aside.

1. The foundation will *disburse* the grants in May.
2. The agency *disbursed* four million dollars last year.
3. Aid has already been *disbursed* to three provinces.

---

### disclaim

**Pronunciation:** /dɪsˈkleɪm/ &middot; *dis-KLAYM*
**Русский:** отказываться (от ответственности, прав); отрицать
**Forms:** *disclaim* &middot; *disclaimed* &middot; *disclaimed*
**Third person:** *disclaims* &middot; **-ing form:** *disclaiming*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To state officially that you have no responsibility for something or no claim to it.

1. The site *disclaims* any responsibility for what users post.
2. The company *disclaimed* all knowledge of the leak.
3. He has *disclaimed* his share of the inheritance in writing.

---

### discolor

**Pronunciation:** /dɪsˈkʌlər/ &middot; *dis-KUL-ur*
**Русский:** обесцвечивать, менять цвет; выцветать
**Forms:** *discolor* &middot; *discolored* &middot; *discolored*
**Third person:** *discolors* &middot; **-ing form:** *discoloring*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (coffee *discolors* teeth), intransitive (the pages *discolored* with age)
**Contrast:** American *discolor, discolored*; British *discolour, discoloured*.

To change the color of something for the worse, usually by staining or fading.

1. Coffee will *discolor* your teeth over time.
2. The sun *discolored* the curtains in a single summer.
3. The wall was *discolored* where the water got in.

---

### disconcert

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdɪskənˈsɜːrt/ &middot; *dis-kuhn-SURT*
**Русский:** смущать, приводить в замешательство
**Forms:** *disconcert* &middot; *disconcerted* &middot; *disconcerted*
**Third person:** *disconcerts* &middot; **-ing form:** *disconcerting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To make someone feel uneasy or unsure of themselves.

1. A silence like that can *disconcert* even a good speaker.
2. His steady stare *disconcerted* her.
3. The audience was *disconcerted* by the sudden blackout.

---

### discredit

**Pronunciation:** /dɪsˈkrɛdɪt/ &middot; *dis-KRED-it*
**Русский:** дискредитировать, подрывать доверие; опровергать
**Forms:** *discredit* &middot; *discredited* &middot; *discredited*
**Third person:** *discredits* &middot; **-ing form:** *discrediting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To make people stop believing or respecting someone or something.

1. The defense tried to *discredit* the witness.
2. New data *discredited* the original study.
3. The theory has been thoroughly *discredited*.

---

### disembark

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdɪsɪmˈbɑːrk/ &middot; *dis-im-BARK*
**Русский:** высаживаться, сходить (с судна, самолёта)
**Forms:** *disembark* &middot; *disembarked* &middot; *disembarked*
**Third person:** *disembarks* &middot; **-ing form:** *disembarking*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (they *disembarked* the passengers first), intransitive (we *disembarked* at noon)

To get off a ship, a plane or a train.

1. Passengers *disembark* through the rear door.
2. We *disembarked* in Naples at dawn.
3. The troops had already *disembarked* by midnight.

---

### disenchant

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdɪsɪnˈtʃænt/ &middot; *dis-in-CHANT*
**Русский:** разочаровывать, лишать иллюзий
**Forms:** *disenchant* &middot; *disenchanted* &middot; *disenchanted*
**Third person:** *disenchants* &middot; **-ing form:** *disenchanting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Usually passive, with *with*: *disenchanted with* politics.

To make someone stop believing that something is as good as they thought.

1. A year in the industry can *disenchant* almost anyone.
2. The scandal *disenchanted* many longtime fans.
3. Voters have grown *disenchanted* with both parties.

---

### disentangle

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdɪsɪnˈtæŋɡəl/ &middot; *dis-in-TANG-guhl*
**Русский:** распутывать, распутать; высвобождать
**Forms:** *disentangle* &middot; *disentangled* &middot; *disentangled*
**Third person:** *disentangles* &middot; **-ing form:** *disentangling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To free something from knots, or to separate things that have become confused with each other.

1. It took an hour to *disentangle* the fishing line.
2. She *disentangled* herself from the argument and left.
3. The two firms are still *disentangling* their finances.

---

### disfigure

**Pronunciation:** /dɪsˈfɪɡjər/ &middot; *dis-FIG-yur*
**Русский:** обезображивать, уродовать; портить вид
**Forms:** *disfigure* &middot; *disfigured* &middot; *disfigured*
**Third person:** *disfigures* &middot; **-ing form:** *disfiguring*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To spoil the appearance of a person or a thing badly.

1. Graffiti *disfigures* half the buildings on the block.
2. The fire *disfigured* his hands.
3. The statue was badly *disfigured* by vandals.

---

### dishearten

**Pronunciation:** /dɪsˈhɑːrtən/ &middot; *dis-HAR-tuhn*
**Русский:** приводить в уныние, обескураживать
**Forms:** *dishearten* &middot; *disheartened* &middot; *disheartened*
**Third person:** *disheartens* &middot; **-ing form:** *disheartening*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To make someone lose hope or confidence.

1. Three rejections in a row would *dishearten* anyone.
2. The result *disheartened* the whole team.
3. She was *disheartened* by how little had changed.

---

### dishonor

**Pronunciation:** /dɪsˈɑːnər/ &middot; *dis-AH-nur*
**Русский:** бесчестить, позорить; не выполнять (обязательство)
**Forms:** *dishonor* &middot; *dishonored* &middot; *dishonored*
**Third person:** *dishonors* &middot; **-ing form:** *dishonoring*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** American *dishonor*; British *dishonour*. The h is silent: dis-AH-nur.

To bring shame on someone, or to refuse to honor an agreement or a check.

1. Breaking that promise would *dishonor* everyone who fought for it.
2. The bank *dishonored* the check.
3. He felt *dishonored* by the accusation.

---

### disillusion

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdɪsɪˈluːʒən/ &middot; *dis-ih-LOO-zhuhn*
**Русский:** разочаровывать, лишать иллюзий
**Forms:** *disillusion* &middot; *disillusioned* &middot; *disillusioned*
**Third person:** *disillusions* &middot; **-ing form:** *disillusioning*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To show someone that what they believed in is not as good or true as they thought.

1. I hate to *disillusion* you, but the job isn't glamorous.
2. Two years in politics *disillusioned* him completely.
3. Many young doctors are *disillusioned* with the system.

---

### disinherit

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdɪsɪnˈhɛrɪt/ &middot; *dis-in-HER-it*
**Русский:** лишать наследства, лишить наследства
**Forms:** *disinherit* &middot; *disinherited* &middot; *disinherited*
**Third person:** *disinherits* &middot; **-ing form:** *disinheriting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To arrange legally that someone will not receive your property after your death.

1. He threatened to *disinherit* all three children.
2. She *disinherited* her nephew in the final will.
3. The son was *disinherited* without any explanation.

---

### dislodge

**Pronunciation:** /dɪsˈlɑːdʒ/ &middot; *dis-LAHJ*
**Русский:** смещать, сдвигать; выбивать, вытеснять
**Forms:** *dislodge* &middot; *dislodged* &middot; *dislodged*
**Third person:** *dislodges* &middot; **-ing form:** *dislodging*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To force something or someone out of a fixed position.

1. Use a stick to *dislodge* the ball from the gutter.
2. The storm *dislodged* several roof tiles.
3. The champion has not been *dislodged* in six years.

---

### dismember

**Pronunciation:** /dɪsˈmɛmbər/ &middot; *dis-MEM-bur*
**Русский:** расчленять, расчленить; раздроблять на части
**Forms:** *dismember* &middot; *dismembered* &middot; *dismembered*
**Third person:** *dismembers* &middot; **-ing form:** *dismembering*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To cut a body apart, or to break a country or an organization into pieces.

1. The plan would *dismember* the company and sell the pieces.
2. The treaty *dismembered* the old empire.
3. The estate was *dismembered* and sold off in lots.

---

### dismount

**Pronunciation:** /dɪsˈmaʊnt/ &middot; *dis-MOWNT*
**Русский:** спешиваться, слезать (с лошади, велосипеда); снимать с крепления
**Forms:** *dismount* &middot; *dismounted* &middot; *dismounted*
**Third person:** *dismounts* &middot; **-ing form:** *dismounting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*dismount* the camera), intransitive (riders must *dismount*)

To get down from a horse or a bicycle, or to take something off its mounting.

1. Riders must *dismount* before crossing the bridge.
2. She *dismounted* and led the horse to the barn.
3. The lens had already been *dismounted* and packed away.

---

### disorient

**Pronunciation:** /dɪsˈɔːriɛnt/ &middot; *dis-OR-ee-ent*
**Русский:** дезориентировать, сбивать с толку
**Forms:** *disorient* &middot; *disoriented* &middot; *disoriented*
**Third person:** *disorients* &middot; **-ing form:** *disorienting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** American English prefers *disorient*; British English usually says *disorientate*.

To make someone lose their sense of where they are or what is happening.

1. Bright lights and loud music can *disorient* you fast.
2. The fog *disoriented* even the local guides.
3. She woke up cold and completely *disoriented*.

---

### disparage

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈspɛrɪdʒ/ &middot; *dih-SPER-ij*
**Русский:** принижать, унижать; отзываться пренебрежительно
**Forms:** *disparage* &middot; *disparaged* &middot; *disparaged*
**Third person:** *disparages* &middot; **-ing form:** *disparaging*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To speak about someone or something as though they have little value.

1. I don't want to *disparage* anyone's work here.
2. He *disparaged* his opponent at every stop on the tour.
3. Her research was *disparaged* for years before it was accepted.

---

### dispel

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈspɛl/ &middot; *dih-SPEL*
**Русский:** рассеивать, развеять (сомнения, страхи)
**Forms:** *dispel* &middot; *dispelled* &middot; *dispelled*
**Third person:** *dispels* &middot; **-ing form:** *dispelling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The stress falls on the last syllable, so the l doubles: *dispelled*, *dispelling*.

To make a feeling or a false belief go away.

1. Let me *dispel* a few myths about the test.
2. Her calm voice *dispelled* the panic in the room.
3. The rumor was quickly *dispelled* by the photos.

---

### displease

**Pronunciation:** /dɪsˈpliːz/ &middot; *dis-PLEEZ*
**Русский:** не нравиться, вызывать недовольство; сердить
**Forms:** *displease* &middot; *displeased* &middot; *displeased*
**Third person:** *displeases* &middot; **-ing form:** *displeasing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To annoy someone or make them unhappy.

1. Any decision here is bound to *displease* somebody.
2. The tone of the letter *displeased* her.
3. He was clearly *displeased* with the result.

---

### dispose

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈspoʊz/ &middot; *dih-SPOHZ*
**Русский:** избавляться (от чего-л.); располагать, склонять
**Forms:** *dispose* &middot; *disposed* &middot; *disposed*
**Third person:** *disposes* &middot; **-ing form:** *disposing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (the news *disposed* him to accept), intransitive (*dispose* of the packaging)
**Contrast:** In the 'get rid of' sense it always takes *of*: *dispose of the trash*, never 'dispose the trash'.

To get rid of something, or to make someone inclined to feel or act a certain way.

1. Please *dispose* of the packaging in the blue bin.
2. The court *disposed* of the case in a single morning.
3. She seemed *disposed* to give him another chance.

---

### dispossess

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdɪspəˈzɛs/ &middot; *dis-puh-ZES*
**Русский:** лишать собственности, отнимать имущество
**Forms:** *dispossess* &middot; *dispossessed* &middot; *dispossessed*
**Third person:** *dispossesses* &middot; **-ing form:** *dispossessing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To take land, a home or property away from the person who owns it.

1. The new law let the state *dispossess* absentee owners.
2. The war *dispossessed* millions of farmers.
3. Families *dispossessed* in the 1950s are still seeking compensation.

---

### disregard

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdɪsrɪˈɡɑːrd/ &middot; *dis-rih-GARD*
**Русский:** игнорировать, пренебрегать; не принимать во внимание
**Forms:** *disregard* &middot; *disregarded* &middot; *disregarded*
**Third person:** *disregards* &middot; **-ing form:** *disregarding*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To pay no attention to something, especially something you should consider.

1. Please *disregard* the last email.
2. He *disregarded* every warning on the label.
3. The jury was told to *disregard* the remark.

---

### diss

**Pronunciation:** /dɪs/ &middot; *DIS*
**Русский:** оскорблять, унижать; наезжать (разг.)
**Forms:** *diss* &middot; *dissed* &middot; *dissed*
**Third person:** *disses* &middot; **-ing form:** *dissing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Informal. The s doubles: *dissed*, *dissing*. Also written *dis*.

To insult someone or treat them with open disrespect, in informal speech.

1. Don't *diss* the food before you've tried it.
2. He *dissed* her in front of the whole class.
3. She felt *dissed* by the way they announced it.

---

### dissatisfy

**Pronunciation:** /dɪsˈsætɪsfaɪ/ &middot; *dis-SAT-is-fy*
**Русский:** не удовлетворять, вызывать недовольство
**Forms:** *dissatisfy* &middot; *dissatisfied* &middot; *dissatisfied*
**Third person:** *dissatisfies* &middot; **-ing form:** *dissatisfying*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Two s's in the middle, and the y becomes ie before -s and -d: *dissatisfies*, *dissatisfied*.

To fail to give someone what they wanted or expected.

1. A wait that long will *dissatisfy* even a loyal customer.
2. The settlement *dissatisfied* both sides.
3. Many employees are *dissatisfied* with the new schedule.

---

### dissect

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈsɛkt/ &middot; *dih-SEKT*
**Русский:** препарировать, вскрывать; разбирать, анализировать
**Forms:** *dissect* &middot; *dissected* &middot; *dissected*
**Third person:** *dissects* &middot; **-ing form:** *dissecting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To cut something open to study it, or to examine an idea part by part.

1. We had to *dissect* a frog in biology class.
2. Analysts *dissected* every line of the speech.
3. The play has been *dissected* by critics for a century.

---

### disseminate

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈsɛmɪneɪt/ &middot; *dih-SEM-uh-nayt*
**Русский:** распространять, распространить (информацию)
**Forms:** *disseminate* &middot; *disseminated* &middot; *disseminated*
**Third person:** *disseminates* &middot; **-ing form:** *disseminating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To spread information widely to many people.

1. The agency will *disseminate* the new guidelines next week.
2. The group *disseminated* the report to every member.
3. The findings have been widely *disseminated* online.

---

### dissent

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈsɛnt/ &middot; *dih-SENT*
**Русский:** не соглашаться, выражать несогласие; возражать
**Forms:** *dissent* &middot; *dissented* &middot; *dissented*
**Third person:** *dissents* &middot; **-ing form:** *dissenting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** Sounds exactly like *descent* (a way down) and close to *decent* (respectable). Usually *dissent from*.

To disagree openly with an official decision or a majority opinion.

1. Two justices *dissent* in almost every case of this kind.
2. Only one board member *dissented*.
3. He has *dissented* from the party line more than once.

---

### dissociate

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈsoʊsieɪt/ &middot; *dih-SOH-see-ayt*
**Русский:** отмежёвываться, отделять(ся), разъединять
**Forms:** *dissociate* &middot; *dissociated* &middot; *dissociated*
**Third person:** *dissociates* &middot; **-ing form:** *dissociating*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (*dissociate* the two questions), intransitive (the molecule *dissociates* in water)
**Contrast:** Also written *disassociate* with the same meaning, but *dissociate* is the standard form. Takes *from*.

To separate one thing from another, or to declare that you have no connection with something.

1. He tried to *dissociate* himself from the campaign.
2. The board *dissociated* itself from the remarks within an hour.
3. The two problems cannot easily be *dissociated*.

---

### dissuade

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈsweɪd/ &middot; *dih-SWAYD*
**Русский:** отговаривать, отговорить; разубеждать
**Forms:** *dissuade* &middot; *dissuaded* &middot; *dissuaded*
**Third person:** *dissuades* &middot; **-ing form:** *dissuading*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The opposite of *persuade*, and it takes *from* + -ing: *dissuaded him from going*.

To persuade someone not to do something.

1. Nothing I said could *dissuade* her.
2. His parents *dissuaded* him from dropping out.
3. He was not *dissuaded* by the price.

---

### distance

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɪstəns/ &middot; *DIS-tuhns*
**Русский:** дистанцироваться, отстраняться
**Forms:** *distance* &middot; *distanced* &middot; *distanced*
**Third person:** *distances* &middot; **-ing form:** *distancing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Nearly always reflexive: *distance yourself from* something.

To make it clear that you are not connected with someone or something.

1. Politicians hurry to *distance* themselves from a scandal.
2. She *distanced* herself from the group's statement.
3. The brand has *distanced* itself from the old ads.

---

### distend

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈstɛnd/ &middot; *dih-STEND*
**Русский:** раздувать, раздуть; растягиваться
**Forms:** *distend* &middot; *distended* &middot; *distended*
**Third person:** *distends* &middot; **-ing form:** *distending*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (gas distends the stomach), intransitive (her abdomen distended)

To swell outward from pressure inside, or to make something swell that way.

1. Too much air will *distend* the tissue.
2. Her stomach *distended* painfully after the meal.
3. The vet found a *distended* loop of intestine.

---

### distill

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈstɪl/ &middot; *dih-STIL*
**Русский:** перегонять, дистиллировать; извлекать суть
**Forms:** *distill* &middot; *distilled* &middot; *distilled*
**Third person:** *distills* &middot; **-ing form:** *distilling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** American English writes distill, British distil with one -l. Both double the l before -ed and -ing: distilled, distilling.

To purify a liquid by boiling and condensing it, or to reduce something to its essential part.

1. They *distill* the whiskey twice before bottling it.
2. She *distilled* three hundred pages into a single chart.
3. The findings have been *distilled* into one page.

---

### distort

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈstɔːrt/ &middot; *dih-STORT*
**Русский:** искажать, исказить
**Forms:** *distort* &middot; *distorted* &middot; *distorted*
**Third person:** *distorts* &middot; **-ing form:** *distorting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (distort the facts), intransitive (the sound distorts at high volume)

To twist something out of its true shape, sound, or meaning.

1. Cheap lenses *distort* straight lines.
2. The article *distorted* what she actually said.
3. His voice was *distorted* by the bad connection.

---

### dither

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɪðər/ &middot; *DITH-ur*
**Русский:** колебаться, мяться, тянуть с решением
**Forms:** *dither* &middot; *dithered* &middot; *dithered*
**Third person:** *dithers* &middot; **-ing form:** *dithering*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive

To hesitate and delay instead of making a decision.

1. Do not *dither* — the offer expires tonight.
2. The committee *dithered* for months over the budget.
3. While they were *dithering*, a rival bought the company.

---

### diverge

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈvɜːrdʒ/ &middot; *dih-VURJ*
**Русский:** расходиться, разойтись; отклоняться
**Forms:** *diverge* &middot; *diverged* &middot; *diverged*
**Third person:** *diverges* &middot; **-ing form:** *diverging*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** Diverge takes from: the results diverge from earlier findings.

To separate and go in different directions, or to differ from something.

1. The two trails *diverge* just past the bridge.
2. Their opinions *diverged* sharply after the vote.
3. Our careers had *diverged* long before graduation.

---

### divest

**Pronunciation:** /daɪˈvɛst/ &middot; *dy-VEST*
**Русский:** продавать активы, избавляться от; лишать
**Forms:** *divest* &middot; *divested* &middot; *divested*
**Third person:** *divests* &middot; **-ing form:** *divesting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (divest its holdings), intransitive (divest from coal)
**Contrast:** Divest takes of when someone is stripped of something and from when an investor sells out: divested of his rank, divested from oil.

To sell off a business or investment, or to strip someone of something they hold.

1. The university plans to *divest* from fossil fuels.
2. The company *divested* its European division last year.
3. He was *divested* of his title after the scandal.

---

### divine

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈvaɪn/ &middot; *dih-VYN*
**Русский:** угадывать, угадать; постигать
**Forms:** *divine* &middot; *divined* &middot; *divined*
**Third person:** *divines* &middot; **-ing form:** *divining*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Spelled and pronounced exactly like the adjective divine (божественный), but the verb means to guess something hidden.

To work out or guess something hidden without direct evidence.

1. Analysts try to *divine* the bank's next move.
2. She *divined* from his tone that something was wrong.
3. No one has *divined* the author's real intent.

---

### divulge

**Pronunciation:** /dɪˈvʌldʒ/ &middot; *dih-VULJ*
**Русский:** разглашать, разгласить
**Forms:** *divulge* &middot; *divulged* &middot; *divulged*
**Third person:** *divulges* &middot; **-ing form:** *divulging*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To reveal information that was supposed to stay secret.

1. The company refuses to *divulge* the terms of the deal.
2. He *divulged* the password to a stranger.
3. Nothing has been *divulged* about the investigation.

---

### doctor

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdɑːktər/ &middot; *DAHK-tur*
**Русский:** подделывать, подделать; фальсифицировать
**Forms:** *doctor* &middot; *doctored* &middot; *doctored*
**Third person:** *doctors* &middot; **-ing form:** *doctoring*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Same word as the noun doctor (врач), but the verb means to tamper with something.

To change something dishonestly, especially a document, a photo, or a drink.

1. It is a crime to *doctor* official records.
2. Someone *doctored* the photo before posting it.
3. The books had been *doctored* to hide the losses.

---

### dog

**Pronunciation:** /dɔːɡ/ &middot; *DAWG*
**Русский:** преследовать, донимать, изводить
**Forms:** *dog* &middot; *dogged* &middot; *dogged*
**Third person:** *dogs* &middot; **-ing form:** *dogging*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** One syllable ending in a single consonant, so the g doubles: dogged, dogging.

To follow or trouble someone persistently.

1. Money problems *dog* the project at every stage.
2. Injuries *dogged* him throughout the season.
3. The scandal has *dogged* the mayor for years.

---

### doom

**Pronunciation:** /duːm/ &middot; *DOOM*
**Русский:** обрекать, обречь
**Forms:** *doom* &middot; *doomed* &middot; *doomed*
**Third person:** *dooms* &middot; **-ing form:** *dooming*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Doom takes to: doomed to failure, doomed to repeat it.

To make someone or something certain to fail or suffer.

1. One bad decision can *doom* an entire campaign.
2. The leak *doomed* their chances of winning.
3. The plan was *doomed* from the start.

---

### dope

**Pronunciation:** /doʊp/ &middot; *DOHP*
**Русский:** накачивать наркотиками; принимать допинг
**Forms:** *dope* &middot; *doped* &middot; *doped*
**Third person:** *dopes* &middot; **-ing form:** *doping*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (dope the horse), intransitive (athletes who dope)
**Contrast:** Drop the silent -e before -ed and -ing: dope → doped, doping.

To give someone drugs secretly, or to take banned performance drugs in sport.

1. A few riders still *dope* to gain an edge.
2. The trainer *doped* two of the horses.
3. He admits he was *doping* all season.

---

### dot

**Pronunciation:** /dɑːt/ &middot; *DAHT*
**Русский:** усеивать, усеять; ставить точки
**Forms:** *dot* &middot; *dotted* &middot; *dotted*
**Third person:** *dots* &middot; **-ing form:** *dotting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** One syllable, one vowel, one final consonant, so the t doubles: dotted, dotting.

To mark something with dots, or to be scattered all over a surface.

1. Small farms *dot* the valley floor.
2. She *dotted* the map with red pins.
3. The hills are *dotted* with wind turbines.

---

### dovetail

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdʌvteɪl/ &middot; *DUV-tayl*
**Русский:** совпадать, состыковываться; подгонять
**Forms:** *dovetail* &middot; *dovetailed* &middot; *dovetailed*
**Third person:** *dovetails* &middot; **-ing form:** *dovetailing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — intransitive (the plans dovetail), transitive (dovetail the schedules)
**Contrast:** Dovetail takes with: the findings dovetail with earlier work.

To fit together neatly with something else.

1. Her research and his *dovetail* almost perfectly.
2. The new rules *dovetailed* with existing state law.
3. We are *dovetailing* the two schedules this week.

---

### down

**Pronunciation:** /daʊn/ &middot; *DOWN*
**Русский:** сбивать, сбить; залпом выпить
**Forms:** *down* &middot; *downed* &middot; *downed*
**Third person:** *downs* &middot; **-ing form:** *downing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To knock something down, or to swallow a drink or food quickly.

1. High winds can *down* power lines in seconds.
2. He *downed* the coffee and ran for the bus.
3. Three trees were *downed* by the storm.

---

### downplay

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdaʊnˈpleɪ/ &middot; *down-PLAY*
**Русский:** преуменьшать, преуменьшить
**Forms:** *downplay* &middot; *downplayed* &middot; *downplayed*
**Third person:** *downplays* &middot; **-ing form:** *downplaying*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Vowel + -y, so the y stays: downplays, downplayed, never downplaid.

To make something seem less important or serious than it really is.

1. Officials tend to *downplay* early warnings.
2. She *downplayed* her role in the discovery.
3. The risks were *downplayed* in the summary.

---

### downshift

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdaʊnˌʃɪft/ &middot; *DOWN-shift*
**Русский:** переключаться на пониженную передачу; сбавлять темп жизни
**Forms:** *downshift* &middot; *downshifted* &middot; *downshifted*
**Third person:** *downshifts* &middot; **-ing form:** *downshifting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — intransitive (*downshift before the curve*), transitive (*downshift the car into second*)
**Contrast:** The American term; British English says *change down*. The life sense — leaving a demanding job for a quieter life — is common enough in writing about work that it is worth knowing, and it gives the noun *downshifter*. The opposite is *upshift* for gears, but there is no matching life sense.

To change to a lower gear, or to deliberately move to a slower, simpler way of living.

1. *Downshift* before you reach the curve, not in it.
2. He *downshifted* into second and the engine caught.
3. They have *downshifted* to one income and a smaller house.

---

### downsize

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdaʊnsaɪz/ &middot; *DOWN-syz*
**Русский:** сокращать штат, сокращаться
**Forms:** *downsize* &middot; *downsized* &middot; *downsized*
**Third person:** *downsizes* &middot; **-ing form:** *downsizing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (downsize the staff), intransitive (the company downsized)
**Contrast:** Drop the silent -e before -ing: downsize → downsizing.

To reduce the size of a company or a household, usually by cutting jobs or moving somewhere smaller.

1. Firms *downsize* when orders dry up.
2. The plant *downsized* twice in three years.
3. Her whole department was *downsized* last spring.

---

### downvote

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdaʊnvoʊt/ &middot; *DOWN-voht*
**Русский:** голосовать против, минусовать (в интернете)
**Forms:** *downvote* &middot; *downvoted* &middot; *downvoted*
**Third person:** *downvotes* &middot; **-ing form:** *downvoting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Drop the silent -e before -ing: downvote → downvoting. The opposite is upvote.

To vote against a post or comment online so that its score goes down.

1. Readers *downvote* anything that looks like an ad.
2. Someone *downvoted* every answer in the thread.
3. His question was *downvoted* within minutes.

---

### drawl

**Pronunciation:** /drɔːl/ &middot; *DRAWL*
**Русский:** говорить протяжно, растягивать слова
**Forms:** *drawl* &middot; *drawled* &middot; *drawled*
**Third person:** *drawls* &middot; **-ing form:** *drawling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — intransitive (he drawls a little), transitive (drawl an answer)

To speak slowly, stretching the vowels out.

1. People from that part of Texas *drawl* a little.
2. 'Take your time,' he *drawled*.
3. She was *drawling* her words on purpose.

---

### dredge

**Pronunciation:** /drɛdʒ/ &middot; *DREJ*
**Русский:** чистить дно, углублять (дно); вычерпывать
**Forms:** *dredge* &middot; *dredged* &middot; *dredged*
**Third person:** *dredges* &middot; **-ing form:** *dredging*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Drop the silent -e before -ing: dredge → dredging.

To clear mud and silt from the bottom of a river or harbor, or to drag something up from it.

1. The city plans to *dredge* the shipping channel.
2. Crews *dredged* the lake for the missing boat.
3. The harbor has not been *dredged* since 2009.

---

### drone

**Pronunciation:** /droʊn/ &middot; *DROHN*
**Русский:** гудеть, жужжать; монотонно говорить
**Forms:** *drone* &middot; *droned* &middot; *droned*
**Third person:** *drones* &middot; **-ing form:** *droning*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** Drop the silent -e before -ed and -ing: droned, droning.

To make a low continuous humming sound, or to speak in a dull monotonous voice.

1. Air conditioners *drone* all summer here.
2. The engine *droned* for hours.
3. He was *droning* through forty slides.

---

### droop

**Pronunciation:** /druːp/ &middot; *DROOP*
**Русский:** поникать, поникнуть; обвисать
**Forms:** *droop* &middot; *drooped* &middot; *drooped*
**Third person:** *droops* &middot; **-ing form:** *drooping*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive

To bend or hang down from weakness, tiredness, or weight.

1. The flowers *droop* if you forget to water them.
2. His eyelids *drooped* halfway through the lecture.
3. The flag was *drooping* in the still air.

---

### drug

**Pronunciation:** /drʌɡ/ &middot; *DRUG*
**Русский:** подмешивать наркотик, опоить
**Forms:** *drug* &middot; *drugged* &middot; *drugged*
**Third person:** *drugs* &middot; **-ing form:** *drugging*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** One syllable ending in a single consonant, so the g doubles: drugged, drugging.

To give someone drugs, usually secretly, to make them sleepy or unconscious.

1. Thieves sometimes *drug* a victim's drink.
2. Someone *drugged* the guard dogs.
3. She believes she was *drugged* at the party.

---

### dry-clean

**Pronunciation:** /ˌdraɪˈkliːn/ &middot; *dry-KLEEN*
**Русский:** чистить в химчистке, отдавать в химчистку
**Forms:** *dry-clean* &middot; *dry-cleaned* &middot; *dry-cleaned*
**Third person:** *dry-cleans* &middot; **-ing form:** *dry-cleaning*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The verb keeps its hyphen: dry-clean, dry-cleaned. The shop is a dry cleaner's, two words.

To clean clothes with chemicals instead of water.

1. You have to *dry-clean* a wool coat.
2. I *dry-cleaned* the suit before the interview.
3. The curtains are being *dry-cleaned* this week.

---

### duel

**Pronunciation:** /ˈduːəl/ &middot; *DOO-uhl*
**Русский:** сражаться на дуэли; состязаться
**Forms:** *duel* &middot; *dueled* &middot; *dueled*
**Third person:** *duels* &middot; **-ing form:** *dueling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** intransitive
**Contrast:** American English keeps one l — dueled, dueling — while British writes duelled, duelling. Sounds the same as the adjective dual (двойной).

To fight or compete one against one.

1. The two aces *duel* again tonight.
2. They *dueled* over the amendment for weeks.
3. The candidates have been *dueling* on television all month.

---

### dull

**Pronunciation:** /dʌl/ &middot; *DUL*
**Русский:** притуплять, притупить; тускнеть
**Forms:** *dull* &middot; *dulled* &middot; *dulled*
**Third person:** *dulls* &middot; **-ing form:** *dulling*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** both — transitive (dull the pain), intransitive (the colors dulled)

To make something less sharp, bright, or intense, or to become so.

1. Two aspirin will *dull* the pain for a while.
2. The paint *dulled* after a single winter.
3. Years of routine had *dulled* his curiosity.

---

### dumbfound

**Pronunciation:** /dʌmˈfaʊnd/ &middot; *dum-FOWND*
**Русский:** ошеломлять, ошеломить
**Forms:** *dumbfound* &middot; *dumbfounded* &middot; *dumbfounded*
**Third person:** *dumbfounds* &middot; **-ing form:** *dumbfounding*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** The b is silent, as in dumb: dum-FOWND. The spelling dumfound also exists, but dumbfound is the usual American form.

To astonish someone so much that they cannot speak.

1. Results like that *dumbfound* the experts.
2. The verdict *dumbfounded* everyone in the courtroom.
3. She was *dumbfounded* by the size of the offer.

---

### dupe

**Pronunciation:** /duːp/ &middot; *DOOP*
**Русский:** обманывать, обмануть; одурачить
**Forms:** *dupe* &middot; *duped* &middot; *duped*
**Third person:** *dupes* &middot; **-ing form:** *duping*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Drop the silent -e before -ed and -ing: dupe → duped, duping.

To trick someone into believing or doing something.

1. Fake sites *dupe* thousands of shoppers a year.
2. He *duped* investors out of millions.
3. They were *duped* into signing away their rights.

---

### dwarf

**Pronunciation:** /dwɔːrf/ &middot; *DWORF*
**Русский:** затмевать, превосходить; казаться крошечным рядом
**Forms:** *dwarf* &middot; *dwarfed* &middot; *dwarfed*
**Third person:** *dwarfs* &middot; **-ing form:** *dwarfing*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive

To make something else look small by comparison.

1. The new tower will *dwarf* every building nearby.
2. This year's losses *dwarfed* last year's.
3. The stage was *dwarfed* by the size of the crowd.

---

### dynamite

**Pronunciation:** /ˈdaɪnəmaɪt/ &middot; *DY-nuh-myt*
**Русский:** взрывать динамитом, взорвать
**Forms:** *dynamite* &middot; *dynamited* &middot; *dynamited*
**Third person:** *dynamites* &middot; **-ing form:** *dynamiting*
**Verb class:** regular
**Transitivity:** transitive
**Contrast:** Drop the silent -e before -ed and -ing: dynamited, dynamiting.

To blow something up with dynamite.

1. Engineers had to *dynamite* the old dam.
2. They *dynamited* the roadblock at dawn.
3. The tunnel was *dynamited* shut.
